## PREFACE

These notes are based on a course I gave at the University of Chicago in the Spring Quarter of 1964. They were originally published in 1965 in the Lecture Notes of the Mathematics Department of the University of Chicago under the title of "Topics in Ring Theory." This present version is a reworking of these notes - many of the proofs in the first chapters have been changed and some material has been added.

The subject matter divides itself naturally into three distinct parts. In the first three chapters I treat the structure of a simple associative ring and some of its subsets as Lie and Jordan rings and use these results to obtain theorems about the associative nature of the ring itself. Chapters 4, 5, 6 are devoted to the theorems of Goldie and some of their consequences for rings conditioned by some appropriate chain condition. The material of the last chapter is concerned with recent examples and counter-examples; we find there the Golod-Shafarevitch theorem and examples due to Bergman and Sasiada.

The overlap of the material here and in my Carus Monograph "Non-Commutative Rings" is not large; it consists of one of the proofs of Goldie's theorem, ultra-products and Posner's theorem and the Golod-Shafarevitch theorem.

(Tier-1 Error)

## CHAPTER 1

LIE AND JORDAN STRUCTURES IN SIMPLE RINGS

In this course we shall study certain aspects of the theory of associative rings. From time to time we shall have to refer to results proved in our set of notes "Theory of Rings" (University, of Chicago) which we shall refer to as ( N ) or to Jacobson's book "Structure of Rings" referred to as (J).

We start things off with
LEMMA 1.1, Let R be a ring and $0 \neq \rho$ a right ideal of R . Suppose that, given $\mathrm{a} \epsilon \rho, \mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{n}}=0$ for a fixed integer n , then R has a non-zero nilpotent ideal.

Proof. The argument we use is a variation of one given by Levitzki. We go by induction on $n$.

Let $a \neq 0$ be in $\rho$ satisfying $a^{2}=0$; let $A=a \rho$. Suppose for the moment that $\mathrm{A} \neq(0)$. If $\mathrm{x} \in \mathrm{R}$ then $(\mathrm{a}+\mathrm{ax})^{\mathrm{n}}=0$ since it is in $p$, hence on expansion we get $(a x)^{n-1} a=0$. Thus $(\mathrm{ax})^{\mathrm{n}-1} \mathrm{~A}=(0)$. Let $\mathrm{T}=\{x \in \mathrm{~A} \mid \times \mathrm{A}=(0)\}$; of course, T is an ideal of $A$. Moreover, as we have just seen, $y$ in $A$ implies that $y^{n-1} \in T$. Therefore in $\bar{A}=A / T$ every element satisfies $\bar{y}^{n-1}=0$. By our induction hypothesis $\bar{A}$ has a nilpotent ideal $\bar{U} \neq(0)$, Let $U$ be its inverse image in $A$; since $\bar{U}^{k}=(0)$, $\mathrm{U}^{\mathrm{k}} \subset \mathrm{T}$ hence $\mathrm{U}^{\mathrm{k}+1} \subset \mathrm{TA}=(0)$. Also, since $\overline{\mathrm{U}} \neq 0, \mathrm{U} \not \subset \mathrm{T}$ whence $U D U \neq(0)$. But then $U A=U a p \neq(0)$ is a nilpotent right ideal of R .

Suppose then that $a \in \rho, a^{2}=0$ implies that $a \rho=(0)$. For any $x \in p$, since $x^{n}=0$ we have $\left(x^{n-1}\right)^{2}=0$ and so $x^{n-1} \rho=(0)$. Let $W=\{x<\rho \mid x \rho=(0)\} ; W$ is an ideal of $\rho$. If $W=\rho$ then $\rho^{2}=(0)$ and $\rho$ would provide us with a nilpotent right ideal. If $W=\rho$ then in $\bar{\rho}=\rho / W, \bar{x}^{n-1}=0$; our induction gives us a nilpotent ideal $\overline{\mathrm{V}} \neq(0)$ in $\overline{\mathrm{p}}$. If V is the inverse image of $\overline{\mathrm{V}}$ in $\rho$ then $V_{p} \neq(0) \subset V$ and is nilpotent since $V$ is. Again we have seen that $R$ must have a non-zero nilpotent right ideal.

If R has a non-zero nilpotent right ideal it has (almost trivially) a non-zero nilpotent ideal. This proves the lemma.

Given any associative ring $R$ we can induce on $R$, using its operations, two new structures, the Lie structure and the Jordan structure by defining the new products $[x, y]=x y-y x$ and $x y=x y+y x$ respectively. We propose to investigate the relationship between the associative structure of R and those induced Lie and Jordan structures.

We say that a subset $A$ of $R$ is a Lic subring of $R$ if $A$ is an additive subgroup such that for $\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b}$ in $\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{ab}$ - ba must also be in $A$, We similarly define a Jordan subring of $R$.

DEFINITION, Let $A$ be a Lie subring of $R$. The additive subgroup UCA is said to be a Lie ideal of A if whenever u $\in U$ and $\mathrm{a} \in \mathrm{A}$ then $[\mathrm{u}, \mathrm{a}]=\mathrm{ua}-\mathrm{au}$ is in U .

We similarly define the concept of a Jordan ideal of a Jordan subring of R .

Our first objective will be to determine the Lie and Jordan ideals of the ring $R$ itself in the case when $R$ is restricted to be a simple ring.

We begin with the Jordan ideals of $R$, which are a good deal easier to characterize.

LEMMA 1.2. If $U$ is a Jordan ideal of $R$ then for all $a, b \in U$ and $x<R,(a b+b a) x-x(a b+b a) \in U$.

Proof. Since $a \in U$, for $a n y x \in R, a(x b-b x)+(x b-b x) a$ is in U. But

$$
\begin{aligned}
a(x b-b x)+(x b-b x) a & =\{(a x-x a) b+b(a x-x a)\} \\
& +\{(a b+b a)-(a b+b a) x\}
\end{aligned}
$$

The left side and the first term on the right side are in $U$, hence $\mathrm{x}(\mathrm{ab}+\mathrm{ba})-(\mathrm{ab}+\mathrm{ba}) \mathrm{x}$ is also in U , proving the lemma.

From this we now obtain
THEOREM 1.1. Let R be a ring in which $2 \mathrm{x}=0$ implies $x=0$ and suppose further that $R$ has no non-zero nilpotent Ideals. Then any non-zero Jordan ideal of R contains a non-zer (associative) ideal of R .

Proof. Let $U \neq(0)$ be a Jordan ideal of $R$ and suppose that $\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b} \in \mathrm{U}$. By Lemma 1,2 , for any $\mathrm{x} \in \mathrm{R}, \mathrm{xc}-\mathrm{cx} \in \mathrm{U}$ where $c=a b+b a$. However, since $c \in U, x c+c x<U$. Adding we get $2 x c<U$ for all $x$, hence for $y \in R,(2 x c) y+y(2 x c) \in U$. Since Zyxce $U$ we obtain $2 x c y \in U$, that is ZRcR $C U$. Now 2RcR is an ideal of R so we are done unless $2 \mathrm{RcR}=(0)$. If $2 \mathrm{Rc} \mathrm{R}=(0)$, by our assumptions $\mathrm{RcR}=(0)$ and so $(\mathrm{Rc})^{2}=(0)$. Since R has no nilpotent ideals this forces $c=0$, that is, given $a, b \in U$ then $\mathrm{ab}+\mathrm{ba}=0$.

Let $0 \neq \mathrm{a} \in \mathrm{U}$; then for $\mathrm{x} \in \mathrm{R}, \mathrm{b}=\mathrm{ax}+\mathrm{xa} \in \mathrm{U}$ hence $\mathrm{a}(\mathrm{ax}+\mathrm{xa})+(\mathrm{ax}+\mathrm{xa}) \mathrm{a}=0$. That is, $\mathrm{a}^{2} \mathrm{x}+\mathrm{x} \mathrm{a}^{2}+2 \mathrm{axa}=0$. Now for $\mathrm{a} \in \mathrm{U}, 0=\mathrm{a} \mathrm{a}+\mathrm{a} \mathrm{a}=2 \mathrm{a}^{2}$ whence $\mathrm{a}^{2}=0$. The top relation then reduces to $2 a x a=0$ for all $x \in R$ and so $a R a=(0)$. But then $\mathrm{aR} \neq(0)$ is a nilpotent right ideal of $R$, contrary to as sump tion. In other words, we have shown that $U$ contains a non-zero ideal of R .

COROLLARY. If $R$ is a simple ring of characteristic $\neq 2$ then R is simple as a Jordan ring.

We now turn to the case of the Lie ideals of $R$,
DEFINITION, If $\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{B}$ are subsets of R then [ $\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{B}$ ] is the additive subgroup of R generated by all ab - ba with $\mathrm{a} \in \mathrm{A}$, $\mathrm{b} \in \mathrm{B}$.

LEMMA 1.3. Let R be a ring with no non-zero nilpotent ideals in which $2 \mathrm{x}=0$ implies $\mathrm{x}=0$. Suppose that $\mathrm{U} \neq(0)$ is both a Lie ideal and subring of R . Then either UCZ , the center of R , or U contains a non-zero ideal of R .

Proof. Let us first suppose that $U$, as a ring, is not commutative. Then for some $x, y \in U, x y-y x \neq 0$. For any $r \in R$, $x(y r)-(y r) x$ is in $U$, that is $(x y-y x) r+y(x r-r x)$ is in $U$. The second member of this is in U since both y and $\mathrm{xr}-\mathrm{rx}$ are in U (since U is both a Lie ideal and subring). The net result of all this is that $(x y-y x) R \subset U$. But then for $r_{1} s \in R$, $((x y-y x) r) s-s((x y-y x) r) \in U$ leading to $R(x y-y x) R \subset U$. We have now shown that the ideal $R(x y-y x) R$ is in $U$. If $R(x y-y x) R=(0)$ then $(R(x y-y x))^{2}=(0)$ contrary to assumption. We have shown that the result is correct if $U$ as a subring of R is not commutative.

So, suppose that U is commutative; we want to show that it lies in the center of $R$. Given $a<U, x \in R$ then $a x-x a \in U$ so commutes with a. Now, for $x, y, R, a(a(x y)-(x y) a)=(a(x y)-(x y) a) a$. Expanding $a(x y)-(x y) a$ as $(a x-x a) y+x(a y-y a)$ and using that a commutes with this, with $\mathrm{ax}-\mathrm{xa}$ and with ay - ya yields $2(\mathrm{ax}-\mathrm{xa})(\mathrm{ay}-\mathrm{ya})=0$ for all $\mathrm{x}, \mathrm{y} \in \mathrm{R}$. Since $2 \mathrm{r}=0$ forces $\mathrm{r}=0$ we obtain $(a x-x a)(a y-y a)=0$. Inthis put $y=a x_{2}$-this results in $(a x-x a) R(a x /-x a)=(0)$. Since $R$ has no nilpotent ideals we conclude that ax of $\mathrm{xa}=0$ and so, a must be in the center of R .

Note that in the latter part of the proof of Lemma 1.3 we have also proved the

Sublemma. Let R be a ring having no non-zero nilpotent ideals in which $2 \mathrm{x}=0$ implies that $\mathrm{x}=0$. If a $<\mathrm{R}$ commutes with all $a x-x a, x \in R$ then $a$ is in the center of $R$.

Lemma 1.3 immediately implies
THEOREM 1.2, Let $R$ be a simple ring of characteristic $\neq 2$. Then any Lie ideal of $R$ which is also a subring of $R$ must either be R itself or is contained in the center of R .

DEFINITION. If U is a Lie ideal of R let $T(U)=\{x \in R \mid\{x, R] \subset U\}$.

LEMMA 1.4. For any ring $R$, if $U$ is a Lie ideal of $R$, then $T(U)$ is both a subring and a Lie ideal of $R_{\text {; moreover, }} U \subset T(U)$.

Proof. Since $U$ is a Lie ideal of $R, U \subset T(U)$; since $[\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), \mathrm{R}] \subset \mathrm{U} \subset \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$ must certainly be a Lie ideal of R .

Now suppose that $a, b \in T(U), r \in R$. Then

$$
(a b) r-r(a b)=\{a(b r)-(b r) a\}+\{b(r a)-(r a) b\}
$$

so, since $\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b} \in \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$, the right side is in U . Therefore $[\mathrm{ab}, \mathrm{R}] \subset \mathrm{U}$, that is $\mathrm{ab} \in \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$.

We now prove the
THEOREM 1.3. Let R be a simple ring of characteristic $\neq 2$ and let $U$ be a Lie ideal of $R$. Then either $U \subset Z$, the center of R , or $\mathrm{U} \supset[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$.

Proof. By Theorem 1.2 and Lemma 1.4, since $T(U)$ is both a subring and a Lie ideal of $R$, either $T(U) \subset Z$ or $T(U)=R$. If $\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})=\mathrm{R}$ then by its very definition $[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}] \subset \mathrm{U}$; if $\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}) \in \mathrm{Z}$, since $U \subset T(U)$, we obtain $U \subset Z$.

COROLLARY, If R is a non-commutative simple ring of characteristic $\neq 2$, then the subring generated by $[R, R]$ is $R$.

Proof. Any additive subgroup containing $[R, R]$ is, trivially, a Lie ideal of $R$. Hence the subring generated by $[R, R]$ is a Lie Ideal thus, by Theorem 1.2, it equals $R$ or is in $Z$. If it is in $Z$ then $[R, R] \subset Z$. Thus for $a \in R$, a commutes with all $a x-x a, x \in R$; by the sublemma we get that $a \in Z$, that is, RCZ . Since we assumed R to be non-commutative, this is ruled out; hence the corollary.

We now should like to settle the problem even when $R$ has characteristic 2. Note that the characteristic of R has not entered into the discussion in the passage from Theorem 1.2 on. So we ask: when, in characteristic 2, docs Theorem 1.2 fail? It certainly fails in $F_{2}$, the ring of all 2 by 2 matrices over $F$, a field of characteristic 2 for $U=\left(\left.\left(\begin{array}{ll}a & b \\ b & a\end{array}\right) \right\rvert\, a, b \in F\right\}$ is a Lie ideal and subring of $R$ which is neither in $Z$ nor does it equal $R$. We aim to show that this is, effectively, the only counterexample.

Suppose that $R$ is a simple ring of characteristic 2 and that $U$ is a Lie ideal and subring of $R, U \neq R$ and $U \notin Z$. As in the proof of Lemma 1.3 we obtain that $U$, as a subring of $R$, must be commutative. That is, given $u, v \in U$ then $u v+v u=0$.

Let $a \in U$; then $a s+s a \in U$ for all $s \in R$ hence $\mathrm{a}(\mathrm{as}+\mathrm{sa})=(\mathrm{as}+\mathrm{sa}) \mathrm{a}$. This says that $\mathrm{a}^{2} \in \mathrm{Z}$. Since for any $r \in R_{1}$ ar $+r a \in U$ we also have that $(a r+r a)^{2} \in Z$.

If $Z=(0)$ then $a^{2}=0,(a r+r a)^{2}=0$ from which we get $(\mathrm{ar})^{3}=0$. But then aR is a right ideal of R in which the cube of every element is 0 ; by Lemma 1.1 R would have a nilpotent ideal, that is, $R$ would be nilpotent, which is impossible for a simple ring.

Therefore we may assume that $Z \neq(0)$ and that there is an element $a \in U, a ; Z$ such that $a^{2} \neq 0 \in Z$ and $(a r+r a)^{2} \in Z$ for all $r \in \mathrm{R}$.

To answer completely what the structure of R must be we prove a subsidiary

THEOREM 1.4. Let $R$ be a simple ring of characteristic 2 and suppose that there exists an $a \in R$, $a / Z$ such that $a^{2} \in Z$ and $(a x+x a)^{4} \in Z$ for all $x \in R$. Then $R$ is 4 -dimensional over $Z$.

Before proving the theorem we should like to point out that a more general theorem actually holds, namely: if $R$ is a simple ring with an element $\mathrm{a} / \mathrm{Z}$ such that $(\mathrm{ax}-\mathrm{xa})^{\mathrm{n}} \in \mathrm{Z}$ for all $\mathrm{x} \in \mathrm{R}$ then $R$ is 4 -dimensional over $Z$.

Proof. of Theorem 1.4. If $z=(0)$ then both $a^{2}=0$ and $(a x+x a)^{4}=0$ hence $(a x)^{5}=a(a x+x a)^{4} x=0$ for all $x \in R$. But then the right ideal $a R$ satisfies $u^{5}=0$ for all elements $u \in a R$; by Lemma i.i this is not possible in a simple ring.

Suppose, then, that $Z \neq(0)$, hence $i<R$. If $a^{2}=0$ then $b=a+1$ satisfles $b^{2}=1$ and $(b x+x b)^{4}, Z$ for all $x \in R$. Therefore we may assume that $a^{2}=\alpha \neq 0$ in $Z$. Let $Z^{\prime}=Z(\sqrt{\alpha})$; then $R^{\prime}=R \otimes_{Z} Z^{\prime}$ is simple. Moreover, in $R^{\prime}$ we have $\left(a x^{t}+x^{t} a\right)^{4} \in Z^{\prime}$ for all $x^{t} \in R^{\prime}$, Since $\operatorname{dim} R^{\prime} / Z^{\prime}=\operatorname{dim} R / Z$, to prove the theorem it is enough to do so in $\mathrm{R}^{\prime}$. Also $\mathrm{b}=\mathrm{a} / \beta$ where $\beta, Z^{\prime}, \beta^{2}=\alpha$ satisfies $b^{2}=1$ and $\left(\mathrm{bx}^{\prime}+\mathrm{x}^{\prime} \mathrm{b}\right)^{4} \in \mathrm{Z}$. Hence, without loss of generality we may suppose that $a \in R$, a. $z, a^{2}=1$ and $(a x+x a)^{4} \& z$ for all $x \in R$.

Now $R$ is a dense ring of linear transformations on a vector space $V$ over a division ring $D$ (since $Z \neq(0)$ and $R$ is simple). Since $(a+1)^{2}=0, a+1 \neq 0, v$ must be more than 1 -dimensional
over $D$. Since $a \neq 1$ it is immediate that there is $a v \in V$ such that $v$,va are linearly independent over $D$.

If for some $w \in V, v, v a$ and $w(1+a)$ are linearly independent over $D$ then the subspace $V_{o}$ spamed by these is invariant under a and a induces the linear transformations

$$
\left(\begin{array}{lll}
0 & 1 & 0 \\
1 & 0 & 0 \\
0 & 0 & 1
\end{array}\right)
$$

on $V_{0}$. By density of $R$ on $V$ there is an $x \in R$ which induces

$$
\left(\begin{array}{lll}
0 & 1 & 0 \\
0 & 0 & 0 \\
0 & 0 & 0
\end{array}\right)
$$

on $V_{\rho}$ hence $a x+x a$ induces

$$
\left(\begin{array}{lll}
0 & 1 & 0 \\
1 & 0 & 0 \\
0 & 0 & 0
\end{array}\right)
$$

on $V_{o}$. But $(a x+x a)^{4} E Z$ yet does not induce a scalar on $V_{o}$ since it induces

$$
\left(\begin{array}{lll}
1 & 0 & 0 \\
0 & 1 & 0 \\
0 & 0 & 0
\end{array}\right)
$$

Thus we have that for all $w \in V, v$,va and $w(1+a)$ are linearly dependent over $D_{\text {. }}$

If $V$ is more than 2 -dimensional over $D$ there is a $w \in V$ such that $v, v a, w$ are linearly independent over $D$. By the above, wa is in the subspace $V_{1}$ they span. The matrix of a on $V_{1}$ is

$$
\left(\begin{array}{lll}
0 & 1 & 0 \\
1 & 0 & 0 \\
\alpha & \beta & \gamma
\end{array}\right)
$$

By density there is an $x<R$ which induces $\left(\begin{array}{lll}0 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0\end{array}\right)$ on $v_{1}$;
but then ax + ax induces

$$
\left(\begin{array}{lll}
1 & 0 & 0 \\
0 & 1 & 0 \\
0 & \alpha & 0
\end{array}\right)
$$

whose fourth power is not a scalar.
Thus we must have that V is 2 -dimensional over D. All that remains is to show that D is commutative.

Let $\mathrm{a}=\left(\begin{array}{ll}\alpha & \beta \\ \gamma & \delta\end{array}\right)$; then $\mathrm{a}^{2}=1$ yields $\alpha^{2}+\beta_{\mathrm{Y}}=1$, $\alpha \beta+\beta \delta=0, \gamma \beta+\delta^{2}=1$. In particular not both $\alpha=0$ and $\gamma=0$. If $\sigma \in D$ then using $x=\left(\begin{array}{ll}0 & \sigma \\ 0 & 0\end{array}\right)$ and $(a x+x a)^{4} \in Z$ yields

$$
\left(\begin{array}{rr}
\sigma \alpha & \alpha \sigma+\sigma \beta \\
0 & y \sigma
\end{array}\right)^{4}, z
$$

This gives for all $\sigma \in D,(\sigma \alpha)^{4} \in Z$ and $(\gamma \sigma)^{4} \in Z$. If $\alpha \neq 0$ then $\sigma a$ runs through $D$ as $\sigma$ does, so every $x \in D$ would satisfy $x^{4} \in Z$. But a non-commutative division ring can not be purely inseparable over its center (see (N), Lemma 2.16). Thus $\alpha \neq 0$ implies $D$ is commutative. Similarly $Y \neq 0$ implies that $D$ is commutative. Since one of these must hold we get that $D$ is commutative and so $R$ is 4 -dimensional over $Z$.

Since the hypothesis of Theorem 1.4 is prectsely the one lead to by the assumption that Theorem 1.2 (and so Theorem 1.3) was false we obtain

THEOREM 1.5. If R is a simple ring and if U is a Lie Ideal of R then either $\mathrm{U} \subset \mathrm{Z}$ or $\mathrm{U} \supset[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$ except if R is of characteristic 2 and is 4 -dimensional over its center.

The theorem has as an immediate coroliary the
COROLLARY. If $R$ is a simple non-commutative ring then the subring generated by $[R, R]$ is $R$.

As a consequerce of these results we prove a theorem about simple rings with involution. Let R be a simple ring of characteristic not 2 and suppose that on $R$ there is defined an involution ${ }^{*}$, that is, a map of R into itself satisfying $\mathrm{a}^{* *}=\mathrm{a}$, $(a+b)^{*}=a^{*}+b^{*},(a b)^{*}=b^{*} a^{*}$. Let $S=\left\{a \in R \mid a^{*}=a\right\}$ and $\left.K=\{a \in R] a^{*}=-a\right\} . \quad S$ is the set of symmetric elements, K the set of skew-symmetric elements of R , Note that, since the characteristic of $R$ is not $2, R=S+K, S \cap K=(0)$, and that $S$ is a Jordan subring of $R$ and $K$ a Lie subring of $R$. Also, if $s \in S$ and $k \in K$ then $s k-k s<S$. We shall make an intensive study of $S$ and $K$ in the next chapter. But now we content ourselves with proving the

THEOREM 1.6. Let R be a simple ring with involution of characteristic not 2 , and let $S$ be the set of symmetric elements of R . Then $\overline{\mathrm{S}}$, the subring of R generated by S , is R unless R is of dimension 4 or less (thus ${ }^{4}$ or ${ }^{1)}$ over its center.

Proof. We claim that $\bar{S}$ is a Lie ideal of $R$. To see this note first that trivially $[\overline{\mathrm{S}}, \mathrm{S}] \subset \overline{\mathrm{S}}$. If $\mathrm{k} \in \mathrm{K}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{s}} \in \overline{\mathrm{S}}$ we want to show that $[\bar{s}, k] / \bar{s}$; to do so, since $\bar{s}$ is a sum of monomials from $S$, we need merely do it for monomials $\bar{B}=s_{1} s_{2} \ldots s_{n}$, $s_{1} \in S$. But then $\left[s_{1} s_{2} \ldots s_{n}, k\right]=\left[s_{1}, k\right] s_{2} \ldots s_{n}+\ldots +s_{1} \ldots s_{1-1}\left[s_{1}, k\right] s_{i+1} \ldots s_{n}+\ldots+s_{1} s_{2} \ldots s_{n-1}\left[s_{n}, k\right]$ which is certainly in $\bar{S}$. Thus $[\bar{S}, R]=[\bar{S}, S+K]=[\bar{S}, S]+[\bar{S}, K] \subset \bar{S}$ and so $\bar{S}$ is a Lie ideal of $R$. By definition it is a subring of $R$. Therefore by Theorem 1.2 we conclude that either $\bar{S}=R$ or $\overline{\mathrm{S}} \subset \mathrm{Z}$.

We consider the second possibility, namely, $\bar{S} \subset Z$. But then $S \subset Z$. Given $a \in R, a=s+k, s<S \subseteq Z, k \in K$ hence $(a-s)^{2}=k^{2}$ e $S \subset Z$. Consequently $a^{2}-2 s a+s^{2}-k^{2}=0$, which
is to say, $R$ is quadratic over $Z$. By Theorem 2.38 of ( $N$ ) we get that R is at most 4-dimensional over Z .

Related to this theorem is the following romark which holds for simple rings, of any characteristic, which have involutions.

THEOREM 1.7. Let $R$ be a simple ring with involution whose center $Z=(0)$ or for which $\operatorname{dim} R / Z>4$. Then the only elements commuting with all the symmetric elements lle in Z .

Proof, Let $a \in R$ commute with all $s \in S$. If the characteristic of R is not 2 , by Theorem $1.6 \overline{\mathrm{~S}}=\mathrm{R}$ hence a $\in \mathrm{Z}$ follows. Thus we may suppose that R is of characteristic 2 .

Let $T=\{r \in R \mid r s=s r, s \in S\} . T$ is clearly a subring of $R$. Given $x \in R, a \in T$ then $a\left(x+x^{*}\right)=\left(x+x^{*}\right) a$ hence $a x+x a=a x^{*}+x^{*} a$. We want to show that $T$ is a Lie ideal of $R$. Given $a \in T, y \in R, s \in S$ then $(a y+y a) s=$ ays $+y a s =a y s+y s a=a\left(y s+s y^{*}\right)+a s y^{*}+y s a=\left(y s+s y^{*}\right) a+s a y^{*}+y s a =s y^{*} a+s a y^{*}=s\left(y^{*} a+a y^{*}\right)=s(y a+a y)$, as we have just shown. In other words, $T$ is both a Lie ideal and subring of $R$. By our assumption on $\mathrm{dim} R / Z$ we get from Theorem 1.5 and its corollary that $T \subset Z$ or $T=R$. If $T=R$ then $S \subset Z$ which we have seen forces $\operatorname{dim} \mathrm{R} / \mathrm{Z} \leq 4$. Thus $\mathrm{T} \subset \mathrm{Z}$, which is the assertion of the theorem.

We now turn to a study of the Lie structure of $[R, R]$ as a Lie subring of R .

We begin with an easy result,
LEMMA 1.5. Let $R$ be a ring without any non-zero nilpotent ideals. Then any element of R which commutes with all elements of $[R, R]$ must lie in the center of $R$.

Proof. Let a commute with all elements of $[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$. Given $x, y \in R$ then a commutes with $x y-y x$ and with $x(x y)-(x y) x =x(x y-y x)$. The upshot of this is that $(a x-x a)(x y-y x)=0$ for all $x, y \in R$. In this, replace $y$ by $y a$ since $x(y a)-(y a) x =(x y-y x) a+y(x a-a x)$ we get $0=(x a-a x)(x(y a)-(y a) x) =(x a-a x)(x y-y x) a+(x a-a x) y(x a-a x)$. This results in $(x a-a x) \mathrm{R}(x a-a x)=(0)$; but then $(x a-a x) \mathrm{R}$ is a nilpotent right ideal so is (0). Since $R$ has no nilpotent ideals we get from this $x a-a x=0$, that is, a must be in the center of $R$.

THEOREM 1.8. If R is a simple ring and is not 4 -dimensional over a field of characteristic 2, then $[[R, R],[R, R]]=[R, R]$.

Proof. If $R$ is commutative the result is clearly correct; so suppose R is not commutative. Now, $[[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}],[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]]$ is a Lie ideal of $R$ hence, by Theorem 1,6, either $[[R, R],[R, R]] \supset[R, R]$ (the desired conclusion) or $[[R, R],[R, R]] C Z$. Suppose, then, that $[[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}],[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]] \subset \mathrm{Z}$.

If $[R, R] \subset Z$, then by the corollary to Theorem 1.5, $R$ is commutative. So $[R, R] \notin Z$. Let $a \in[R, R], a \notin Z$ and let $x \in R$, Thus $\alpha=[\mathrm{a},[\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{x}]]$ and $\beta=[\mathrm{a},[\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{ax}]]$ are in $[[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}],[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]]$, hence in Z . Since $\beta=\alpha \mathrm{a}$ and Z is a field, if $\alpha \neq 0$ then it follows that $a \in Z$. So a must be 0 . Thus $[a,[a, x]]=0$ for all $x \in R$.

If the characteristic of $R$ is not 2 this forces $a \in Z$ on invoking the sublemma. If the charactaristic of $R$ is 2 then $[a,[a, x]]=0$ implies $a^{2} \in Z$; hence $a^{2}, Z$ for all $a \in[R, R]$. But for $x \in R$, ax-xa< $[R, R]$ so $(a x-x a)^{2} \leqslant Z$ and $a^{2} \in Z$ and $a / Z$. By Theorem 1.4 we get that $\operatorname{dim} R / Z=4$ contrary to hypothesis. The theorem is now proved.

Let $U$ be a Lie ideal of $[R, R]$ and set $T(U)=\{x \in R \|[x, R] C U\}$, It is immediate that $T(U)$ is a subring of $R$. We investigate the interrelationship of $T(U)$ and $U$ in

LEMMA 1.6. If U is a Lie ideal of $[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$ then:

1. $[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}] \subset \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$
2. $[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})] \subset \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$
3. $[[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})], \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})] \subset \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$
4. $[[\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})], \mathrm{R}] \subset \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$.

Proof. The proofs are all of the same spirit, making use of the Jacobi identity.

Now $[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}], \mathrm{R}] \subset[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{R}], \mathrm{U}]+[\mathrm{U},[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{R}]][\mathrm{U},[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]] \mathrm{C} \mathrm{U}$ hence $[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}] \subset \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$. Also $[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})], \mathrm{R}] \subset[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{R}], \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})]+[\mathrm{U},[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})]] \subset \mathrm{U}$ by definition of $\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$, so $[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})] \subset \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$. Now $[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})] \subset \mathrm{U}$ so (3) follows from (2). Finally
$[[\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})], \mathrm{R}] \subset[\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}),[\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), \mathrm{R}]] \subset[\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), \mathrm{U}] \subset \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$
by (2). This proves the lemma.
Let $A=[T(U), T(U)]$ i we prove
LEMMA 1.7. $\quad \mathrm{R}[\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{A}] \mathrm{R} \subset \mathrm{U}+\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$.
Proof. Let $\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b}=\mathrm{A}$; then for $\mathrm{r} \in \mathrm{R},[\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b}] \mathrm{r}=[\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{br}]-\mathrm{b}[\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{r}]$. The first term is in $T(U)$ by part 4 of Lemma1.6, as is $[a, r]$. Since $\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$ is a subring of $\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{b}[\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{r}]$ \& $\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$, hence $[\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{A}] \mathrm{R} \in \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$. Similarly $\mathrm{R}[\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{A}] \subset \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$. Now $\mathrm{R}[\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{A}] \mathrm{R} \subset[\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), \mathrm{R}]+[\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{A}] \mathrm{R} C U+T(U)$.

THEOREM 1.9. Let R be a simple ring and let $\mathrm{U} \neq[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$ be a Lie ideal of $[R, R]$. Suppose $R$ is not 4-dimensional over a field of characteristic 2. Then

$$
[[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}],[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}]],[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}],[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}]]]=(0) .
$$

Proof. Since $U \neq[R, R], T(U) Z[R, R]$ for otherwise $U \supset[T(U), R] \supset[[R, R], R]=[R, R]$ by Theorem 1.8. Now

$$
[\mathrm{U}+\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), \mathrm{U}+\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})] \subset[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}]+[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{~T}(\mathrm{U})+[\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})] \subset \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})
$$

by Lemma 1.6. Since $T(U) \varnothing[R, R]$ we must have that $U+T(U) \neq R$. However by the previous lemma $R[A, A] R C U+T(U)$ and is an ideal of R ; since $\mathrm{U}+\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}) \neq \mathrm{R}$ we get that $\mathrm{R}[\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{A}] \mathrm{R}=(0)$, hence $[A, A]=(0)$. Since $[U, U] C T(U)$ and $A=[T(U), T(U)]$ we get the theorem.

LEMMA 1.8. If R is a simple ring, $\lambda \neq(0)$ a left ideal of $R$ then $\lambda+[R, R]=R$,

Proof. Clearly $\lambda+[R, R] D \lambda+[\lambda, R]$; if $a<\lambda, r \in R$ then $\mathrm{ar}=(\mathrm{ar}-\mathrm{ra})+\mathrm{ra} \varepsilon \lambda+[\lambda, \mathrm{R}]$, that is $\mathrm{R}=\lambda \mathrm{RC} \lambda+[\lambda, \mathrm{R}]$.

This gives us
LEMMA 1.9. If $U \neq(0)$ is a Lie ideal of $[R, R], R$ simple, then $\mathrm{xU}=(0)$ implies $\mathrm{x}=0$.

Proof. Let $\lambda=\{x \in R \mid x U=(0)\}$; clearly $\lambda$ is a left ideal of $R$. If $\lambda \neq(0)$ then by the previous lemma $R=\lambda+[R, R]$. Let $x \in \lambda, a \in[R, R], u \in U ;$ then $(x a-a x) u=x a u=x(a u-u a)=0$, that is, $[\lambda,[R, R]] C \lambda$. Since $\lambda$ is a left ideal of $R$ this implies that $\lambda[R, R] C \lambda$, hence $R=\lambda R=\lambda(\lambda+[R, R]) \subset \lambda$ forcing $\mathrm{RU}=(0)$. This contradicts that $\mathrm{U} \neq(0)$, so we must conclude that $\lambda=(0)$.

We now prove the basic
THEOREM1.10, Let $R$ be a simple ring and $U$ a Lie ideal of $[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$ such that $[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}]=(0)$. Then:

1. if the characteristic of R is not $2, \mathrm{U} \subset \mathrm{Z}$.
2. if the characteristic of R is 2 then for every $\mathrm{u} \in \mathrm{U}, \mathrm{u}^{2} \in \mathrm{Z}$.

Proof. Let $u \neq 0 \in U$ and let $d(x)$ be defined by $d(x)=u x-x u$ for $x \in R$.

By assumption, if $x, y \in R$ then since $[u,[x, y]] \in U$, $d^{2}([x, y])=0$. In this last relation replace $y$ by $z v$ with $v \in U$. Using Leibnitz's rule we get

$$
\begin{aligned}
0= & \left.d^{2}([x, z v])=d^{2}(x, z] v+z[x, v]\right) \\
= & d^{2}(z[x, v]) \quad\left(\text { since } v \in U, d(v)=0 \text { and } d^{2}([x, z])=0\right. \text { so } \\
& \left.d^{2}([x, z] v)=0\right) \\
= & d^{2}(z)[x, v]+2 d(z) d([x, v])+z d^{2}([x, v]) \\
= & d^{2}(z)[x, v]+2 d(z) d([x, v])
\end{aligned}
$$

If $x \in[R, R]$ then $[x, v] \in U$ hence $d([x, v])=0$, thus $d^{2}(z)[U,[R, R]]=\langle 0\rangle$ for all $z \in R$. Since $[U,[R, R]]$ is a Lie ideal of $[R, R]$, were it not $(0)$, we would get by Lemma 1.9 that $d^{2}(z)=0$.

On the other hand if $[\mathrm{U},[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]]=(0)$ by Lemma 1.5 U would be in $Z$. So we may suppose that $d^{2}(z)=0$ for all $z \in R$, that is, $u \in U$ commutes with all ux - xu. By the sublemma following Lemma 1.3 we get that if the characteristic of $R$ is not 2 then $U \subset Z$. If the characteristic is 2 then from $u(u x+x u)=(u x+x u) u$ we get $u^{2} x=x u^{2}$ for all $x \in R$ and so $u^{2} \in Z$. This proves the theorem.

We sharpen the last theorem to
THEOREM 1.11. If R is simple of characteristic not 2 and if $U$ is a Lie ideal of $[R, R]$ such that $[U, U] \subset Z$ then $U \subset Z$.

Proof. If $[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}]=(0)$ then by Theorem 1.10 we are done. So suppose that there are $u, v \in U$ such that $\alpha=[u, v] \neq 0$; by assumption $\alpha<Z$. Let $d(x)=u x-x u$ for $x \in R$. By assumption, $d(v)=a \neq 0 \in Z$.

If $d^{2}(x)=0$ for all $x \in R$ then $d^{2}(x v)=0$; expanding, using $d^{2}(v)=0$, we get $0=d^{2}(x) v+2 \alpha d(x)=2 \alpha d(x)$. Since $2 \alpha \neq 0$ \& $Z$ it is invertible so $\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{x})=0$ follows, for all $\mathrm{x} \in \mathrm{R}$. This contradicts that $d(v)=\alpha \neq 0$. We claim that $d^{2}(x) \in Z$ for all $x \in R$ is also impossible. For suppose $d^{2}(y)=\beta \neq 0 \in Z$; then $d^{2}(u y)=u d^{2}(y)=u \beta \in Z$ leading to $u<Z$.

So we can pick $x<R$ so that $d^{2}(x) / Z$. Since $U$ is a Lie ideal of $[R, R], d^{2}(x) \in U$ hence $\left[u, d^{2}(x)\right]=y \in[U, U] C z, B y$ the same token $\left[v, d^{2}(u x)\right]=\delta \in Z$; but $\delta=\left[v, d^{2}(u x)\right]=\left[v, u d^{2}(x)\right] =[v, u] d^{2}(x)+u\left[v, d^{2}(x)\right]=-\alpha d^{2}(x)+\delta u$. Since $\alpha \neq 0$ we get $d^{2}(x)=\mu+\lambda u ;$ because $d^{2}(x)\left\langle z, \lambda \neq 0\right.$. Similarly $d^{2}(u x)=\mu_{1}+\lambda_{1} u_{1} \mu_{1}, \lambda_{1} \in$ Z. But $\mu_{1}+\lambda_{1} u=d^{2}(u x)=u d^{2}(x)=u(\mu+\lambda u)=\lambda u^{2}+\mu u$. Since $\lambda \neq 0$ we get that $u^{2}+\sigma u+\tau=0$ for some $\sigma_{,} \tau \in Z$.

Thus $0=\left[u^{2}+\sigma u+\tau, v\right]=\left\{u^{2}, v\right]+\sigma[u, v]=u(u v-v u)+(u v-v u) u+\alpha \sigma z \alpha \mathrm{u}+\alpha \sigma$. Since $2 \alpha \neq 0$ we get from this the contradiction $u$, $Z$. Thus the theorem is proved, and $U \subset Z$.

We are now in a position to prove one of the most fundamental theorems in this area of results, narnely

THEOREM 1.12. Let R be a simple ring of characteristic not 2; then any Lie ideal of $[R, R]$ is either $[R, R]$ or is contained in the center of R .

Proof. Let $U \neq[R, R]$ be a Lie ideal of $[R, R]$. According to Theorem 1.9, $[[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}],[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}]],[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}],[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}]]=(0)$, hence by Theorem 1.10, $[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}],[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}]] \subset \mathrm{Z}$. By. Theorem 1.11 $[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}] \subset \mathrm{Z}$ and so again by use of the same theorem $\mathrm{U} \subset \mathrm{Z}$.

We now consider the case of characteristic 2. Suppose that $U$ is a Lie ideal of $[R, R]$ such that $[U, U] C Z$. Given $u \in U$, $x \in[R, R]$ then $u(u x+x u)+(u x+x u) u \in Z$, that is $u^{2} x+x u^{2} \in Z$. Let $W=\{a \in R \mid[a,\{R, R] \mid C Z\} ; W$ is a Lie ideal of $R$ so if
$\operatorname{dim} R / Z>4$ by Theorem 1.5 WCZ or W $D[R, R]$. However, if $W \supset[R, R]$ then $[[R, R],[R, R]] C z$, so by Theorem 1.8 , $[R, R] \subset Z$, thus since $U \subset[R, R]$ we have $U \subset Z$. If $W \subset Z$ then $a \in U$ implies $a^{2} \in Z$ since $a^{2} \in W$.

If $\mathrm{a} \in \mathrm{U}$ and $\mathrm{x} \in \mathrm{R}$ then $\mathrm{a}(\mathrm{x}(\mathrm{ax})+(\mathrm{ax}) \mathrm{x})+(\mathrm{x}(\mathrm{ax})+(\mathrm{ax}) \mathrm{x}) \mathrm{a} \in \mathrm{U}$, that is, $(a x)^{2}+a^{2} x^{2}+(x a)^{2}+a x^{2} a \in U$. But since $a^{2} \in Z$, $(a x)^{2}+a^{2} x^{2}+(x a)^{2}+a x^{2} a=(a x+x a)^{2} ;$ since $(a x+x a)^{2} \in U$ then $(a x+x a)^{4}<z$ for all $x \in R$. By Theorem 1.4 if $\operatorname{dim} R / Z>4$ this forces a $\varepsilon Z$, that is $U C Z$. We have sharpened Theorem 1.12 to its definitive form.

THEOREM 1.13. If R is a simple ring and if U is a Lie ideal of $[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$ then $\mathrm{U}=[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$ or UCZ except if R is of characteristic $Z$ and 4 -dimensional over $Z$.

If $T$ is an additive subgroup of $R$ such that $[T,[R, R]] C T$, even if $T \not \subset[R, R]$ we can still pin it down tightly as we see in

THEOREM 1.14. If R is a simple ring and if $[\mathrm{T},[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]] \subset \mathrm{T}$ then either $T C Z$ or $T \supset[R, R]$ except if $R$ is of characteristic 2 and 4-dimensional over $Z$.

Proof. Let $S=[T,[R, R]]$; from the assumption on $T, S$ is a Lie ideal of $[R, R]$ therefore by Theorem 1.13 either $S \subset Z$ or $S \supset[R, R]$. In the second possibility, since $T D S$, we get that $T D[R, R]$.

If $[T,[R, R]]=S \subset Z$ let $W=\{x \in R \|[x,[R, R]] \subset Z)$. As is easily verified, $W$ is a Lie ideal of $R$ hence $W \subset Z$ or $W][R, R]$. Since, as we have seen several times, $[R, R] \frac{\mathrm{L}}{\mathrm{Z}}$ and since $[[R, R],[R, R]]=[R, R]$, we obtain that $W][R, R]$. Thus we are forced to conclude that $W \subset Z$. Since $T C W$, we get the desired conclusion TCZ.

We now show a general construction of a Lie ideal in any ring. We shall use this to obtain a theorem of Amitsur about subspaces of a simple ring invariant with respect to a suitable set of automorphisms.

LEMMA 1.10. In any ring $R$ the additive subgroup, $E$, of $R$ generated by the idempotents of R is a Lie ideal of R .

Proof. Let e be any idempotent in R and x any element of $R$. Then, as is quickly checked, $f_{1}=e+x e-e x e$ and $f_{2}=e+e x-e x e$ are idempotents. Hence xe - $e x=f_{1}-f_{2}$ is in the additive group E generated by the idempotents. This proves the lemma,

COROLLARY, Let $R$ be a simple ring having an idempotent $\mathrm{e} \neq 0,4$. Then $\mathrm{E} \supset[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$.

Proof. Since the center, $Z$, of $R$ is $(0)$ or a field, e $/ Z$, hence $E \not \subset Z$. But by Lemma $1.10 E$ is a Lie ideal of $R$, hence $\mathrm{E} \supset[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$ unless R is 4 -dimensional over Z and Z is of characteristic 2 (Theorem 1.13). If $\operatorname{dim} R / Z=4$ and $Z$ is of characteristic 2, since $R$ is simple and has a non-trivial idempotent, by Wedderburn's theorem $\mathrm{R} \approx \mathrm{Z}_{2}$. But then $[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$ consists of the matrices of trace 0 , that is, of matrices of the form $\left(\begin{array}{ll}\alpha & \beta \\ \gamma & \alpha\end{array}\right), \alpha, \beta, \gamma \in Z$, But $\left(\begin{array}{ll}\alpha & \beta \\ \gamma & \alpha\end{array}\right)=e_{1}+e_{2}+e_{3}+e_{4}$ where the $e_{i}$ are the following idempotents:

$$
\begin{array}{ll}
e_{1}=\left(\begin{array}{lr}
\alpha & \alpha \\
1+\alpha & 1+\alpha
\end{array}\right), e_{2}=\left(\begin{array}{ll}
1 & \alpha+\beta \\
0 & 0
\end{array}\right), e_{3}=\left(\begin{array}{ll}
1 & 0 \\
0 & 0
\end{array}\right) \\
e_{4}=\left(\begin{array}{lr}
0 & 0 \\
1+\alpha+\gamma & 1
\end{array}\right) .
\end{array}
$$

This establishes the corollary.

COROLLARY. Let R be a simple ring having an idempotent $e \neq 0,1$. Then $[E, R] \supset[R, R]$

Proof. If R is not 4-dimensional over a field of characteristic 2 , since $E \supset[R, R],[E, R] \supset[[R, R], R]$ and $[[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}], \mathrm{R}]=[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$ by Theorem 1.8. If R is 4-dimensional over a field of characteristic 2 , since $R=Z_{2}$ and, by a direct computation $[[R, R], R]=[R, R]$, the lact that $E \supset[R, R]$ implies $[\mathrm{E}, \mathrm{R}] \geq[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$.

Given $a \in R, a^{2}=0$ the mapping

$$
x \rightarrow(1+a) x(1-a)=x+a x-x a-a x a
$$

is an automorphism of $R$. We call such an automorphism a special inner automorphism of R .

If $R$ is a simple ring it is an algebra over its centroid, which is a field; if, in addition, R has a unit element its centroid coincides with Z (see (N), p.53-54). By a subspace of R we shall mean a subset of $R$ which is a vector space over the centroid of R .

We now prove
THEOREM 1.15. Let $R$ be a simple ring whose controid $F$ is not $G F(2)$; suppose further that R has an idempotent $\mathrm{e} \neq 0,1$. Then any subspace of $R$ invariant under all special inner automorphisms of R must be ( 0 ), Z or contain [R, R]. The only subalgebras of R invariant under all special inner automorphisms of R are $(0), \mathrm{Z}$ and R .

Proof, Let $W$ be a subspace invariant under all special inner automorphisms of $R$. Given $a \in R$ with $a^{2}=0$ and $w \in W$ then $(1+a) w(1-a)=w+a w-w a-a w a \epsilon W$, hence aw - wa - awa \& W. Let $\alpha \in F$ with $\alpha^{2} \neq \alpha$; since $(\alpha a)^{2}=0$ we
get $\alpha a w-w(\alpha a)-\left(\alpha a w(\alpha a) \in W\right.$, that is, $\alpha(a w-w a)-\alpha^{2} a w a$ \& $W$. Eliminating with the above yields $\left(\alpha^{2}-\alpha\right)(a w-w a) \in W$ and since $\alpha^{2}-\alpha \neq 0$ is in $F$ we end up with aw - wa $\epsilon W$.

If e is an idempotent in R then $\mathrm{a}=\mathrm{xe}$ - exe satisfies $a^{2}=0$ for all $x \in R$ so $(x e-e x e) w-w(x e-e x e) \& W$, Similarly, $(e x-e x e) w-w(e x-e x e) \in W$. The net result of these is that $(e x-x c) w-w(e x-x e) \in W$, which is to say, $[[E, R], W] \subset W$. Since $[\mathrm{E}, \mathrm{R}] \supset[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$ by the corollary to Lemma 1.10, we get $[[R, R], W]$ CW. Hence if $R$ is not 4-dimensional over a field of characteristic 2, by Theorem 1.14 we get $W C Z$ or $W D[R, R]$. Since $W$ is a subspace over the centroid, $W \subset Z$ implies $W=(0)$ or $W=Z$.

So if $R$ is not 4-dimensional over $Z$ of characteristic 2 we are done. Suppose that $R$ is 4 -dimensional over $Z$; since $R$ has a non-trivial idempotent, $R$ is not a division ring so $R \approx Z_{2}$ the 2 by 2 matrices over $Z, Z \neq G F(2)$ (and $F=Z$ in this case), and $\mathrm{WC} \mathrm{Z}_{2}$ is invariant under all inner automorphisms of $\mathrm{Z}_{2}$. Given $\mathrm{a}=\left(\begin{array}{ll}\alpha & \beta \\ \gamma & \delta\end{array}\right) \in \mathrm{W}$ then ${ }^{a} 12^{a+a e_{12}} \in \mathrm{~W}$ and $a+e_{12} a+a e_{12}+e_{12} a e_{12}$ \& $W$ so that $e_{12}{ }^{a e_{12}}=\left(\begin{array}{ll}0 & y \\ 0 & 0\end{array}\right) \in W$. If $y \neq 0$ we get $e_{12} \in W$ from which $e_{21}$, $W$ follows by applying the inner automorphism induced by $\left(\begin{array}{ll}0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0\end{array}\right)$, and so $e_{11}+e_{22}=e_{12} e_{21}+e_{21} e_{12}<W$. But then $W \supset[R, R]$. So if $W \neq[R, R]$ we must assume $\left(\begin{array}{cc}\alpha & \beta \\ \gamma & \delta\end{array}\right)$, $W$ forces $\gamma=0$, and similarly $\beta=0$. That is, $W$ consists of diagonal matrices. If $\left(\begin{array}{ll}\alpha & 0 \\ 0 & \delta\end{array}\right) \in W$, with $\alpha \neq \delta$ then

$$
\left(\begin{array}{ll}
1 & 1 \\
0 & 1
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}{ll}
a & 0 \\
0 & 6
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}{ll}
1 & 1 \\
0 & 1
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}{ll}
\alpha & 6 \\
0 & \delta
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}{ll}
1 & 1 \\
0 & 1
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}{ll}
\alpha & \alpha+\delta \\
0 & \delta
\end{array}\right)
$$

is in $W$, forcing $\alpha+\delta=0$, that is $\alpha=\delta$. Thus if $W \quad D[R, R]$ then $\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{Z}$.

If $W$ is, in addition, a subring of $R$ being $(0), Z$ or containing $[R, R]$, in light of the corollary to Theorem $1.5, W$ is $(0)$, $Z$ or $R$.

This theorem has many applications of a certain form; for instance, let $M$ be the additive subgroup generated by all elements of square 0 ; then $M$ is invariant under all automorphisms so in the presence of an idempotent $\mathrm{M} \int[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$. Similarly for sums of invertible elements, quasi-regular elements, otc.

If one wanted to study the existence or nature of a simple nil ring - - and in light of Golod's recent results this looks likely -at least when this simple ring has an infinite centroid one can easily show that the subring generated by the elements of square 0 is a Lic ideal of $R$, so must be $R$ itself. Thus to investigate local finiteness of R one could content oneself with studying the subring generated by a finite number of elements whose square is 0 .

Let's return to Theorem 1.15 for a moment. If R is simple without a non-trivial idempotent the result may be false as we show by an example.

Let $F$ be a field of characteristic 0 and $I$ a non-trivial derivation of $F$ into itself, that is $(\alpha+\beta)^{t}=\alpha^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}$, $(\alpha \beta)^{\dagger}=\alpha^{\prime} \beta+\alpha \beta^{\prime}$. Consider all polynomials $F[t]$ in $t$ where we suppose that for $\alpha \in F, \alpha t-t \alpha=a^{f}$. It is easy to show that $F[t]$ is a simple, non-commutative integral domain. Now the only
invertible elements in $\mathrm{F}[\mathrm{t}]$ are precisely the non-zero elements of $F$ so that $F$ is invariant under all automorphisms of $F[t]$. Yet $F \not \subset Z$ nor does $F=F[t]$.

One might ask: can we weaken the hypothesis of Theorem 1.15 as follows; let $R$ be a simple ring with zero divisors, then is a subring invariant under all automorphisms in the center or all of the ring? Sasiada, in an unpublished result, has shown that simple rings with zero divisors can have proper subrings, not in the center, invariant under all automorphisms. In fact in his example such a subring can be chosen to consist of all the nilpotent elements in the ring.

DEFINITION. The involution is of the first kind if ZCS ; the involution is of the second kind if $\mathrm{Z} \& \mathrm{~S}$.

A study of the Lie and Jordan structure of simple rings with involutions of the second kind is easy and we shall dispose of these soon. Involutions of the first kind offer much deeper diffeulties.

Let us recall at this point that in Theorem 1.6 we showed that in a simple ring with involution whose dimension over its center exceeds 4 the subring generated by the symmetric elements is the ring itself.

Notation. If A is a subset of R then $\overline{\mathrm{A}}$ will denote the subring of R generated by A ,

Let R have an involution of the second kind (we do not restrict the characteristic of R ), and let $\mathrm{F}=\left\{\mathrm{a} \in \mathrm{Z} \mid \mathrm{a}^{*}=\mathrm{a}\right\}$. Then $[Z: F]=2$ and it is easy to verify that $R \otimes_{F} Z$ is a direct sum of two simple rings and that the involution on $\mathrm{R} \theta_{\mathrm{F}} \mathrm{Z}$ interchanges these simple rings. The Lie and Jordan results in $S$ and $K$ can be obtained from the results developed in Chapter 1 on simple ringe without involution. However we choose to follow a different path, working in $R$ itself,

Let $R$ be simple, of arbitrary characteristic having an invalution of the second kind. Let $\mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}}=\mathrm{Z} \cap \mathrm{S}$. Then it follows that $\left[\mathrm{Z} ; \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}}\right]=2$ and $\mathrm{Z}=\mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}}(\alpha)$. From this it is immediate that $\mathrm{R}=\mathrm{S}+\alpha \mathrm{S}$,

If the characteristic of $R$ is not 2 let $U$ be a Jordan ideal of S ; then we claim that $\mathrm{U}+\mathrm{ZU}$ is a Jordan ideal of R , as can immediately be verified, so that if $U \neq(0)$ then $U+Z U$ is $R$. But then $\mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{U}=\mathrm{S}$, on comparing R with $\mathrm{S}+\mathrm{ZS}$, hence $U \supset S \circ U=\left(Z_{S} S\right) \circ U=S \circ\left(Z_{S} U\right)=S \circ S$, that is, $U \circlearrowright$ sos. Since
$R=R \circ R$ ( $R$ is simple as a Jordan ring) then $S \circ S+\alpha S \circ S=R \circ R =\mathrm{R}$, we get $\mathrm{U}=\mathrm{S}$.

If, in the case of arbitrary characteristic for $R, V$ is a Lie ideal of $[K, K]$ then, as can be verified, since $[R, R]=[K, K]+\alpha[K, K]$, and since $V+Z V$ is a Lie ideal of $[R, R]$, by Theorem 1.14, if R is not 4-dimensional over $\mathrm{Z}, \mathrm{Z}$ of characteristic 2, then either $\mathrm{V}+\mathrm{ZV} \subset \mathrm{Z}$ or $\mathrm{V}+\mathrm{ZV}=[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$.

The first possibility implies that VCZ , the second that $\mathrm{V}+\alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}=[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$. Since $[[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}],[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]]=[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$ it follows that $[[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}],[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]]=[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$ and so $\left[\mathrm{V}+\alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}, \mathrm{V}+\alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}\right]=[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$. Thus $[\mathrm{V}, \mathrm{V}]+\left[\mathrm{V}, \alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}\right]+\left[\alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}, \alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}\right] \mathrm{D}[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$, and so $\mathrm{V} \supset[\mathrm{V},[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]] \supset[\mathrm{V}, \mathrm{V}]+\left[\mathrm{V}, \alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}\right]+\left[\mathrm{V}, \alpha^{2} \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}\right] \supset[\mathrm{V}, \mathrm{V}]+\left[\mathrm{V}, \alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}\right] +\left[\alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}, \alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}\right] \mathrm{D}[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$.

We have proved
THEOREM 2.1. Let $R$ be a simple ring having an involution of the second kind. Then

1. If the characteristic of R is not 2 then S is a simple Jordan ring.
2. any Lie ideal of $[K, K]$ is either in $Z$ or is $[K, K]$ except if $R$ is of characteristic 2 and is 4 -dimensional over its center.
3. any Lie ideal of $K$ either is in $Z$ or contains $[K, K]$ except if R is of characteristic 2 and is 4 -dimensional over its center.

We leave the proof of part 3 to the reader.
With this theorem out of the way we can focus our attention on the case of involutions of the first kind. While many of the results to follow hold irrespective of the nature of the involution we henceforth assume in this chapter that $R$ is a simple ring of characteristic not 2 having an involution of the first kind.

We have already seen in Theorem 1.6 that $\overline{\mathrm{S}}=\mathrm{R}$ for most simple rings. We now wish to establish its companion theorem, namely, that $\overline{\mathrm{K}}=\mathrm{R}$ in general. To do so we first show another construction, in most rings with involution, of a Lie ideal of the ring.

DEFINITION, $K^{2}$ is the additive group generated by all $k_{1} k_{2}$, with $k_{1}, k_{2} \in K$.

LEMMA 2.1. Let $R$ be any ring with involution such that $R=S+K$. Then $K^{2}$ is a Lie ideal of $R$.

Proof. Let $k_{1}, k_{2} \in K$ and $k \in K$. Then $\left(k_{1} k_{2}\right) k-k\left(k_{1} k_{2}\right)=\left(k_{1} k-k k_{1}\right) k_{2}+k_{1}\left(k_{2} k-k k_{2}\right) \in K^{2}$, so $\left[K^{2}, K\right] \subset K^{2}$. On the other hand, if $s \in S$ then $\left(k_{1} k_{2}\right) s-s\left(k_{1} k_{2}\right) =k_{1}\left(k_{2} s+s k_{2}\right)-\left(k_{1} s+s k_{1}\right) k_{2}$ which is in $K^{2}$ since $K \bullet S \subset K$. Thus $\left[K^{2}, S\right] \subset K^{2}$. Since $R=S+K$ we have $\left[K^{2}, R\right] \subset K^{2}$ whence $K^{2}$ is a Lle ideal of $R$.

The lemma enables us to prove

We sharpen this result to get an extension of a result of Baxter.

We return to simple rings; for these this theorem has a very interesting consequence, namely

THEOREM 2.4. If K has only a finite number of elements linearly independent over $Z$ then $R$ is finite-dimensional over $Z_{\text {; }}$ in fact $\operatorname{dim} R / Z \leq n^{2}+n$, where $n$ is the maximal number of independent elements in K .

Proof, If $k_{1}, \ldots, k_{n}$ is a maximally independent set of elements in $K$ over $Z$ then since $S=K \circ K$ we have that $k_{i} k_{j}+k_{j} k_{i}$ span $S$ over $Z$. Thus $\operatorname{dim} R / Z \leq n^{2}+n$.

Proof. The proof will consist of breaking $\tau$ and $a$ into their symmetric and skew parts and verifying that in these special instances the theorem holds. We do this in the sequence of three lemmas.

LEMMA 2.2. If $\mathrm{x}, \mathrm{y} \in \mathrm{S}$ and $\mathrm{u} \in \mathrm{U}$ then $x u y+y u x \in \mathrm{U}$.

Proof. $2 x u x=\{x(x u+u x)+(x u+u x) x\}-\left(x^{2} u+u x^{2}\right)$. Since $x^{2} \in S, x^{2} u+u x^{2} \in U$; since $x u+u x \in U$ so is $x(x u+u x)+(x u+u x) x$. Thus $2 x u x \in U$; since $2 S=S$ we get xux $\mathrm{c} U$, Linearizing on x we obtain the lemma.

Proof. We notice, first of all, that since $u k-k u$ is in $S$, $u^{2} k-k u^{2}=u(u k-k u)+(u k-k u) u$ is in U. Being a Jordan ideal of $S, s\left(u^{2} k-k u^{2}\right)+\left(u^{2} k-k u^{2}\right) s \in U$. That is

Adding (1) and (2) the right sides add up to $u^{2}(k s+s k)-(k s+s k) u$ which, since $k s+s k \in K$, we have seen must be in $U$. Therefore the sum of the left sides must be in U ; since the left side of (1) is already in $U$ we get that of (2) must also be. Now subtract (1) from (2); doing so we stay in $U$. The result on the righ is $2\left(s u^{2} k-k u^{2} s\right) \in U$. Since $2 S=S$ this gives $s u^{2} k-k u^{2} s \in U$ for all $s \in S, k \in K, u \in U$, which is the desired result.

Proof of Theorem 2.5. Given $u \in U, r, s \in R$ then $r=r_{0}+r_{1}, s=s_{0}+s_{1}$ with $r_{0}, s_{0} \in S, r_{1}, s_{1} \in K$. Thus

We are in a position to prove the basic
THEOREM 2.6. The only Jordan ideals of $S$ are $(0)$ and $S$; that is, $S$ is a simple Jordan ring.

Thus if $\mathrm{U} \neq \mathrm{S}$ we must assume that $\mathrm{u}^{4}=0$ for all $\mathrm{u} \in \mathrm{U}$. By Lemma 1.1 wo conclude that $u^{2}=0$ for all $u \in U$. Linearizing we get that $u v+v u=0$ for all $u, v \in U$. Given s \& $S$, $v=u s+s u \in U$ hence $0=u(u s+s u)+(u s+s u) u=2 u s u$ since $u^{2}=0$. Thus $u s u=0$ for all $u \in U, s \in S$. Given $k \in K$ then kuk $\in S$ hence ukuku $=0$. For any $r z R_{, r}=r_{o}+r_{1}$ with $r_{o} \in S$, $r_{1} \in K_{0}$, whence ururu $=u\left(r_{0}+r_{1}\right) u\left(r_{0}+r_{1}\right) u=u r_{1} u r_{1} u=0$. That is $(u r)^{3}=0$ for all $r<R$. By Lemma 1.1 we conclude that $u=0$. We have proved that $U=(0)$ or $U=S$; this establishes the theorem.

Having determined the Jordan structure of $S$ we now want to determine the Lie structure of $K$ and of $[K, K]$. We begin with the very easy

LEMMA 2.5. If $U$ is a Lie ideal of $K$ and if $u \in U, s \in S$ then $u^{2} s-s u^{2} \in U$.

Proof. To see the result merely note that $u s+s u \in K$ and $u^{2} s-s u^{2}=u(u s+s u)-(u s+s u) u$.

Motivated by what we did in Chapter 1 we make the
DEFINITION, If $U$ is a Lie ideal of $K$ then $T(U)=\{x \in K\} [\mathrm{x}, \mathrm{K}] \subset \mathrm{U})$.

THEOREM 2.12. If R is a simple ring with involution of characteristic not 2 and if $U$ is a Lie ideal of $K$ then either $U \subset Z$ or $U \supset[K, K]$ provided $\operatorname{dim} R / Z>16$.

LEMMA 2.7. If $U$ is a non-zero Lie ideal of $[K, K]$ then $\mathrm{xU}=(0)$ implies $\mathrm{x}=0$.

THEOREM 2.14. If $U$ is a proper Lie ideal of $[K, K]$ then $[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}],[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}]]=(0)$.

\begin{aligned}
& b_{1} b_{2} \cdots b_{n}=\cdots b_{1} \cdots b_{n}=b_{1}\left(b_{2} \cdots b_{n} r\right)-\left(b_{2} \cdots b_{n} r\right) b_{1} \\
&+b_{2} \cdots b_{n}\left(r b_{1}\right)-\left(r b_{1}\right) b_{2} \cdots b_{n} \in\left[B_{1} R\right]+\left[B^{n-1}, R\right]
\end{aligned}
$$

is contained in $[B, R]$. Since $R=\Sigma B^{n},[B, R]=[R, R]$.
Since $\bar{K}=R,[K, R]=[R, R]$; from $R=K+S$ this gives us $[K, K]+[K, S]=[K, K]+[S, S]+[K, S]$ so that $[S, S] \subset[K, K]$, and $[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]=[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]+[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{S}]$. Thus if $[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}],[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}]] \neq(0)$ by Lemma 2.9 $\overline{[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}]}=\mathrm{R}$, hence $[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}], \mathrm{R}]=[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]=[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]+[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{S}]$. But $[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}], \mathrm{K}+\mathrm{S}]=[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}], \mathrm{K}]+[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}], \mathrm{S}]$; equating the skew and symmetric parts we see that $[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}], \mathrm{K}]=[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$. However, by the Jacobi identity. $[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}], \mathrm{K}] \subset \mathrm{U}$ leading to the contradiction $\mathrm{U}=[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$. Therefore we have proved $[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}],[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}]]=(0)$.

The problem of examining the Lie simplicity of $[K, K]$ has been reduced to proving that if $V$ is a Lie ideal of $[K, K]$ for which $[V, V]=(0)$ then $V=(0)$. Were this true, by Theorem 2.14 for any proper Lie ideal U of $[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}],[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}],[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}]]=(0)$ hence we would get $[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}]=(0)$ and so $\mathrm{U}=(0)$. This explains our concern with Lie ideals V of $[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$ such that $[\mathrm{V}, \mathrm{V}]=(0)$.

We prove a remark - actually proved earlier - namely:
$\underline{\text { Remark: If } \mathrm{a}, \mathrm{K}}$ satisfies $\mathrm{aKa}=(0)$ then $\mathrm{a}=0$.

Proof, Let $s$ \& $S$; then sas $\in K$ hence asasa $=0$. Given $r \in R, r=s+k, s \in S$ and $k \vee K$ whence arara $=a(s+k) a(s+k) a =0$. By Lemma 1.1, since in aR every element has cube 0 , $\mathrm{a}=0$,

LEMMA 2.10. Let $V$ be a Lie ideal of $[K, K]$ such that $[\mathrm{V}, \mathrm{V}]=(0)$. Then V has no non-zeronilpotent elements.

Proof. Let $u \neq 0 \in V$. Then for any $k \in[K, K]$, $u(u k-k u)-(u k-k u) u=0$, that is

$$
\begin{equation*}
u^{2} k-2 u k u+k u^{2}=0 . \tag{t}
\end{equation*}
$$

Thus we see that $u^{2}[K, K] \subset R u$. If $k=u b-b u, b \in K$ then $u^{2}(u b-b u) \subset R u$, hence $u^{3} K \subset R u$. Finally, if $s \in S$, $u s+s u<K$ so that $u^{3}(u s+s u) \subset R u$ leading to $u^{4} S \subset R u$. But then $u^{4} R \subset R u$ so that $R u^{4} R \subset R u$; hence either $u^{4}=0$ or $\mathrm{Ru}=\mathrm{R}$. Similarly, if $\mathrm{u}^{4} \neq 0$ we get $\mathrm{uR}=\mathrm{R}$; therefore $\mathrm{u} \in \mathrm{V}$ implies $u^{4}=0$ or $u$ is invertible.

Suppose that $u \in V$ is not invertible; then $u^{4}=0$ hence $u^{2}[K, K] u^{3} \subset R u u^{3}=(0)$, so that $u^{2}[K, K] u^{3}=(0)$. In particular, for $b \in K$ then $u^{2}(u b-b u) u^{3}=0$, that is $u^{3} K u^{3}=(0)$. Since $u^{3}<K$ we have seen this implies that $u^{3}=0$.

Let N be the set of nilpotent elements in V . Since $[\mathrm{V}, \mathrm{V}]=0$, clearly N is closed under addition.

We claim that N is a Lie ideal of $[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$. If the characteristic of $R$ is 3 , then for $a<V,[a,[a,[a, k]]]=0$ implies $a^{3} k=k a^{3}$ for all $k \in K$, hence $a^{3} \in Z \cap K=(0)$ in which case $\mathrm{N}=\mathrm{V}$ so is a Lie ideal of $[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$. If the characteristic of R is not 3 , then if $u \in N$, since $u$ commutes with $u k-k u$, for $k \in[K, K], u^{2} k-k u^{2}=2 u(u k-k u), 0=u^{3} k-k u^{3}=3 u^{2}(u k-k u)$. If $u^{2} \neq 0$ then $u k-k u$ can not be invertible since it annihilates $u^{2}$, so is nilpotent, If $u^{2}=0$ then $u(u k-k u)=0$ so that again $u k-k u$ is nilpotent. Thus $N$ is a Lie ideal of $[K, K]$.

Let $u, v \in N$; then for $k \in[K, K]$, from (1), by multiplying by $u$ from left and right, $u^{2} k u^{2}=0$, so $u^{2}(v b-b v) u^{2}=0$ for all $b \in K$. Thus $u^{2} v b u^{2}=u^{2} b v u^{2}$, and so since $u, v$ commute and $v^{3}=0, u^{2} v^{2} K u^{2} v^{2}=(0)$. Also, for $s \in S, v^{2} s-s v^{2} \in[K, K]$ so that $u^{2}\left(v^{2} s-s v^{2}\right) u^{2}=0$, leading to $u^{2} v^{2} S u^{2} v^{2}=(0)$. This gives $u^{2} v^{2} R u^{2} v^{2}=(0)$, hence $u^{2} v^{2}=0$. Linearizing on $u$, $(u w+w 1) v^{2}=0$, that is $u w v^{2}=0$. Linearizing on $v$ we get $u w v x=0$ for $u, w, v, x \in N$. But this says $u v w N=(0)$, whence by Lemma 2.7, $u v w=0$, hence $u v=0$ hence $u=0$. (Again using Lemma 2.7.) Thus $\mathrm{N}=(0)$.

Note that in the course of proving the result we showed that if the characteristic of R is 3 then $[\mathrm{V}, \mathrm{V}]=(0)$ implies $\mathrm{V}=(0)$ for V a Lie ideal of $[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$. So in what follows we may assume that R is of characteristic not 3 .

LEMMA 2.11, If V is a Lie ideal of $[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$ such that $[\mathrm{V}, \mathrm{V}]=(0)$ then $\mathrm{V}=(0)$.

Proof. In the proof of the previous lemma we got that the non-zero elements of $V$ are invertible. Sincefor $u \in V, k \in K$, $u k-k u \in[K, K],[u,[u,[u, k]]]=0$. Writing $d(k)=u k-k u$, $d^{3}(k)=0$ for $k \in K$.

Now kuk $\in K$, hence $d^{3}(k u k)=0$. Working this out we get $3\left(d^{2}(k) u d(k)+d(k) u d^{2}(k)\right)=0$, so applying $d$ to this we get $d^{2}(k) u d^{2}(k)=0$. Since $d^{2}(k) \in V$, either it is 0 or invertible. We thus get $d^{2}(k)=0$ for all $k \in K$. Thus $d^{2}(k u k)=0$, hence $d(k) u d(k)=0$, If $k<[K, K]$, then $d(k) \in V$ so is 0 or invertible. The net result is $d(k)=0$ for $k \in[K, K]$. But then $u$ centralizes $[K, K]$, hence $[\overline{K, K}]=R$, putting $u \in Z$, a contradiction. Thus $\mathrm{V}=\langle 0\rangle$.

We have all the pieces to prove the basic
THEOREM 2.15. If R is a simple ring of characteristic not 2 and if $\operatorname{dim} R / Z>16$ then any proper Lie ideal of $[K, K]$ is in Z .

Proof. In case the involution is of the second kind this is contained in Theorem 2.1. If the involution is of the first kind and if $U$ is a proper Lie ideal of $[K, K]$, by Theorem 2.14, $[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}],[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}]]=(0)$, hence by Lemma $2.11[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}]=(0)$, By Lemma 2.11 again we get $\mathrm{U}=(0)$.
