## JORDAN HOMOMORPHISMS AND DERIVATIONS

Having studied the Jordan structure of certain rings and subsets of rings we now turn to the study of mappings related to the Jordan structure. We shall say that $R$ is 2 -torsion free if in $R 2 x=0$ forces $x=0$.

DEFINITION. A ring R is a prime ring if $\mathrm{aRb}=(0)$ implies that $\mathrm{a}=0$ or $\mathrm{b}=0$.

The definition just given is equivalent to:

1. The product of two non-zero 2-sided ideals of R is not ( 0 ).
2. The left annihilator of a non-zero left ideal is ( 0 ).

DEFINITION, A mapping ' from a ring $R$ into a ring $R^{\prime}$ which is 2 -torsion free is a Jordan homomorphism if $(a+b)^{\mathrm{r}}=a^{\prime}+$ and $(a b+b a)^{\prime}=a^{\prime} b^{\prime}+b^{\prime} a^{\prime}$.

We shall define a Jordan homomorphism even when there is 2 -torsion a little later. When $R^{\prime}$ is 2 -torsion free note that we could have defined a Jordan homomorphism by merely insisting that $\left(a^{2}\right)^{t}=\left(a^{t}\right)^{2}$.

We begin with
LEMMA 3.1. If ' is a Jordan homomorphism of R into $\mathrm{R}^{\prime}$ which is 2 -torsion tree then $(a b a)^{\prime}=a^{\prime} b^{\prime} a^{\prime}$ for all $a, b \in R$.

Proof. Let $x \circ y=x y+y x$; then $(a \circ(a \circ b))^{t}=a^{\prime} \circ\left(a^{\prime} \circ b^{\prime}\right)$. Evaluating, using $\left(\mathrm{a}^{2}\right)^{\mathrm{t}}=\left(\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{t}}\right)^{2}$ we get $2(\mathrm{aba})^{\mathrm{t}}=2 \mathrm{a}^{\prime} \mathrm{b}^{\mathrm{t}} \mathrm{a}^{\prime}$ hence $(a b a)^{1}=a^{2} b^{2} a^{2}$.

We use the lemma to define Jordan homomorphism even in the case of 2-torsion by defining a Jordan homomorphism ' to be an additive mapping such that $\left(a^{2}\right)^{\prime}=\left(a^{\prime}\right)^{2}$ and $\left(a_{b a}\right)^{\prime}=a^{\prime} b^{\prime} a^{\prime}$. In the 2 -torsion free case this is the same as the original definition we gave.

Linearizing $(\mathrm{aba})^{\mathrm{t}}=\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{t}} \mathrm{b}^{\mathrm{t}} \mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{t}}$ on a we get that ( $\left.\mathrm{abc}+\mathrm{cba}\right)^{\prime} =a^{t} b^{t} c^{t}+c^{t} b^{t} a^{t}$. We use this to prove

LEMMA 3.2. If ' is a Jordan homomorphism of $R$ into $R^{\prime}$ then $\left((a b)^{\prime}-a^{\prime} b^{\prime}\right)\left((b a)^{\prime}-a^{\prime} b^{\prime}\right)=0$ and $\left((b a)^{\prime}-a^{\prime} b^{\prime}\right)\left((a b)^{\prime}-a^{\prime} b^{\prime}\right)=0$ for all $\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b}, \mathrm{R}$.

Proof. We expand

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left\langle(a b)^{t}-a^{\prime} b^{\prime}\right)\left((a b)^{\prime}-b^{\prime} a^{\prime}\right) & =\left((a b)^{\prime}\right)^{2}+a^{\prime}\left(b^{\prime}\right)^{2} a^{\prime}-(a b)^{\prime} b^{\prime} a^{\prime}-a^{\prime} b^{\prime}(a b)^{\prime} \\
& =\left\langle(a b)^{2}+a b^{2} a-a b b a-(a b)^{2}\right)^{\prime} \\
& =0
\end{aligned}
$$

Similarly for the other assertion.
Note that this is enough to answer the question about the nature of Jordan homomorphisms for the special case in which $\mathrm{R}^{\prime}$ has no zero divisors, for then we get $(\mathrm{ab})^{\prime}=\mathrm{a}^{\prime} \mathrm{b}^{\prime}$ or $(a b)^{\prime}=b^{\prime} a^{\prime}$ for every pair $a, b \in R$. From this it is an easy exercise to prove that $(x y)^{\prime}=x^{\prime} y^{\prime}$ for all $x, y \in R$ or $(x y)^{\prime}=y^{\prime} x^{\prime}$ for all $x, y \in R$, that is, the mapping is either a homomorphism or an anti-homomorphism.

We want to show that this conclusion holds in the much more general case wherein $R^{t}$ is any prime ring, provided the mapping is onto.

DEFINITION, For $\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b} \in \mathrm{R}, \mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{b}}=(\mathrm{ab})^{\prime}-\mathrm{a}^{\prime} \mathrm{b}^{\prime}$ and $a_{b}=(a b)^{\prime}-b^{\prime} a^{\prime}$.

A few easy properties of these symbols:

1. $a^{b}=-b^{a}$ (this is merely a restatement of the fact that ' is a Jordan homomorphism). Similarly $\mathrm{a}_{\mathrm{b}}=-\mathrm{b}_{\mathrm{a}}$.
2. $\left[a^{t}, b^{t}\right]=a_{b}-a^{b}$
3. $[a, b]^{t}=a_{b}+a^{b}$
4. $\mathrm{a}_{\mathrm{b}}{ }_{\mathrm{b}}=\mathrm{a}_{\mathrm{b}} \mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{b}}=0$ (Lemma 3. 2)

LEMMA 3.3. For $\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b}, \mathrm{r} \in \mathrm{R}$,

$$
([a, b] r)^{\prime}=r^{\prime} a^{b}+a_{b} r^{\prime}
$$

and

$$
(r[a, b])^{t}=a^{b} r^{t}+r^{\prime} a b_{b} .
$$

Proof. $r^{\prime} a^{b}+a_{b} r^{\prime}=r^{\prime}(a b)^{\prime}-r^{\prime} a^{\prime} b^{\prime}-b^{\prime} a^{\prime} r^{\prime}+(a b)^{\prime} r^{\prime} =(r a b+a b r)^{\prime}-(r a b+b a r)^{\prime}=((a b-b a) r)^{\prime}$. Similarly for the other half of the lemma.

LEMMA 3.4. If ' is a Jordan homomorphism of R into $\mathrm{R}^{\prime}$ then $a_{b} r^{\prime} a^{b}+a^{b} r^{\prime} a_{b}=0$.

Proof. From Lemma 3.3,

1. $\quad r^{\prime} a^{b}+a_{b} r^{\mathrm{l}}=([a, b] r)^{\mathrm{l}}$.

Multiply by $a^{b}$ from the left, using $a^{b} a_{b}=0$ we get
2.

$$
a^{b} r^{\prime} a^{b}=a^{b}([a ; b] r)^{\prime} .
$$

Multiplying (1) from the right by $\mathrm{a}_{\mathrm{b}}$ we get
3. $\quad \mathrm{a}_{\mathrm{b}} \mathrm{r}^{2} \mathrm{a}_{\mathrm{b}}=([\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b}] \mathrm{r})^{1} \mathrm{a}_{\mathrm{b}}$.

In $\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{b}} \mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{1}}+\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{1}} \mathrm{a}_{\mathrm{b}}=(\mathrm{r}[\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b}])^{\prime}$ replace r by $[\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b}] \mathrm{r}$; using 1, 3 and Lemma 3.1 we get

$$
a^{b} r^{\prime} a^{b}+a_{b} r^{\prime} a_{b}=[a, b]^{\prime} r^{\prime}[a, b]^{\prime}=\left(a^{b}+a_{b}\right) r^{\prime}\left(a^{b}+a_{b}\right) .
$$

Expanding out we end up with $\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{b}} \mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{a}} \mathrm{a}_{\mathrm{b}}+\mathrm{a}_{\mathrm{b}} \mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{t}}{ }^{\mathrm{b}}=0$, the assertion of the lemma.

We now prove the
THEOREM 3.1. Let ' be a Jordan homomorphism of $R$ onto $\mathrm{R}^{\mathrm{t}}$, If $\mathrm{R}^{\prime}$ is a prime ring then ${ }^{\mathrm{t}}$ is either a homomorphism or an anti-homomorphism.

Proof. By the previous lemma
(1) $\quad a^{b} r^{\prime} a_{b}+a_{b} r^{\prime} a_{b}=0$.

Suppose that $\left(c^{d}\right)^{2} \neq 0$ for some $c, d \in R$. Multiplying (1) by $c^{d}$ (where $a=c, b=d$ in (1)), we get $\left(c^{d}\right)^{2} R^{\prime} c_{d}=0$. Since $R^{\prime}$ is prime and $\left(c^{d}\right)^{2} \neq 0$, then $c_{d}=0$.

Thus if $\left(c^{d}\right)^{2} \neq 0$ we can corclude that $c_{d}=0$.
We linearize (1) on b to get

$$
\begin{equation*}
a^{b} r^{t_{a}}{ }_{x}+a^{x} r^{\prime} a_{b}+a_{b} r^{\prime} a^{x}+a_{x} r^{\prime} a^{b}=0 . \tag{2}
\end{equation*}
$$

and linearize $\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{b}_{\mathrm{b}}}=0$ to get

$$
\begin{equation*}
a^{b} a_{x}+a^{x} a_{b}=0 . \tag{3}
\end{equation*}
$$

Since $\left(c^{d}\right)^{2} \neq 0, c_{d}=0$ we get on putting $a=c, b=d$ in (2) and (3)

$$
\begin{align*}
& c^{d} r^{\prime} c_{x}+c_{x} r^{\prime} c^{d}=0  \tag{4}\\
& c^{d} c_{x}=0
\end{align*}
$$

for all $r, x \in R$,
Multuplying the first equation of (4) from the left by $c^{d}$, we get since $c^{d} c_{x}=0, \quad\left(c^{d}\right)^{2} R^{\prime} c_{x}=0$; since $R^{\prime}$ is prime and $\left(c^{d}\right)^{2} \neq 0, \quad c_{x}=0$.

Thus, if $\left(c^{d}\right)^{2} \neq 0$ then $c_{x}=0$ for all $x \in R$. Also $\left(d^{c}\right)^{2}=\left(-c^{d}\right)^{2} \neq 0$ hence $d_{x}=0$ for all $x, R$. Similarly if $\left(c_{d}\right)^{2} \neq 0$ then $c^{x}=0$ for all $x \in R$.

We linearize (2) on a to get

$$
\begin{align*}
a^{b} r^{\prime} y_{x} & +a^{x} r^{\prime} y_{b}+y^{b} r^{\prime} a  \tag{5}\\
& +a_{b} r^{\prime} y^{x}+y^{x} r^{\prime} a_{b} r^{\prime} y^{b}+y_{b} r^{\prime} a^{x}+y_{x} r^{\prime} a^{b}=0 .
\end{align*}
$$

If $\left(c^{d}\right)^{2} \neq 0$ then $c_{x} \Rightarrow d_{x}=0$ for all $x$. Putting $a=c$, $\mathrm{b}=\mathrm{d}$ in (S) yields

$$
\begin{equation*}
c^{d} r^{1} y_{x}+y_{x} r^{1} c^{d}=0 \tag{6}
\end{equation*}
$$

for all $\mathrm{x}, \mathrm{y} \in \mathrm{R}$.
Now from $x^{y} x_{y}=0$ linearized on $x, y$ we get

$$
\begin{equation*}
x^{y} w_{z}+w^{y} x_{z}+x^{z} w_{y}+w^{z} x_{y}=0 . \tag{7}
\end{equation*}
$$

In (7) putting $w=c, z=d$ we get since $c_{r}=d_{s}=0$ for all r, s, $c^{d} x_{y}=0$. Multiply (6) by $c^{d}$ to now get $\left(c^{d}\right)^{2} R^{\prime} y_{x}=(0)$ hence $y_{x}=0$ for all $x, y \in R$. That is, $(y x)^{1}=x^{1} y^{t}$, which is to say that ${ }^{\mathrm{r}}$ is an anti-homomorphism. Similarly, if for some $\mathrm{c}, \mathrm{d}$, $\left(\mathrm{o}_{\mathrm{d}}\right)^{2} \neq 0$ then ' must be a homomorphism.

We are left to consider the situation in which $\left(x^{y}\right)^{2}=0$, $\left(x_{y}\right)^{2}=0$ for all $x, y \in R$. But then, since $\left[x^{\prime}, y^{\prime}\right]=x_{y}-x^{y}$, and since $x_{y} x^{y}=x^{y} x_{y}=\left(x^{y}\right)^{2}=\left(x_{y}\right)^{2}=0,\left[x^{\prime}, y^{\prime}\right]^{2}=0$ for all $x^{\prime} y^{\prime} \& R^{\prime}$. Since all commutators in $R^{\prime}$ have square 0 , if $\left(a^{\prime}\right)^{2}=0$, we get a'R' is a nil ideal of index 3; by Lemma 1.1 $R^{\prime}$ would have a nilpotent ideal which is impossible in a prime ring. Thus $\mathrm{R}^{\prime}$ has no nilpotent elements, hence $\left[x^{\prime}, y^{\prime}\right]=0$, that is $R^{\prime}$ is commutative But being prime it would have no zero-divisors, so as remarked at the end of the proof of Lemma 3.2 this forces ${ }^{1}$ to be a homomorphism. We have now proved the theorem.

We consider mappings now that are additive and preserve $n^{\text {th }}$ powers.

DEFINITION. A mapping $\psi$ of R into $\mathrm{R}^{\prime}$ is an n-Jordan mapping if $\varphi$ is additive and $\varphi\left(\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{n}}\right)=\varphi(\mathrm{a})^{\mathrm{n}}$ for all $\mathrm{a} \in \mathrm{R}$.

THEOREM 3.2. Let q be an $n$-Jordan mapping from $R$ onto a prime ring $\mathrm{R}^{\prime}$ whose characteristic is 0 or larger than n . Then if R has a unit element, $\varphi=\in T$ where $T$ is either a hornomorphism or an anti-homomorphism and is an ( $\mathrm{n}-1$ )-st root of unity lying in the center of $\mathrm{R}^{\mathrm{V}}$.

Proof. Since $\varphi\left(x^{n}\right)=\varphi(x)^{n}$ linearizing on $x$ we get

$$
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{\pi \in S_{n}} \varphi\left(x_{\pi(1)} \cdots x_{\pi(n)}\right)=\sum_{\pi \in S_{n}} \varphi\left(x_{\pi(1)}\right) \cdots \varphi\left(x_{\pi(n)}\right), \tag{1}
\end{equation*}
$$

where $S_{n}$ is the symmetric group of degree $n$.
Let $\alpha=\varphi(1)$ thus $\alpha^{n}=\varphi(1)^{n}=\varphi(1)=\alpha$. In (1) put $\mathrm{x}_{1}=\mathrm{x}$, $x_{2}=\ldots x_{n}=1$. We get

$$
\begin{equation*}
n!\varphi(x)=(n-1)!\left(\alpha^{n-1} \varphi(x)+\alpha^{n-2} \varphi(x) \alpha+\ldots+\varphi(x) \alpha^{n-1}\right) . \tag{2}
\end{equation*}
$$

Hence $n \varphi(x)=\left(\alpha^{n-1} \varphi(x)+\ldots+\varphi(x) \alpha^{n-1}\right)$, since the characteristic of $\mathrm{R}^{\prime}$ exceeds n . Commuting this with $\alpha$, since $\alpha^{n}=\alpha$ we get $(n-1)(\alpha \varphi(x)-\varphi(x) \alpha)=0$, hence $\alpha \varphi(x)=\varphi(x) \alpha$. Therefore $\alpha$ is an ( $n-1$ )-st root of unity and lies in the center of $R^{+}$.

We return to (1) putting $x_{1}=x, x_{2}=y$ and $x_{3}=\ldots=x_{n}=1$. (1) then gives, from the condition on the characteristic, $\left.\phi(x y+y x)=\alpha^{n-2}(\varphi(x))+\varphi(y) \varphi(x)\right)$. Let $\tau=\alpha^{n-1} \varphi$; then $\tau$ is a Jordan homomorphism of R onto $\mathrm{R}^{\prime}$. By Theorem 3.1 it is either a homomorphism or anti-homomorphism. This completes the proof of the theorem.

We now turn to the question of Jordan derivations. We assume all ringe to be 2 -torsion free.

DEFINITION, A Jordan derivation ' on R is an additive mapping of R into itself such that $\left(\mathrm{a}^{2}\right)^{\mathrm{t}}=\mathrm{aa}^{\mathrm{t}}+\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{t}} \mathrm{a}$.

Note that this is equivalent to

$$
(a \circ b)^{\prime}=a^{\prime} e b+a \circ b^{\prime}
$$

We begin with
LEMMA 3.5. If $'$ is a Jordan derivation on R then $(\mathrm{aba})^{\mathrm{t}}=\mathrm{a}^{\prime} \mathrm{ba}+\mathrm{ab}^{\prime} \mathrm{a}+\mathrm{aba}$ for $\mathrm{all} \mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b} \in \mathrm{R}$.

Proof. We compute ( $\mathrm{ao}(\mathrm{aob}))^{t}$ in two ways.

$$
\left(a^{2} \circ b+2 a b a\right)^{\prime}=\left(a \circ(a \circ b)^{\prime}=a^{\prime} \circ(a \circ b)+a \circ(a \circ b)^{\prime} .\right.
$$

Evaluating we get the result.
Linearizing we have that
COROLLARY, For $a, b, c \in R$

$$
(a b c+c b a)^{\prime}=a^{\prime} b c+a b^{\prime} c+a b c^{\prime}+c^{\prime} b a+c b^{\prime} a+c b a^{\prime} .
$$

Essential in what follows is
LEMMA 3.6. For $a, b \in R$,

$$
\left((a b)^{\prime}-a^{\prime} b-a b^{\prime}\right)(a b-b a)=0 .
$$

Proof. Let $w=((a b) a b+b a(a b))^{\prime}=(a b)^{\prime} a b+(a b) a^{\prime} b+a b a b^{\prime} +b^{\prime} a(a b)+b a^{\prime} a b+b a(a b)^{\prime} . \quad$ But $w=(a b)^{2}+\left(b a^{2} b\right)^{\prime}=a b(a b)^{\prime} +(a b)^{\prime} a b+b^{\prime} a^{2} b+b\left(a a^{\prime}+a^{\prime} a\right) b+b a^{2} b^{\prime}$. Comparing the results we get the lemma.

DEFINITION. Let $a^{b}=(a b)^{c}-a^{c} b-a b^{c}$.
In these terms the lemma reads $\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathrm{ab}-\mathrm{ba})=0$. Note that $\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{b}}$ is linear in a and b and $\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{b}}=-\mathrm{b}^{\overline{\mathrm{a}}}$.

Linearizing Lernma 3.6 on a we get the

COROLLARY 1. $\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathrm{cb}-\mathrm{bc})+\mathrm{c}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathrm{ab}-\mathrm{ba})=0$ for $\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b}, \mathrm{c} \in \mathrm{R}$.
COROLLARY 2. $\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathrm{a} \mathrm{b}-\mathrm{ba})+\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathrm{ac}-\mathrm{ca})=0$.
This last corollary is merely linearization on b .
LEMMA 3.7. If R is a prime ring and if $\mathrm{ab}=0$ then $a^{b}=0$.

Proof. If $\mathrm{ab}=\mathrm{ba}=0$ then by the corollary to Lemma 3.6, $a^{b}(c b-b c)=0$ for all $c \cdot b$ is not in $Z$ since $Z$ has no zero divisors; but then $\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathrm{cb}-\mathrm{bc})=0$ leads us to $\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathrm{cxb}-\mathrm{bcx})=0$, hence $\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{b}} \mathrm{R}(\mathrm{xb}-\mathrm{bx})=(0)$. This gives $\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{b}}=0$.

Suppose $\mathrm{ab}=0, \mathrm{ba} \neq 0$. Then by the corollary 2 to Lemma 3.6, $a^{b}(a c-c a) b=0$, hence $a^{b} a R b=0$. Since $R$ is prime, $a^{b} a=0$ for any $b$ with $a b=0$. But then since $a^{b}=(a b)^{\prime}-a^{\prime} b-a b^{\prime}$, we get $\left(a^{\prime} b+a b^{\prime}\right) a=0$. For any $r \in R, a b r b=0$, hence $\left(a^{\prime} b r b+a(b r b)^{\prime}\right) a=0$; since $(b r b)^{\prime}=b^{\prime} r b+b r^{\prime} b+b r b^{\prime}$, we get $\left(a^{\prime} b r b+a b^{\prime} r b\right) a=0$, whence $\left(a^{\prime} b+a b^{\prime}\right) R b a=0$ and since $b a \neq 0$ it follows that $a^{2} b+a b^{\prime}=0$. Thus $a^{b}=0$.

Note that in this case $b^{a}=-a^{b}=0$, so that $(b a)^{\prime}=b^{\prime} a+b a^{\prime}$.
LEMMA 3.8. If $\mathrm{ab}=0$ then for $c \in \mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}$ prime, $(c(b a))^{t}=c^{\prime}(b a)+c(b a)^{\prime}$.

Proof. By the corollary to Lemma 3.5

$$
\begin{aligned}
(c b a)^{\prime}=(c b a+a b c) & =c^{\prime} b a+c b^{\prime} a+c b a^{\prime}+a^{\prime} b c+a b^{\prime} c+a b c^{\prime} \\
& =c^{\prime}(b a)+c\left(b^{\prime} a+b a^{\prime}\right)+\left(a^{\prime} b+a b^{\prime}\right) c
\end{aligned}
$$

By Lemma 3,7, $\mathrm{a}^{\prime} \mathrm{b}+\mathrm{a} \mathrm{b}^{\prime}=0$ and $\mathrm{b}^{\prime} \mathrm{a}+\mathrm{b} \mathrm{a}^{\prime}=(\mathrm{ba})^{\prime}$. Thus the lemma.

DEFINITION. $V=\left\{x \in R \mid(a x)^{\prime}=a^{\prime} x+a x^{\prime}\right.$ all $\left.a\right\}$.
Note that by Lemma 3.8 , if $a b=0$ then $b a \in V$.

We need one more lemma.
LEMMA 3.9. If $\mathrm{v}, \mathrm{w} \in \mathrm{V}$ then $\mathrm{a}{ }^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathrm{vw}-\mathrm{wv})=0$ for all $\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b} \in \mathrm{R}$.

Proof. By the corollary to Lemma 3.5

$$
a^{b}(b v-v b)+v^{b}(a b-b a)=0,
$$

hence, since $v^{b}=0, a^{b}(b v-v b)=0$ for all $b$. We linearize this on $b$ to get $a^{b}(c v-v c)+a^{c}(b v-v b)=0$. Putting $c=w \in V$, since $a^{w}=0$ we get $a^{b}(w v-v w)=0$.

We now prove the
THEOREM 3.3. If R is a prime ring of characteristic different from ${ }^{2}$ then any Jordan derivation of $R$ is a derivation of R .

Proof. Suppose that $a^{b} \neq 0$. Given $c \in R$, since $c^{b}(c b-b c)=0$ (Lemma 3.6), we get, for any $r \in R$, $v=(c b-b c) r c^{b} \in V$ by Lemma 3.8. Similarly for $s \in R$, $(\mathrm{ab}-\mathrm{ba}) \mathrm{sa}^{\mathrm{b}} \in \mathrm{V}$. Using these $\mathrm{v}, \mathrm{w}$ in Lemma 3.9 in conjunction with Lemma 3.6 we get $a^{b}(c b-b c) R c^{b}(a b-b a) R a^{b}=(0)$. Since $R$ is prime and $a^{b} \neq 0$ we get $a^{b}(c b-b c)=0$ or $c^{b}(a b-b a)=0$; but $a^{b}(c b-b c)+c^{b}(a b-b a)=0$ by the corollary to Lemma 3.6, whence $\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathrm{cb}-\mathrm{bc})=0$ for all $\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b}, \mathrm{c} \in \mathrm{R}$.

If $\mathrm{b} / \mathrm{Z}$ since the annihilator of all $\mathrm{cb}-\mathrm{bc}, \mathrm{c}<\mathrm{R}$ is an ideal we get $\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{b}}=0$. If $\mathrm{Z} \neq \mathrm{R}$ and $\mathrm{b} \in \mathrm{Z}$, choosing $\mathrm{c} / \mathrm{Z}$ then $b+c<Z$ hence $0=a^{c}=a^{b+c}=a^{b}+a^{c}$ leading to $a^{b}=0$.
Finally if $Z=R$, then the definition of a Jordan derivation yields $2(a b)^{\prime}=2\left(a^{\prime} b+a b^{\prime}\right)$, whence by our assumption on the characteristic we conclude that $(\mathrm{ab})^{\prime}=\mathrm{a}^{\prime} \mathrm{b}+\mathrm{ba}^{\prime}$.

Thus in all cases $a^{b}=0$ for $a l l ~ a, b \in R$, therefore ' is $a$ derivation of $R$.

If one checks the proof given one sees that the assumption that the characteristic of $R$ be different from 2 enters only at two pointsi in proving $(a b a)^{t}=a^{\prime} b a+a b^{\prime} a+a b a^{\prime}$ and at the very end of the argument just given.

If we redefine a Jordan mapping by $\left(\mathrm{a}^{2}\right)^{\mathrm{x}}=\mathrm{aa}^{\mathrm{x}}+\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{a}} \mathrm{a}$ and $(\mathrm{aba})^{\mathrm{I}}=\mathrm{a}^{\top} \mathrm{b} \mathrm{a}+\mathrm{a} \mathrm{b}^{\mathrm{I}} \mathrm{a}+\mathrm{aba} \mathrm{b}^{\mathrm{I}}$, then in characteristic not 2 we have imposed no extra restriction yet in characteristic 2 it allows us to conclude

THEOREM 3.4. If R is a prime ring and ${ }^{\mathrm{t}}$ is a Jordan derivation (as newly redefined) of $R$ then ${ }^{\prime}$ is a derivation except if $R$ is both commutative (and so an integral domain) and of characteristic 2.

At this point we should like to redo the theorem on Jordan homomorphisms from a slightly different point of view. It follows fairly easily from a simple result about prime rings but suffers from the defect that we must insist that the characteristic be different from 2. Outside of this lemma which we are about to prove we only need the result of Lemma 3.1.

LEMMA 3.10. Let $R$ be a prime ring of characteristic not 2 and suppose $\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b} \in \mathrm{R}$ are such that $\mathrm{arb}+\mathrm{bra}=0$ for all $\mathrm{r} \in \mathrm{R}$. Then either $\mathrm{a}=0$ or $\mathrm{b}=0$.

Proof. By assumption $\mathrm{a} r \mathrm{~b}+\mathrm{bra}=0$. Replace r by sat with $s, t \in R$. We get asatb + bsata $=0$. But bsa $=-a s b$ and atb = -bta; substituting these we get -asbta - asbta $=0$, that is, 2aRbRa $=(0)$. Since $R$ is prime of characteristic not 2 this immediately gives $\mathrm{a}=0$ or $\mathrm{b}=0$.

We now reprove Theorem 3, 1, assuming that ${ }^{\mathrm{t}}$ is a Jordan homomorphism of $R$ onto $R^{\prime}, R^{\mathrm{t}}$ of characteristic not 2 .

Consider $\mathrm{u}=(\mathrm{ab})^{1}-\mathrm{a}^{1} \mathrm{~b}^{1}, \mathrm{v}=(\mathrm{ab})^{t}-\mathrm{b}^{1} \mathrm{a}^{t}$; then

$$
\begin{aligned}
u r^{\prime} v & =\left((a b)^{\prime}-a^{\prime} b^{\prime}\right) r^{\prime}\left((a b)^{\prime}-b^{\prime} a^{\prime}\right) \\
& =(a b)^{\prime} r^{\prime}(a b)^{\prime}+a^{\prime} b^{\prime} r^{\prime} b^{\prime} a^{\prime}-a^{\prime} b^{\prime} r^{\prime}(a b)^{\prime}-(a b)^{\prime} r^{\prime} b^{\prime} a^{\prime} \\
& =(a b r a b)^{\prime}+(a b r b a)^{\prime}-a^{\prime} b^{\prime} r^{\prime}(a b)^{\prime}-(a b)^{\prime} r^{\prime} b^{\prime} a^{\prime},
\end{aligned}
$$

using Lemma 3.1. Similarly,

$$
v r u=(a b r a b)^{t}+(b a r a b)^{t}-\left(b^{\prime} a^{\prime} r^{\prime}(a b)^{t}-(a b)^{t} r^{\prime} a^{\prime} b^{\prime}\right.
$$

Thus

$$
\begin{aligned}
u r v+v r u & =\{2 a b r a b+a b r b a+b a r a b\}^{\prime} \\
& -\left\{\left(a^{\prime} b^{\prime}+b^{\prime} a^{\prime}\right) r^{\prime}(a b)^{\prime}+(a b)^{\prime} r^{\prime}\left(a^{\prime} b^{\prime}+b^{\prime} a^{\prime}\right)\right\} \\
& =\{2 a b r a b+a b r b a+b a r a b\}^{\prime} \\
& -\{(a b+b a) r(a b)+(a b) r(a b+b a)\} \\
& =\left\{(2 a b r a b+a b r b a+b a r a b\}^{\prime}\right. \\
& -\{(a b+b a) r(a b)+(a b) r(a b+b a)\}^{\prime}
\end{aligned}
$$

(using the corollary to Lemma 3.1)

$$
=0 .
$$

Since $u r^{1} v+v r^{1} u=0$ for all $r^{\prime}, R^{\prime}$, using Lemma 3.10 we have $u=0$ or $v=0$, that is, $(a b)^{\prime}=a^{\prime} b^{\prime}$ or $(a b)^{\prime}=b^{\prime} a^{\prime}$.

Given $a<R$ let $W_{a}=\left(b \in R \mid(a b)^{+}=a^{+} b^{+}\right\}$and $\left.V_{a}=\{b \in R](a b)^{\prime}=b^{\prime} a^{\prime}\right\}$. Clearly each of $W_{a}$ and $V_{a}$ are additive subgroups of R . Moreover, by the discussion given, $R$ is the set-theoretic union of $W_{a}$ and $V_{a}$. But a group can not be the set-theoretic union of two proper subgroups, hence $W_{a}=R$ or $V_{a}=R$, that is, $(a b)^{\prime}=a^{\prime} b^{\prime}$ for all $b \in R$ or $(a b)^{\prime}=b^{\prime} a^{\prime}$ for all $\mathrm{b} \in \mathrm{R}$. Let $\mathrm{W}=\left\{\mathrm{a} \in \mathrm{R} \mid(\mathrm{ax})^{\mathrm{t}}=\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{t}} \mathrm{x}^{\mathrm{t}}\right.$ for all $\left.\mathrm{x}<\mathrm{R}\right\}$ and $V=\left\{a, R \mid\langle a x\rangle^{t}=x^{t} a^{t}\right.$ for all $\left.x \in R\right\}$; both $W$ and $V$ are additive subgroups of R and, from the above, their union is R , In consequence, $W=R$ or $V=R$. If $W=R$ then $(x y)^{t}=x^{1} y^{t}$ fot all $x, y, R$ and $'$ is a homomorphism; if $V=R$ then $(x y)^{\prime}=y^{\prime} x^{\prime}$ for all $x, y \in R$ and ' is an anti-homomorphism. This is Theorem 3.1.

We close the chapter with a discussion, without proofs, of some highly interesting theorems proved in the last few years by Martindale.

The first of these concerns Jordan maps of the symmetric elements of a ring with involution, that is, additive maps o defined on the symmetric elements such that $\phi\left(s^{2}\right)=\phi(s)^{2}$ for $s$ symmetric. He proves:

Let $R$ be a ring with involution, \#, such that:
a) $R$ contains three non-zero orthogonal idempotents $e_{1}, e_{2}, e_{3}$ such that $e_{1}+e_{2}+e_{3}=1$.
b) $e_{i} R e_{j} R e_{i}=e_{i} R e_{i}$ for $i, j=1,2,3$.

Let $\phi$ be a Jordan homomorphism of the symmetric elements $S$ of $R$ in a ring $R^{\prime}$ whose characteristic is not 2. Then $\phi$ can be extended uniquely to an associative homomorphism $\Phi$ of R into $\mathrm{R}^{\prime}$.

Martindale has obtained very decisive results about Lie homomorphisms of one ring into another. A Lie homomorphism $\phi$ is an additive map such that $\phi(a b-b a)=\phi(a) \phi(b)-\phi(b) \phi(a)$. We now give the statement of some of these.

1. Let $\phi$ be a Lie isomorphism of a primitive ring $R$ onto a primitive ring $R^{\prime}$, where $R$ is not of characteristic 2 or 3 . Suppose that $R$ contains three orthogonal idempotents whose sum is 1 . Then $\phi$ is of the form $\sigma+\tau$ where $\sigma$ is either an isomorphism or the negative of an anti-isomorphism of $R$ into a primitive ring $L^{\prime}$ containing $R^{\prime}$ and $\tau$ is an additive mapping of R into the center of $\mathrm{L}^{\prime}$ taking all commutators ab - ba into 0 .

In an unpublished result (which will appear) he has extended this result significantly in the following way:

1. $R$ and $R^{\prime}$ are merely assumed to be prime rings.
2. Instead of three orthogonal idempotents he only requires that $1, R$ and $R$ have an idempotent $e \neq 0,1$.

With these weakened hypotheses he still gets the same conclusion about the nature of Lie isomorphisms.

In the case when $R$ and $R^{\prime}$ are simple and $R$ is of characteristic not 2 or 3 with unit element and having an idempotent e \& 0,1 a little more can be said, namely, that a Lie isomorphism of $R$ onto $R^{t}$ is of the form $\sigma+\tau, \sigma$ an isomorphism or the negative of an anti-isomorphismof $R$ onto $R^{\prime}$ (here one does not need the extension $\mathrm{L}^{\dagger}$ or $\left.\mathrm{R}^{\prime}\right)$ and $\tau$ is an additive map of $R$ into the center of $R^{\prime}$ vanishing on commutators ab - ba,

A student of Martindale, R. A. Howland has recently proved a related result: Let R be a simple ring of characteristic not 2 or 3 with three non-zero orthogonal idempotents whose sum is 1 and let $T$ be a simple ring with 1. Then any Lie isomorphism of $[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$ onto $[\mathrm{T}, \mathrm{T}]$ can be uniquely extended to an associative isomorphism of R onto T .

These results give much information about the automorphisms of $R$ and $[R, R]$ as Lie algebras in terms of associative automorphisms and trace-like mappings of $R$ itself.

Finally there is the closely connected question of Lie derivations, that is, additive mappings, \%, such that

$$
\delta([a, b])=[\delta(a), b]+[a, \delta(b)] .
$$

(Tier-1 Error)

Let $R$ be a primitive ring containing an idempotent $e \neq 0,1$ and suppose that the characteristic of R is not 2 . Then any Lie derivation of $R$ is of the form $D+T$ where $D$ is an associative derivation of R into a primitive ring $\overline{\mathrm{R}} \supset \mathrm{R}$ and T is an
$(a, b) \sim(c, d)$ if $d_{1} a=b_{1} c$ where $b_{1} d=d_{1} b, b_{1}$ regular, (And so $d_{1}$ is regular.) We claim this is independent of $b_{1}, d_{1}$ for if $b_{2} d=d_{2} b$ pick $e_{1}, e_{2}$ regular so that $e_{2} b_{2}=e_{1} b_{1}$. Then

$$
\begin{aligned}
& e_{1} b_{1} d=e_{1} d_{1} b \\
& e_{2} b_{2} d=e_{2} d_{2} b .
\end{aligned}
$$

By the regularity of $b$, $e_{1} d_{1}=o_{2} d_{2}$, hence from $d_{1} a=b_{1} c$, $e_{2} d_{2} a=e_{1} d_{1} a=e_{1} b_{1} c=e_{2} b_{2} c$ and since $e_{2}$ is regular, $\mathrm{d}_{2} \mathrm{a}=\mathrm{b}_{2} \mathrm{c}$.

We claim the relation defined on $M$ is an equivalence relation. Let the class of ( $\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b}$ ) be written $\mathrm{as} \mathrm{a} / \mathrm{b}$.

If $b_{1} d=d_{1} b$ define $a / b+c / d=\left(d_{1} a+b_{1} c\right) /\left(b_{1} d\right)$ and $(\mathrm{a} / \mathrm{b})(\mathrm{c} / \mathrm{d})=\left(\mathrm{a}_{1} \mathrm{c}\right) /\left(\mathrm{d}_{1} \mathrm{~b}\right)$, where $\mathrm{d}_{1} \mathrm{a}=\mathrm{a}_{1} \mathrm{~d}$.

One can easily verify that the equivalence classes form a ring with unit, $Q(R)$, which is a left quotient ring for $R$.

DEFINITION, If $S \subset R$ is a subset then $f(S)=\{x \in R\} x S=(0)\}$, the left-annihilator of $S$ and $r(S)=\{x \in R \mid S x=(0)\}$, the right annihilator of $S$.

Clearly $\ell(S)$ is a left ideal of $R$, and $r(S)$ is a right ideal of R . We say that a left ideal $\lambda$ of R is a left annihilator if $\lambda=I(S)$ for some $S \subset R$.

We now introduce the Goldie chain conditions.
DEFINITION. $R$ satisfies the Goldie chain conditions if:

1. any ascending chain of left annihilators becomes stationary after some point.
2. $R$ has no infinite direct sum of left ideals.

A ring satisfying the Goldie conditions will be called a Goldie ring.

LEMMA 4.1. Let R be a Goldie ring without zero divisors. Then R has a left quotient ring which is a division ring.

Proof. We shall show that the intersection of any two nonzero left ideals of R is not ( 0 ). Thus if, $\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b} \in \mathrm{R}$, neither 0 , then $\mathrm{Ra} \cap \mathrm{Rb} \neq(0)$ so by Theorem $4,1 \mathrm{R}$ will have a ring of left quotients which will obviously be a division ring.

We claim that there is a left ideal $\mathrm{U} \neq(0)$ of R such that any two non-zero left ideals of $R$ lying in $U$ have a non-zero intersection. If not, there are two non-zero left ideals $\mathrm{U}_{1}, \mathrm{U}_{2}$ with intersection $(0), \mathrm{U}_{1} \cap \mathrm{U}_{2}=(0)$. If in $\mathrm{U}_{1}$ there are two non-zero left ideals of $\mathrm{R} \quad \mathrm{U}_{1}^{(1)}, \mathrm{U}_{1}^{(2)}$ with intersection (0) then $U_{1}^{(1)}+U_{1}^{(2)}+U_{2}$ is a direct sum. Continue this on $U_{1}^{(1)}$, and so on, to get longer and larger direct sums. Since there are no infinite direct sums of left deals of R we finally must stop. We thus produce the required left ideal U of R ,

Suppose that $\lambda, \mu$ are non-zero left ideals of $R$. Pick $a \neq 0$ in U ; then $\lambda \mathrm{a}$ and $\mu \mathrm{a}$ are non-zero left ideals of R lying in U hence must have a non-zero intersection, so there is an $0 \notin \mathrm{x}=\alpha \mathrm{a}=\beta \mathrm{a}$ where $\alpha \in \lambda, \beta \in \mu$. Since R has no zero divisors $0 \neq \alpha=\beta \in \lambda \cap \mu$, whence $\lambda \cap \mu \neq(0)$.

DEFINITION. A ring $R$ will be called an Ore domain if it has no zero divisors and the intersection of any two non-zero left ideals of $R$ is not zero.

Properly speaking such a ring should be called a left Ore domain but for the sake of brevity we drop the "left." Note that by Theorem 4, 1 a ring is an Ore domain if it has a ring of left quotients which is a division ring.

Let $R$ be an Ore domain and let $D$ be the division ring of left quotients of R . If $\mathrm{k}_{1}, \ldots, \mathrm{k}_{\mathrm{n}} \in \mathrm{D}$ then $\mathrm{k}_{i}=\mathrm{a}_{i}^{-1} \mathrm{~b}_{i}$ with
$a_{i}, b_{i} \in R$. We claim that we can find an eloment $a \in R$ and $c_{i} \in R$ such that each $k_{i}=a^{-1} c_{i}$. We proceed by induction on $n$; suppose that $t$ has been found in $R$ such that $k_{1}=t^{-1} d_{1}, \ldots, k_{n-1}=t^{-1} d_{n-1}$, where $d_{1}, \ldots, d_{n-1} \in R$. Now $k_{n}=a^{-1} b_{n}$; moreover there is an element $a \neq 0$ such that $a=s t=u a_{n}$. Then
$k_{1}=a^{-1} s d_{1}, \ldots, k_{n-1}=a^{-1} s d_{n-1}, k_{n}=a^{-1} u b_{n}$ is the desired representation. We shall use this remark often so we record it as

Sublemma A. If R is an Ore domain with a ring of left quotients $D$ then, given $k_{1}, \ldots, k_{n}$ in $D$, wecan find an element $a \in R$, and $c_{1}, \ldots, c_{n} \in R$ such that $k_{i}=a^{-1} c_{i}$ for $i=1,2, \ldots, n$.

We now prove a condition that a ring be an Ore domain.
LEMMA 4.2. Let R be a ring without zero divisors. Then the following are equivalent:

1. R is an Ore domain.
2. In R there is no infinite direct sum of left ideals.
3. If $\lambda \neq(0)$ is a fixed given left ideal of R then there is no infinite direct sum of left ideals of $\lambda$.

Proof. That (1) implies (2) is trivial for any two left ideals in an Ore domain intersect.
(2) implies (3) for if $\lambda \neq(0)$ is a left ideal of $R$ if $\lambda_{1} \neq(0)$ is a left ideal of $\lambda$ then $(0) \neq \lambda \lambda_{1} \subset \lambda_{1}$ and $\lambda \lambda_{1}$ is a left ideal of $R$. So if $\lambda_{1}+\ldots+\lambda_{n}$ is a direct sum of left ideals of $\lambda$ then $\lambda \lambda_{1}+\ldots+\lambda \lambda_{n}$ is one of left ideals of $R$.
(3) implies (1) for suppose $\lambda \neq(0)$ is a left ideal of R having no infinite direct sums. As in Theorem 4.1 we can find a left ideal $\lambda_{1} \neq(0)$ of $\lambda$ in which any two left ideals intersect. Since $\lambda \lambda_{1} \subset \lambda_{1}$ we may assume that $\lambda_{1}$ is a left ideal of $R$. Let $a, b$ be non-zero in R ; pick $\alpha \neq 0$ in $\lambda_{1}$. Then Ra $\alpha$, Rb $\alpha$ are left ideals in $\lambda_{1}$ so intersect, that is thereare $x, y \in R$ with $x a \alpha=y b \alpha \neq 0$. Since $R$ is without zero divisors $x a=y b \neq 0$ follows.

We record one more fact about the relationship between an Ore domain and its ring of quotients.

LEMMA 4.3. Let $E$ be an Ore domain, $K$ its ring of left quotients and suppose that $A \neq(0)$ is a left ideal of $E$. Then $A$ has $K$ as its ring of left quotients.

Proof. Given $k \in K, k=a^{-1} b$ where $a, b \in E$, Pick $c \neq 0$ in $A$; therefore there are elements $x, y, R$, not zero, such that $x a=y c \neq 0$; also there are elements $r, s \in R$ such that $r x b=s c \neq 0$. Bus $k=a^{-1} b=(r x a)^{-1} r x b=(r y c)^{-1} a c=u^{-1} v$ where $u=r y c \& A$ and $v=s c \& A$. This proves the lemma.

We recall from Chapter 3 that a ring $R$ is a prime ring if $\mathrm{aRb}=(0)$ implies that $\mathrm{a}=0$ or $\mathrm{b}=0$. If $\lambda \neq(0)$ is a left ideal of $R$ and $p \neq(0)$ is a right ideal of $R$ where $R$ is prime then (0) $\neq \rho \lambda \subset \lambda \cap \rho$, that is, every non-zero left ideal intersects every non-zero right ideal non-trivially.

In what follows $R$ will be a prime Goldie ring. Let $F$ be a maximal left annihilator in $R$ and let $F_{r}=r(F)$. We shall maintain this notation. (If $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{r}}=(0)$ we know from Lemma 4.1 that R has a ring of left quottents which is a division ring. So we suppose henceforth that $F_{\mathrm{r}} \neq(0), F \neq(0)$. We follow the proof given by Procesi.

DEFINTION, For $u \in F_{r}$ let $\lambda_{u}: F_{r} \rightarrow F_{r}$ defined by $\lambda_{u}(x)=u x$.

Let $E=\left\{\lambda_{u} \mid u \in F_{r}\right\}$; clearly $E$ is a ring. However much more can be said.

THEOREM 4.2. E is an Ore domain, and so has a ring of left quotients K which is a division ring.

Proof, Since $R$ is prime and $F_{r} \neq(0), F_{r}^{2} \neq(0)$, that is, $E F_{r} \neq(0)$; thus $E \neq(0)$. Let $\lambda_{u} \neq 0, \lambda_{v} \neq 0 \in E$ and suppose that $\lambda_{u} \lambda_{v}=0$; then $u v E_{r}=(0)$. Since $\lambda_{v} \neq 0, v E_{r} \neq(0)$; but then since $\mathrm{R} \neq \ell\left(\mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{r}}\right) D F$ by the maximality of $F$ we conclude that $\ell\left(v F_{r}\right)=F$. Since $u<\ell\left(v F_{r}\right), u$ must be in $F$ so $\lambda_{u}=0$ contrary to assumption. We have shown E to be free of zero divisors.

Now claim that E has no infinite direct sum of left ideals for if $E \lambda_{u_{1}}+\ldots+E \lambda_{u_{k}}$ is a direct sum, since $\lambda_{u_{i}} \neq 0$, $F_{r} u_{i} \approx E \lambda_{u_{i}}$ from the definition of the $\lambda^{\prime} s$. Therefore $F_{r} u_{1}+\ldots+F_{r} u_{k}$ is a direct sum. We claim that $R_{1}+\ldots+R_{k}$ is a direct sum of left ideals of $R$ for if $r_{1} u_{1}+\ldots+r_{k} u_{k}=0$ with $r_{1} u_{1} \neq 0$, from the primeness of $R_{\text {, since }} F_{r} r_{1} u_{1} \neq(0)$ there is a $v \in F_{r}$ with $v r_{1} u_{1} \neq 0$. But then $v r_{1} u_{1}+\ldots+v r_{k} u_{k}=0$ with $\mathrm{vr}_{1} \mathrm{u}_{1} \neq 0$ contradicting the directness of $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{r}} \mathrm{u}_{1}+\ldots+\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{r}} \mathrm{u}_{\mathrm{k}}$. Since R has no infinite direct sum of left ideals we see that E also has no infinite direct sums of left ideals. By Lemma 4.2 E is an Ore domain. By Theorem 4.1, E has a ring of left quotients which is a division ring $K$.

We return to our study of prime rings, using the symbols $E, K, F_{r}$, eto. as they were used prior to and in Theorem 4.2. We first make the

DEFINITION, $\quad V=K \otimes_{E} F_{r}$.
Notice that $V$ is a left vector space over the division ring $K$. Since $F_{r}$ is a vight ideal of $R, V$ is a right $R$-module where $(k \otimes a) s=k \otimes(a s)$ for $a \in F_{r^{\prime}} s \in R$. Since $R$ is prime, $V$ is a faithful R -module. The elements of R induce K -linear transformations on $V$ so we can consider $R$ to be contained in $L(V)$.

Let $j: F_{r} \rightarrow K \otimes F_{r}=V$ defined by $j(u)=1 \otimes u$ for $u$ e $F_{r}$; $j$ is an injection of $F_{x}$ into $V$, Given $v \in V, v=\Sigma k_{i} \otimes u_{i}$ and since, by Sublemma $A, k_{i}=\lambda_{a}^{-1} \lambda_{b_{i}}$. $a, b_{i} \in F_{r}$, we get

$$
v=\lambda_{a}^{-1} \Sigma \lambda_{b_{i}} \otimes u_{i}=\lambda_{a}^{-1} \Sigma\left(\otimes \lambda_{b_{i}} u_{i}=\lambda_{a}^{-1}\left(1 \otimes \Sigma b_{i} u_{i}\right)\right),
$$

that is, every $v \in V$ has the form $v=\lambda_{a}^{-1} j(w)$ where $a, w \in F_{r}$.
We now establish some vital facts about $V$ which will allow us to get the first Goldie theorem.

(Tier-1 Error)

## THEOREM 4.3. V is finite-dimensional over K.

Proof. As we have just seen, $V=K_{j}\left(E_{I}\right)$ so we can find a basis of $V$ over $K$ of the form $j\left(u_{\alpha}\right), u_{\alpha} \& F_{r}$. If $j\left(u_{1}\right), \ldots, j\left(u_{n}\right), u_{i} \in F_{r^{\prime}}$ are linearly independent over $K$, we claim that $\mathrm{Ru}_{1}+\ldots+\mathrm{Ru}_{\mathrm{n}}$ is a direct sum. For suppose that $a_{1} u_{1}+\ldots+a_{n} u_{n}=0$; if $a_{1} u_{1} \neq 0$, say, then we can find an element $b, F_{T}$ such that $b a_{1} u_{1} \neq 0$, by the primeness of $R$. Thus $b a_{1} u_{1}+\ldots+b a_{n} u_{n}=0$; since $b a_{i}$, $F_{I}$ this says that $\lambda_{b a_{1}} j\left(u_{1}\right)+\ldots+\lambda_{b a_{n}} j\left(u_{n}\right)=0$ contradicting the linear independence of the $j(u)$ 's over $K$. Therefore the $a_{i} u_{i}=0$ and the sum is direct. Since R has no infinite direct sums of left-ideals we conclude that $V$ is finite-dimensional over $K$.

Since $V$ is $n$-dimensional, say, over $K$ and $R$ acts faithfully on $V$ as a ring of linear transformations we get that $R \subset K_{n}$, the ring of all $n \times n$ matrices over $K$. This is not at all obvious ab initio, and gives enough information to prove most of what we would usually want. However, we want to push deeper and to find out exactly how R sits in $\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{n}}$.

We prove an easy consequence of Theorem 4.3. the ring of K-linear transformations on V.

LEMMA 4.4. If $j\left(u_{1}\right), \ldots, j\left(u_{n}\right) \in V \quad\left(u_{i} \in F_{r}\right)$ is a basis of $V$ over $K$ then $\mathrm{Ru}_{1}+\ldots+\mathrm{Ru}_{\mathrm{n}}$ intersects every non-zero left-ideal of R in a non-zero fashion.

Proof. Let $\mathrm{I}=\mathrm{Ru}_{1}+\ldots+\mathrm{Ru}_{\mathrm{n}}$ and suppose that $\mathrm{J} \neq(0)$ is a left-ideal of $R$ such that $I \cap J=(0)$. Since $R$ is prime $J \cap F_{\mathrm{r}} \neq(0)$; let $\mathrm{u} \neq 0$ be in $J \cap F_{\mathrm{r}^{*}}$

We claim that $j\left(u_{1}\right), \ldots, j\left(u_{n}\right), j(u)$ are linearly independent over $K$; this has already been proved in the course of proving Theorem 4.3. Since this contradicts the fact that $j\left(u_{1}\right), \ldots, j\left(u_{n}\right)$ is a basis of $V$ we get the lemma.

DEFINITION. We say that a left-ideal I of R meets all left-ideals of R if, given $\mathrm{J} \neq(0)$ a left-ideal of R then $I \cap J \neq(0)$.

Sublemma B. Let V be a vector space of dimension ${ }^{\mathrm{n}}$ over a division ring $K$ and suppose that $T_{1}, \ldots, T_{k}$ are linear transformations on V of rank 1 such that:

1. $T_{i}^{2} \neq 0$,
2. $\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{i}} \mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{j}}=0$ for $\mathrm{j}<\mathrm{i}$.

Then $S=T_{1}+\ldots+T_{k}$ is of rank $k$ and the null space of $S$ is the intersection of the null spaces of the $T_{i}$ 's.

Proof. Since $T_{i}$ is of rank 1, $V T_{i}=K v_{i}$ where $v_{i} T_{i} \neq(0)$ (since $T_{i}^{2} \neq 0$ ) and $v_{i} T_{j}=0$ for $j<i$ (since $T_{i} T_{j}=0$ for $j<i)$. From this it is trivial that $v_{1}+\ldots, v_{k}$ are linearly independent over $K$ and that $V S$ is the subspace spanned by the $v_{i}$ 's. If $\mathrm{v} S=0$ then $\mathrm{vST}_{\mathrm{k}}=0$ giving $\mathrm{vT}_{\mathrm{k}}=0$. Also $\mathrm{vST}_{\mathrm{k}-1}=0$, and since $\mathrm{v} \mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{k}}=0$ this gives $\mathrm{v} \mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{k}-1}=0$. Continuing we get $\mathrm{v} \mathrm{T}_{1}=0$ for $i=1,2, \ldots, k$.

Let $\varphi$ be the isomorphism imbedding $R$ into $Q$, the ring of K-linear transformations on $V$. It is clear that $a \in R$ is regular if and only if $\varphi(\mathrm{a})$ is invertible in $Q$.

Basic to our needs is the
LEMMA 4.5. If I meets all left ideals of R then I contains a regular element.

Proof. If $x \in R$ is such that $\varphi(x) \in Q$ has rank 1 then $\mathrm{axb}=0$ forces $\mathrm{ax}=0$ or $\mathrm{xb}=0$. We will find n elements $u_{1}, \ldots, u_{n}$ in $I$ such that:

1. $\varphi\left(u_{i}\right)$ is of rank 1 for each i
2. $u_{i}^{2} \neq 0$
3. $u_{i} u_{j}=0$ for $j<i$,

We proceed by induction, supposing that we have $u_{1}, \ldots, u_{k}$, $k<n$ with the desired properties. There is $a \mathrm{u} \neq 0$ in $F_{\mathrm{r}}$ so that $j(u) \varphi\left(u_{i}\right)=0$ for $i=1,2, \ldots, k$ since there is a $j(u)$ annihilated by $\varphi\left(u_{1}\right)+\ldots+\varphi\left(u_{k}\right)$ (which is of rank less than $n$, so by the Sublemma $B$ the result holds). Since $u$ \& $F_{r^{\prime}} \varphi(u)$ has rank 1. Also $\mathrm{Ru} \neq(0)$ thus must intersect I; by the primeness of R , $u(R u \cap 1) \neq(0)$. But then there is $a=r u \neq 0$ in $R_{u} \cap 1$ such that uru $\neq 0$. If $a^{2}=0$ then ruru $=0$, whence, since $\varphi(u)$ is of rank 1, $\mathrm{ru}=0$ or $\mathrm{uru}=0$; at any rate uru would result contrary to choice. Let $u_{k+1}=a$; then $a^{2} \neq 0$ so $u_{k+1}^{2} \neq 0, \varphi\left(u_{k+1}\right)$ is of rank 1, $u_{k+1}$ is in 1 and since $j(u)$ is annihilated by all the $\varphi\left(u_{i}\right), i=1,2, \ldots k$ and $a=r u, j\left(u_{k+1}\right) \varphi\left(u_{j}\right)=0$ for all $j \leq k$. We have established the existence of $u_{1}, \ldots, u_{n}$ with the requishe properties.

But then $\varphi\left(u_{1}+\ldots+u_{n}\right)$ is of rank $n$ so is invertible in $Q$. Therefore $u_{1}+\ldots+u_{n}$ is regular and is in $I$. This proves the lemma.

We pass to the first Goldie theorem.
THEOREM 4.4. The ring $Q$ of all K-linear transformations on $V$ is a left quotient ring for $R$. In other words, a prime Goldie ring has a left quotient ring which is a simple ring with descending chain conditions on left ideals.

Proof. We already know that a regular element in $R$ picks up an inverse in $Q$. It remains to show that if $m \in Q$ then $\mathrm{m}=\varphi(\mathrm{a})^{-1} \varphi(\mathrm{~b})$ where $\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b} \in \mathrm{R}$ and a is regular.

Let $T=\{x \in R \mid \psi(x) m<\varphi(R)\}$; $T$ is clearly a left ideal of $R$. We claim that $T$ meets all left ideals of $R$. Let $I \neq(0)$ be a left ideal of R and let $\mathrm{u} \neq 0 \in I \cap \mathrm{~F}_{r}$. The clement $J(u) m \in V$ so we know that $j(u) m=\lambda_{v}^{-1} j(w)$ for suitable $v, w \in F_{r}$. Thus $\lambda_{v} j(u) m=j(w)$ giving us $j(v u) m=j(w)$. But then for $x \in F_{r}, j(x) \varphi(\mathrm{vu}) m=\lambda_{x} j(\mathrm{vu}) m=\lambda_{x} j(w)=j(x) \varphi(w)$, that is $\varphi(\mathrm{vu}) \mathrm{m}=\varphi(w)$. Therefore $\mathrm{vu} \epsilon \mathrm{T}$ and since $\lambda_{\mathrm{v}} \neq 0$, vu $\neq 0$, The element vu is in $\mathrm{T} \cap \mathrm{L}$. By Lemma 4.5, T has a regular element $a$, hence $\varphi(a) m \in \varphi(R)$ that is, $\varphi(a) m=\varphi(b)$ therefore $\mathrm{m}=\varphi(\mathrm{a})^{-1} \varphi(\mathrm{~b})$. This proves the theorem.

We prove a converse to Goldie's theorem. However since we shall need a wider result for later use we now prove this more general result.

DEFINITION, $R$ is said to be left-order in $S$ if $S$ is a ring of left quotients for R .

THEOREM 4.5. Let R be a left-order in S where S is a semi-simple ring with descending chain condition. Then R is a Goldie ring having no non-zero nilpotent ideals. Moreover, if $S$ is simple then $R$ is prime.

Proof. We first show that $R$ is a Goldie ring, Since $S$ is semi-simple with descending chain conditions then, as is wellknown, $S$ must satisfy the ascending chain condition on leftideals, hence in particular the ascending chain conditions on left annihilators. Since this last condition is inherited by subrings we see that $R$ satisfies condition 1 of the Goldie conditions.

Let $\lambda_{1}, \ldots, \lambda_{k}$ form a direct sum of left ideals of $R$; we claim that $S \lambda_{1}, \ldots, S \lambda_{k}$ form a direct sum of left ideals in $S$. If $s_{1} \alpha_{i}+\ldots+s_{k} \alpha_{k}=0$ where $s_{i} \in S, \alpha_{i} \in \lambda_{i}$ then, as in Sublemma A , we can write each $\mathrm{s}_{\mathrm{i}}=\mathrm{s}^{-1} \mathrm{~b}_{\mathrm{i}}, \mathrm{s}, \mathrm{b}_{\mathrm{i}} \in \mathrm{R}$, s regular, thus $\mathrm{b}_{1} \alpha_{1}+\ldots+\mathrm{b}_{k} \alpha_{k}=0$. By the directness of the sum in $R$ we get each $b_{i} \alpha_{i}=0$ hence $s_{i} \alpha_{i}=0$. Therefore $R$ is a Goldie ring.

Let $\mathrm{N} \neq(0)$ be a nilpotent ideal in R with $\mathrm{N}^{\mathrm{m}}=(0)$, $\mathrm{N}^{\mathrm{m}-1} \neq(0)$. The ideal SNS of $S$ is not $(0)$; since $S$ is a semisimple ring with minimum condition $\mathrm{SNS}=\mathrm{eS}$ where e is a central idempotent contained in SNS. Now $e=\Sigma a_{i} u_{i} b_{i}$, $a_{i}, b_{i} \in S$ and $u_{i} \in N$; we can write each $a_{i}$ as $a_{i}=a^{-1} c_{i}, a \in R$ is regular and $c_{i} \in R$. Thus $e=a^{-1} \Sigma c_{i} u_{i} b_{i}$ whence $e=a^{-1} \Sigma w_{i} b_{i}, w_{i} \in N$. Since $e$ is central, $a e=e a$ therefore $N^{m-1}{ }_{a e}=N^{m-1} \Sigma w_{i} b_{i}=(0)$; because a is regular this forces $N^{m-1} e=(0)$. Therefore $\left(N^{m-1} S\right)^{2}=N^{m-1} S N^{m-1} S \subset N^{m-1} e S=(0)$. Since $S$ is semi-simple this is impossible unless $N^{m-1}=(0)$. This contradiction proves that $\mathrm{N}=(0)$.

Finally, suppose that $S$ is simple. Let $A \neq(0)$ be an ideal of R ; thus $\mathrm{SAS}=\mathrm{S}$. Since S has a unit element $1,1=\Sigma \mathrm{b}_{i} \mathrm{a}_{i} \mathrm{C}_{i}$, $b_{i}, c_{i} \in S, a_{i} \in A$. As before $b_{i}=b^{-1} d_{i}, b$ regular, $d_{i} \in A_{,}$ thus $b=\Sigma d_{i} a_{i} c_{i} \in A S$. If for some ideal $B$ of $R, B A=(0)$ then $B A S=(0)$ whence $B b=(0)$. The regularity of $b$ forces $B=(0)$, consequently $R$ is a prime ring.

We have all that we need to prove a theorem due to Faith and Utumi. The very simple, elegant proof we give is due to Claudio Procesi.

THEOREM 4.6. Let R be a left-order in $\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{n}}$, the ring of $n \times n$ matrices over the division ring $D$. Then we can find an Ore domain $\Lambda$ whose ring of quatients is isomorphic to $D$ and such that R contains a subring isomorphic to $\Lambda_{\mathrm{n}}$.

Proof. By the previous theorem $R$ is a prime Goldie ring so has a ring of left quotients isomorphic to $K_{m}$; by Wedderburn's Theorem $m=n$ and $K \approx D$. We use our previous notation $F_{r^{\prime}} E$, $K_{1} V=K Q_{E} F_{r}$. Since $K, F_{r} \subset K_{n}$ we can identify $V$ with $K F_{r}$. Let $u_{1}, \ldots, u_{n} \in F_{r}$ form a basis of $V$ over $K$; by Lemma 4.4, $\mathrm{Ru}_{1}+\ldots+\mathrm{Ru}_{\mathrm{n}}$ meets all left ideals of R hence, by Lemma 4.5 , it contains a regular element $c, c=\sum r_{i} u_{i}$. The elements $u_{i} c^{-1}$ still form a basis of $V$; let $\Lambda=\left\{\lambda \in E \mid \lambda u_{i} c^{-1} \in F_{r}\right.$ all $\left.i\right\}$. Since $v \in V$ can be written as $\lambda_{a}^{-1} w, a, w \in F_{r}$ we know that $\Lambda \neq(0)$. Now $\Lambda$ is a left ideal of $E$ so by Lemma 4.3 its ring of quotients is also K . Now Q contains the ring $S=\left\{q \in Q \mid u_{i} c^{-1} q, \sum_{j} \Lambda u_{j} c^{-1}\right\}$ for all $i$ (that is the matrices in the basis $u_{i} c^{-1}$ having entries in $\Lambda$ ). $S$ is isomorphic to $\Lambda_{n}$.

We claim that $S \subset R$ since if $u_{i} c^{-1} q<\Sigma \Lambda u_{j} c^{-1} \subset F_{r}$ (by definition of $\Lambda$ ) then $q=c c^{-1} q=\left(\Sigma r_{i} u_{i}\right) c^{-1} q=\Sigma r_{i}\left(u_{i} c^{-1} q^{\prime}\right) \in R F_{\mathrm{r}} \subset \mathrm{R}$. Thus $\mathrm{R} \supset \mathrm{S}$ and $\mathrm{S} \approx \Lambda_{\mathrm{n}}$.

We should like to extend Goldie's theorem from prime rings to a much wider context. We so so soon, but first a definition.

DEFINITION, A ring $R$ is said to be semi-prime if it has no non-zero nilpotent ideals.

Until further notice $R$ will denote a semi-prime Goldie ring.

Let $m$ be the set of all two-sided ideals of R which are the left annihilators of non-zero two-sided ideals of R , If R consisted merely of ( 0 ) then R would be a prime ring so we would know its structure from Theorem 4.4. So we may assume that (0) is not maximal in $m$.

As a set of left annihilators $T_{\text {has maximal elements; we }}$ shall denote these by $A$ or by $A$ with a subscript. Our first task is to study these. But first we note: if A is maximal in $m$ and $r(A)$ is the right-annihilator of $A$ then $A$ is precisely the left annihilator of any non-zero ideal of $R$ which is contained in $r(A)$. In particular, if $x r(A)=0$ then $x \in A$.

LEMMA 4,6. If A is maximal in $M$ then A is a prime ideal of R.

Proof. Suppose that $\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{V}$ are ideals of R such that UV CA; thus $\mathrm{UVr}(\mathrm{A})=(0)$. If $\mathrm{V} \not \subset \mathrm{A}$ then $\operatorname{Vr}(\mathrm{A}) \neq(0)$ is an ideal of R which is contained in $r(A)$. Since $U$ annihilates $V r(A), U$ must lie in $A$, Therefore $A$ is a prime ideal of $R$.

COROLLARY. If $\mathrm{A}_{1} \neq \mathrm{A}_{2}$ are maximal in $m$ then $r\left(A_{1}\right) \subset A_{2}$.

LEMMA 4.7. If $\mathrm{A}_{1}, \ldots, \mathrm{~A}_{\mathrm{n}}$ are maximal in $m$ then $r\left(A_{1}\right)+\ldots+r\left(A_{n}\right)$ is a direct sum. In particular, $m$ has only a finite number of maximal elements.

Proof. Gonsider $\mathrm{B}=r\left(A_{i}\right) \cap\left(r\left(A_{1}\right)+\ldots+r\left(\hat{A}_{i}\right)+\ldots+r\left(A_{n}\right)\right)$; then $B$ annihilates $A_{i}+\prod_{j \neq i} A_{j}$ from the right. Since the $A^{t_{s}}$ are prime ideals, $\prod_{j \neq i} \mathrm{~A}_{j} \notin \mathrm{~A}_{i}$ so $\mathrm{A}_{i}+\prod_{j \neq i} \mathrm{~A}_{j}$ annihilates B and is larger than $\mathrm{A}_{i}$. Therefore by the choice of the $\mathrm{A}^{\prime} \mathrm{s}$, $B=(0)$. Thus the sum $r\left(A_{1}\right)+\ldots+r\left(A_{n}\right)$ is direct,

Since $R$ is a Goldie ring it has no infinite direct sums of ideals, hence there are only a finite number of distinct $r\left(A_{i}\right)$ and so a finite number of maximal elements in $M$.

Let $A_{1}, \ldots, A_{n}$ be a complete list of the maximal elements in $M$. We want to show that $A_{1} \cap A_{2} \cap . . . \cap A_{n}=(0)$. Before doing this we first make a remark.

Remark: In any semi-prime ring, if $A, B$ are 2 -sided ideals such that $A B=(0)$ then $B A=(0)$.

To see this merely note that $(B A)^{2}=B A B A=(0)$ since $\mathrm{AB}=(0)$. Thus $\mathrm{BA}=(0)$.

LEMMA 4.8. $\mathrm{A}_{1} \cap \mathrm{~A}_{2} \cap \ldots \cap \mathrm{~A}_{\mathrm{n}}=(0)$.
Proof. Let $W=A_{1} \cap \ldots . \cap A_{n}$. Now $W\left(r\left(A_{1}\right)+\ldots+r\left(A_{n}\right)\right) =(0)$ consequently by the above remark $\left(r\left(A_{1}\right)+\ldots+r\left(A_{n}\right)\right) W=(0)$. If $W \neq(0)$ then $r\left(A_{1}\right)+\ldots+r\left(A_{n}\right)$ would annihilate a non-zero ideal of R from the left hence would be contained in an element of $m$ and so in a maximal element of $m$. In short $r\left(A_{1}\right)+\ldots+r\left(A_{n}\right) \subset A_{j}$ for some $j$; since $r\left(A_{j}\right) \subset r\left(A_{1}\right)+\ldots+r\left(A_{n}\right) \subset A_{j}$ we get $r\left(A_{j}\right)^{2} \subset A_{j} r\left(A_{j}\right)=(0)$, forcing $r\left(A_{j}\right)=0$, a contradiction. Therefore $W=(0)$.

We now have an imbedding of $R$ into $R / A_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus R / A_{n}$ each of whose constituents, by Lemma 4.6, is a prime ring. We want -- in fact need -- that these are themselves Goldie rings. It is towards this goal we proceed, but first we establish a lemma which holds for any ring.

LEMMA 4.9. Let R be any ring and suppose that $\mathrm{A}=\ell(\mathrm{S})$ happens to be a two-sided ideal of R . Then if $\bar{\lambda} \subset \overline{\mathrm{R}}=\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{A}$ is a left annihilator in $\overline{\mathrm{R}}$, the left ideal $\lambda=\{\mathrm{x} \in \mathrm{R} \mid \overline{\mathrm{x}} \in \bar{\lambda}\}$ is also a left annihilator in R.

Proof. Suppose that $\bar{\lambda}=I(T)$; let $T$ be the inverse image of $\bar{T}$ in $R$. Since $\bar{T} \neq(0)$ (otherwise $\bar{\lambda}=\bar{R}, \lambda=R$ ), $T \notin A$ so $\mathrm{TS} \neq(0)$. We claim that $\lambda=\ell(\mathrm{TS})$. Clearly $\lambda \mathrm{TS}=\langle 0\rangle$ since $\bar{\lambda} \bar{T}=(0)$, hence $\lambda T \subset A$, Also, if $x T S=(0)$ then $x T \subset A$ so that $\bar{x} \bar{T}=(0)$, putting $\bar{x} \epsilon \bar{\lambda}$. This proves the lemma.

We return to our semi-prime Goldie ring.
LEMMA 4.10. If A is maximal in $m$ then $\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{A}$ is a Goldie ring.

Proof. By Lemma 4.7 there is a correspondence between left annihilators in R/A (since $A=\ell(r(A)))$ and certain left annihilators in R . Thus the ascending chain condition on left annihilators in $R$ implies that in $R / A$.

What about direct sums of left ideals? Let $\bar{\lambda}_{1}, \ldots, \bar{\lambda}_{k}$ in $R / A$ form a direct sum of left ideals, and let $\lambda_{1}, \ldots, \lambda_{k}$ be their inverse images in $R$. Hence $\lambda_{i} \cap\left(\lambda_{1}+\ldots+\hat{\lambda}_{i}+\ldots+\lambda_{k}\right) \subset A$, Since $\lambda_{i} \notin A, \lambda_{i} r(A) \neq(0)$ therefore by the remark made earlier $r(A) \lambda_{i} \neq(0)$. Let $\mu_{i}=r(A) \lambda_{i}$; these are non-zero left ideals of $R_{\text {, }}$ and are contained in the corresponding $\lambda_{i} \cap r(A)$. However, $\tau_{i}=\mu_{i} \cap\left(\mu_{i}+\ldots+\hat{\mu}_{i}+\ldots+\mu_{k}\right) \subset \lambda_{i} \cap\left(\lambda_{i}+\ldots+\hat{\lambda}_{i}+\ldots+\lambda_{k}\right) \subset A ; \tau_{i}$ also being in $r(A)$ we get it in $A \cap r(A)=(0)$. The $\mu_{i}$ thus give us a direct sum of non-zero left ideals in R of the same length as that given us by the $\bar{\lambda}_{i}$ 's. Since there are no infinite direct sums of non-zero left ideals in $R$ we get the same conclusion in R/A, that is, R/A is a Goldie ring.

We have all the requisite pieces to prove the second Goldie theorem.

THEOREM 4.7. Let R be a semi-prime Goldie ring; then R has a ring of left quotients which is a semi-simple ring with descending chain conditions on left ideals.

Proof. We already know that we have a finite set of prime ideals $A_{1}, \ldots, A_{n}$ such that each $R_{i}=R / A_{i}$ is a prime Coldie ring and such that $R$ is isomorphically imbeddable in $\mathrm{S}=\mathrm{R}_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus \mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{n}}$. Moreover, by Theorem 4.4 each $\mathrm{R}_{1}$ has a ring of left quotients $Q\left(R_{i}\right)$ which is a total matrix ring over a division ring $D_{i}$.

We claim that $T=Q\left(R_{1}\right) \oplus \ldots \oplus Q\left(R_{n}\right)$ is a ring of left quotients of R .

Now for any $\mathrm{J} \neq 1$, by the corollary to Lemma 4.6, $r\left(A_{i}\right) C A_{j}$ so tts image in $S$ lies in $r\left(A_{i}\right)$, is not $(0)$ and is a two-sided ideal of $R_{i}$. Since $R_{i}$ is prime, the irnage of $r\left(A_{i}\right)$ in $R_{i}$ must meet every left ideal of $R_{i}$, thus by Lemma 4.5 it contains a regular element $\bar{c}_{i}$. Let $c_{i} \in r\left(A_{i}\right)$ be an inverse image of $\bar{c}_{i}$; the element $\left(c_{1}, c_{2}, \ldots, c_{n}\right)$ is the image in $S$ of $c_{1}+c_{2}+\ldots+c_{n}$, and so, quite easily, is regular. But then $\left(c_{1}, \ldots . c_{n}\right)$ is invertible in $T$.

Let $T^{\mathrm{t}}=\left\{\mathrm{a}^{-1} \mathrm{~b} \mid \mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b} \in \mathrm{R}\right.$, a invertible in $\left.T\right\}$. From the above $e_{i}=(0,0, \ldots, 1,0, \ldots, 0)=\left\langle c_{1}, \ldots, c_{n}\right)^{-1}\left(0, \ldots, c_{i}, 0, \ldots, 0\right)$ is in $T^{\prime}$ (we have identified $c_{1}+\ldots+c_{n}$ with its image in $S$ ) consequently $T^{\prime} \supset R_{i}$ for each $i$. But then $T^{t}=T$. We have proved that $T$ is a ring of left quotients for $R$. This proves the second Goldie theorem.

We now go to the third Goldie theorem wherein we shall investigate the nature of rings in which every left ideal is principal. The proof we give here is due to Procesi.

LEMMA 4.11. Let $R$ be aprime ring in which every left ideal is principal. Then R is isomorphic to a total matrix ring over an Ore domain.

Proof. We use the notation used earlier in proving Theorem 4. 4. If $1 \otimes u_{1}, \ldots, 1 \otimes u_{n}$ is a basis of $V$ over $K_{,} u_{i} \in F_{r}$, we have seen in Lemma 4.4 that $T=\mathrm{Ru}_{1}+\ldots+\mathrm{Ru}_{n}$ meets all left ideals of $R$, thus by Lemma 4.5 it contains a regular element $d$. Since every left ideal is principal $T=R c$ and so $d=r c$. The regularity of $d$ implies that of $c$, hence $c$ is regular. Moreover, $\mathrm{c}=\Sigma \mathrm{b}_{\mathrm{i}} \mathrm{u}_{\mathrm{i}}$ being in $\mathrm{T}=\mathrm{R} \mathrm{u}_{1}+\ldots+\mathrm{Ru} \mathrm{n}_{\mathrm{n}}$. Since each $u_{i} \varepsilon T, u_{i}=r_{i} c$ therefore $c=\sum b_{i} r_{i} c=\left(\sum b_{i} r_{i}\right) c$; since $c$ is regular we deduce from this that $\sum b_{i} x_{i}=1$. Since $u_{i} \in F_{r}$, $F_{u_{i}}=(0)$ so we get $F_{F_{i}} c=(0)$ therefore $F_{r_{i}}=(0)$, that is, each $r_{i} \in F_{r}$. We claim that $1 \otimes r_{1}, \ldots, 1 \otimes r_{n}$ form a basis of $V$ over $K$, since the $i \otimes\left(r_{i} c\right)$ do and $c$ is regular. If $u \in F_{r}$ then $u=u i=\Sigma u b_{i} r_{i}, u b_{i}, F_{r}$ so that $u=\Sigma \lambda_{u b_{i}} j\left(r_{i}\right)$ and hence $F_{r}$ is a free Edmodule of rank $n$ with generators $j\left(r_{1}\right), \ldots, j\left(r_{n}\right)$,

Every element in R induces an E-endomorphism on $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{r}}$. Given an $E$-endomorphism of $F_{r}$, by Goldie's theorem (Theorem 4. 4) it is inducible by right multiplication by $a^{-1} b, a, b \in R$ and a regular. Since $\mathrm{a}^{-1} \mathrm{~b}$ is an $E$-endomorphism of $F_{r^{\prime}}$, each $\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{i}} \mathrm{a}^{-1} \mathrm{~b}$ is in $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{r}}$. But $\mathrm{a}^{-1} \mathrm{~b}=1 \mathrm{a}^{-1} \mathrm{~b}=\left(\sum \mathrm{b}_{\mathrm{i}} \mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{i}}\right) \mathrm{a}^{-1} \mathrm{~b}=\sum \mathrm{b}_{\mathrm{i}}\left(\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{i}} \mathrm{a}^{-1} \mathrm{~b}\right)$ is an element of $R F_{r} \subset R$ since each $r_{i} a^{-1} b \in F_{r}$. Therefore $\mathrm{a}^{-1} \mathrm{~b} \in \mathrm{R}$ and the result is proved.

One could ask if the Ore domain in the statement of the lemma also has every left ideal principal. This is false as is shown by an example due to Swan.

LEMMA 4.12. A semi-prime ring whose leit ideals are principal is a direct sum of a finite number of prime rings.

Proof. In the proof of Theorem 4.7 it is enough to show that $A_{i}+A_{j}=R$ for $i \neq j$ for then the result holds by the Chinese Remainder Theorem,

Now $A_{i}=R a, A_{j}=R b$ so $A_{i}+A_{j}=R t$. The element $t$ is regular for if $t x=0$ then $A_{i} x=(0)$ and $A_{j} x=(0)$ so $x \in r\left(A_{i}\right) \cap r\left(A_{j}\right)=(0)$.

However, $\mathrm{t}=\mathrm{xa}+\mathrm{yb}, \mathrm{a}=\mathrm{ut}, \mathrm{b}=\mathrm{vt} s o(\mathrm{xu}+\mathrm{yv}) \mathrm{t}=\mathrm{t}$ whence $x u+y v=1$. If $I=\ell(R a)$ then since $I A_{i}=(0), A_{i} I=(0)$ by the semi-primeness of $R_{\text {, and so }} I=r\left(R_{1}\right)$. But $I R a=(0)$ forces IRut $2(0)$ hence $I R u=(0)$, so $R u I=(0)$. Since $\operatorname{Ru}\left(\ell\left(r\left(A_{i}\right)\right)=A_{i}\right.$ we get $R u$ CRa. Similarly Rv CRb, so $1 \in R u+R v \subset R a+R b=A_{i}+A_{j}$. Thus $A_{i}+A_{j}=R$,

DEFINITION. R is a primary ring if $\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{N}$ is a prime ring where $N$ is the maximal nilpotent ideal of $R$.

LEMMA 4.13. A principal left ideal ring is the direct sum of primary rings.

Proof. Let N be the maximal nilpotent ideal of R . By Lemma 4.10, $R / N=\bar{R}_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus \bar{R}_{k}$ where the $\bar{R}_{i}$ are prime rings. We want to show that $R=R_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus R_{k}$ where $R_{i}$ maps onto $\overline{\mathrm{R}}_{i}$ modulo N .

Now $\overline{\mathrm{R}}=\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{N}=\overline{\mathrm{A}} \oplus \overline{\mathrm{B}}$ where $\overline{\mathrm{A}}$ is semi-prime and $\overline{\mathrm{B}}$ is prime $\left(\bar{B}=R_{k}, \bar{A}=\bar{R}_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus \bar{R}_{k-1}\right)$. Now $r(\bar{A})=\ell(\bar{A})=\bar{B}$ and $r(\bar{B})=\ell(\bar{B})=\bar{A}$ by the semi-primeness of $\bar{A}$ and $\bar{B}$. Let $A, B$ be the inverse images of $\bar{A}$ and $\bar{B}$ respectively. Thus $\mathrm{A}=\mathrm{Ra}, \mathrm{B}=\mathrm{Rb}$ and $\mathrm{NC}(\mathrm{Ra} \cap \mathrm{Rb})$. If $\mathrm{xa} \in \mathrm{N}$ then $\overrightarrow{\mathrm{x}} \overrightarrow{\mathrm{a}}=0$; but $\bar{R} \bar{a}=\bar{A}$ so $\bar{a}$ is regular in $\bar{A}$ which forces $\bar{x} \in \bar{B}$, hence $x a \in N$ implies that $\mathrm{x} \in \mathrm{B}$. Since N CRa we get N C Rba (for $\mathrm{n} \in \mathrm{N}$, $n=x a$ forces $x \in B=R b$ ) and $b a \in N$ since $\bar{b} \bar{a}=0$. Thus $\mathrm{N}=\mathrm{Rbr}$.

Also $N=R a b \subset R a R b \subset N$ so we find that $A B=B A=A \cap B =N$. We claim that for any integer $m>0, R=A^{m}+B^{m}$; we go by induction. Now $R=R^{2}=\left(A^{m}+B^{m}\right)(A+B)=A^{m+1}+B^{m+1}+U$ where $U \subset N$. Since $N C A$ and $N C B$ and $U C N=N R =N\left(A^{m}+B^{m}\right) C A^{m+1}+B^{m+1}$ we get $R=A^{m+1}+B^{m+1}$.

Since we have the ascending chain condition on left ideals in $R$ there is an integer $k$ such that $t\left(\mathrm{a}^{k}\right)=t\left(\mathrm{a}^{k+1}\right)=\ldots$. But then $\mathrm{Ra}^{\mathrm{k}} \cap \ell\left(\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{k}}\right)=(0)$ for if $x \mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{k}}, \ell\left(\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{k}}\right)$ then $0=x \mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{k}} \mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{k}}=x \mathrm{a}^{2 \mathrm{k}}$ hence $x \in I\left(a^{2 k}\right)=I\left(a^{k}\right)$ giving us $x a^{k}=0$.

Suppose that $N^{t}=(0)$; pick $m>\max (k, t)$. Then $B^{m} A^{m}=(B A)^{m} =(0)$ (since $B A=A B=N$ ); also $B^{m} \subset I\left(a^{m}\right)$ so $B^{m} \cap R^{m}=(0)$. Since $\mathrm{A}=\mathrm{Ra}$ is a 2 -sided ideal, aR CRa hence $A^{m}=(R a)^{m} \subset R a^{m} \subset A^{m}$, thus $A^{m}=R a^{m}$ and $B^{m} \cap A^{m}=(0)$.

Since $A^{m} \oplus B^{m}=R$ and since $B^{m}$ maps on $\bar{B}$ and $\bar{A}^{m}$ maps on $\overleftarrow{A}$ modulo $N$ we get the result.

We now prove the third Goldie theorem.
THEOREM 4.8. Let $R$ be a principal left ideal ring which atisfies the ascending chain condition on right ideals. Then R is the direct sum of a semi-prime ring in which all left ideals are principal and a ring with descending chain condition on left and right ideals.

Proof. In light of the preceding lemmas, in order to prove the theorem it is enough to show that a primary but not prime ring of this type satisfies the descending chain conditions.

Let R be a primary ring and let $(0) \neq \mathrm{N}=\mathrm{Rn}$ be its maximal nilpotent ideal. We first want to show that $\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{N}$ satisfies the descending chain conditions, or in other words (by Goldie's theorem) that $\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{N}$ is its own ring of left quotients.

We claim that we can reduce to the case in which $\mathrm{N}^{2}=(0)$ for $\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{N} \approx\left(\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{N}^{2}\right) /\left(\mathrm{N} / \mathrm{N}^{2}\right)$ and $\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{N}^{2}$ is primary, not prime (if $\mathrm{N}^{2} \neq(0)$ ), in which $\mathrm{N}^{2}=(0)$. So we suppose that $\mathrm{N}^{2}=(0)$.

1. $r(N)=N$. For if $r(N) \neq N$ being an ideal in $R$ which contains $N, r(N) / N \neq(0)$ is an ideal of $R / N$ which is prime so meets all left ideals of $\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{N}$ hence has a regular element. So there would be an element $c \in R$ with $N_{c}=(0)$ where $\bar{c}$ is regular in $\overline{\mathrm{R}}=\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{N}$. Since $\overline{\mathrm{c}}$ is not nilpotent neither is c and there is an integer $k$ such that $l\left(c^{k}\right) \cap \operatorname{Rc}^{k}=(0)$. Thus since $N C t\left(c^{k}\right), N \cap R c^{k}=(0)$. Now $N+R c^{k}$ is a left ideal so $\mathrm{N}+\mathrm{Rc}^{\mathrm{k}}=\mathrm{Rd}, \mathrm{c}^{\mathrm{k}}=\mathrm{sd}, \mathrm{n}=\mathrm{rd}, \mathrm{an}+\mathrm{bc} \mathrm{c}^{\mathrm{k}}=\mathrm{d}$. Therefore in $\overline{\mathrm{R}}$, $\bar{d}$ is regular (since $\bar{c}^{k}$ is) and since $\bar{r} \bar{d}=0, \bar{r}=0$ so $x \in N$. Thus $r a n+r b c^{k}=r d=n$; since $r a n \in N^{2}=(0)$ and $r b \in N$, $r b c^{k}=0$ we get $n=0$, a contradiction.
2. If $\bar{c}$ is regular in $\bar{R}$ then $N c=N=N c{ }^{k}$. For if $c \rightarrow \bar{c}$ then $R_{n}+R_{c}=R d$ where $\bar{d}$ is regular in $\bar{R}$ (since $\bar{c}=\overline{s d}$ ). Now $n=t d$ and $d=p n+q c$; since $\overline{t d}=0, t \in N$ since $\bar{d}$ is regular. So $n=t d=t p n+t q c=t q c$ since $t p n \in N^{2}=\{0\}$. Thus $\mathrm{N} \subset \mathrm{Nc} \subset \mathrm{N}$ proving $\mathrm{Nc}=\mathrm{N}$, and so also $\mathrm{Nc}^{\mathrm{k}}=\mathrm{N}$.
3. If $\bar{c}$ is regular in $\bar{R}$ then $c$ is left-regular in $R$, that is, $t(c)=(0)$. For we have $N=N c^{k}$; pick $k$ so that $\operatorname{Rc}^{\mathrm{k}} \cap \ell\left(\mathrm{c}^{\mathrm{k}}\right)=(0)$. Since $\overline{\mathrm{c}}$ is regular, $\ell\left(\mathrm{c}^{\mathrm{k}}\right) \subset \mathrm{N}=\mathrm{Nc}{ }^{\mathrm{k}}$, hence $l\left(c^{k}\right)=(0)$. Thus $c^{k}$, and so, c is left-regular.

We finish the proof of the theorem Let $\overline{\mathrm{C}}$ be regular in $\overline{\mathrm{R}}$ and pick $m \in N$ such that $R m=N$ and $m R$ is maximal over all such m . Now since $\mathrm{Nc}=\mathrm{N}, \mathrm{m}=\mathrm{m}_{1} \mathrm{c}$ with $\mathrm{m}_{1} \in \mathrm{~N}$. Also $\mathrm{Nc}=\mathrm{N}=\mathrm{Rm}=\mathrm{Rm} \mathrm{m}_{1} \mathrm{c}$ hence, since c is left-regular, $\mathrm{N}=\mathrm{Rm} \mathrm{m}_{1}$ Since $m=m_{1} c<m_{1} R$, by the maximality of $m R$ we must have $m R=m_{1} R$, that is, $m_{1}=m x=m_{1} c x$, hence $m_{1}(1-c x)=0$.

Consequently $1-c x$ \& $r(N)=N$ whence in $\bar{R}=R / N, 1=\overline{c x}$ so $\bar{c}$ is invertible in $\overline{\mathrm{R}}$. This proves $\overline{\mathrm{R}} \approx \mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{n}}$ by Goldie's theorem so certainly satisfies the descending chain conditions.

We now must merely show that $R$ also satisfies the descending chain conditions. Now $N^{k}=(0)$ and the $R / N$ modules $N / N^{2}, N^{2} / N^{3} \ldots, N^{i} / N^{i+1}$ are finitely generated. The nature of $\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{N}$ from above then tells us N has a finite composition series, which together with the fact that $R / N$ also has one tells us $R$ satisfies the descending chain condition. A recent example due to Jategaonkar shows that one can not drop the assumption that $R$ be right Noetherian.

We close this chapter with a fast simple and self-contained proof of Goldie's theorem for semi-prime rings recently worked out jointly by Procesi and Small.

For the first lemma we merely need that R be semi-prime with ascending chain condition on left annihilators and not the whole assumption that $R$ be Goldie.

LEMMA 4.14. If $A \supset B$ are left ideals of $R$ and $r(A) \subset r(B)$ but $r(A) \neq r(B)$ then there exists an $a \in A$ such that $A_{a} \neq(0)$ and $\mathrm{Aa} \cap \mathrm{B}=(0)$.

Proof. Let $U$ be a right annihilator minimal with respect to being contained in $x(B)$ and properly containing $r(A)$. By the choice $\mathrm{AU} \neq(0)$ and since there are no nilpotent ideals, AUAU $\neq(0)$. Pick ua $\in$ UA such that AuaU $\neq(0)$, We claim that $A u a \cap B=(0)$. If not, $0 \neq x u a \varepsilon A u a \cap B$ where $x \in A$. Since $x u a \in B$, and $U \subset r(B)$, xua $U=(0)$. Since $x \in A, r(x) \supset r(A)$. Consider $r(x) \cap \mathrm{U}$; it is a right annihilator, contains $r(A)$ and is contained in $U$; moreover, since uaUC $r(x) \cap U$ and is not in $\mathrm{r}(\mathrm{A})$ we get that $\mathrm{r}(\mathrm{x}) \cap \mathrm{U}=\mathrm{U}$, that is, $\mathrm{UC} \mathrm{r}(\mathrm{x})$, so $\mathrm{xU}=(0)$, contradicting that xua $\neq 0$. This proves the lemms.

DEFINITION, A left ideal I is essential if it intersects every non-zero left ideal non-trivially.

COROLLARY 1. If Rx and Ry are essential then Rxy is essential.

Proof. Let $\mathrm{A} \neq(0)$ be a left ideal of R and let $\bar{A}=\{r \in R \mid r y \in A\}$. By the essentiality of $R y, \bar{A} y=R y \cap A \neq(0)$. By definition, $\bar{A} \supset \ell(y)$ and $\bar{A} y \neq(0)$ so we can apply Lemma 4.14 to $\overline{\mathrm{A}}$ and $\ell(y)$ to get a left ideal $T \neq(0)$ such that $T \subset \bar{A}, T \cap \ell(y)=(0)$. Let $\bar{T}=\{r \in R \mid r x \in T\}$; thus $\overline{\mathrm{T}} \mathrm{x}=\mathrm{Rx} \cap \mathrm{T} \neq(0)$, and $\overline{\mathrm{T}} \mathrm{xy} \neq(0)$ since $\mathrm{T} \cap \ell(\mathrm{y})=(0)$. Thus since Txy $\subset$ Ty $\subset$ Ay $\subset A$ we get Rxy $A \neq(0)$, proving that Rxy is essential.

COROLLARY 2. If Ra is essential then a is regular.
Proof. That $r(a)=(0)$ follows from the special case of Lemma 4.14 in which $A=R$ and $B=R a$.

We consider $l(\mathrm{a})$. We know there is an integer $k$ such that $\boldsymbol{\ell}\left(\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{k}}\right)=\boldsymbol{\ell}\left(\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{k}+1}\right)=\ldots$. If $\mathrm{x} \in \mathrm{Ra}^{\mathrm{k}} \cap \boldsymbol{\ell}(\mathrm{a})$ then $\mathrm{x}=\mathrm{ya}^{\mathrm{k}}$ and $0=\mathrm{xa}=\mathrm{ya}^{\mathrm{k}+1}$ placing $\mathrm{y} \in I\left(\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{k}+1}\right)=I\left(\mathrm{a}^{\mathrm{k}}\right)$, that is, $\mathrm{x}=0$. Since $\mathrm{Ra}^{\mathrm{k}}$ is essential by Corollary 1 we get that $\boldsymbol{I}(\mathrm{a})=(0)$.

From now on, $R$ will be a semi-prime Goldie ring.
LEMMA 4.15. R satisfies the descending chain condition on left annihilators.

Proof, Let $L_{1} \supset L_{2} \supset \ldots \supset L_{n} \supset \ldots$ be a properly des. cending chain of left annthilators. By Lemma 4.14 there exist non-zero left ideals $C_{n} \subset L_{n}$ such that $C_{n} \cap L_{n+1}=(0)$, Since the $C_{n}$ form a direct sum of left ideals we get that the chain of annihilators must terminate.

LEMMA 4.16. If $f(c)=(0)$ then Rc is essential and so c is regular.

Proof. If $\mathrm{A} \neq(0)$ is a left ideal satisfying $\operatorname{Rc} \cap \mathrm{A}=(0)$, since $\ell(c)=(0)$, we get that the $\mathrm{Ac}{ }^{\mathrm{n}}$ form a direct surn of left ideals. Therefore $\mathrm{Rc}_{\mathrm{C}} \cap \mathrm{A} \neq(0)$ and $\mathrm{Rc}_{\mathrm{C}}$ is essential.

A two-sided ideal of $S$ is said to be an annihilator ideal if $S$ is the left annihilator of some left ideal $T$.

LEMMA 4.17. a) A non-zero minimal annihilator ideal is a prime Goldie ring.
b) There is a finite direct sum of such ideals which is an essential left ideal of R .

Proof. a) Let $S$ be a minimal annihilator ideal. If $T \neq(0)$ is a left ideal of $S$, since $R$ is semi-prime, $S T \neq(0)$; since $S T C T$ is a left ideal of $R$ this immediately implies that $S$ has no infinite direct sums of left ideals. It is immediate that the chain condition on left annihilators is inherited by subrings, hence $S$ is a Goldie ring.

Suppose that $A B=(0)$ where $A, B$ are ideals of $S$. Since $S B \subset B, A S B=(0)$, thus $A \subset(S B) \cap S$; if $A \neq(0)$ then by the minimality of S we get $\mathrm{S} \subset \ell(\mathrm{SB})$, and since $\mathrm{SB} \subset \mathrm{S}$ this gives $\mathrm{SB}=(0)$ and so $\mathrm{B}=(0)$. In short, S is a prime Goldie ring.
b) Let $A=S_{1} \oplus S_{2} \oplus \ldots \oplus S_{n}$ be a maximal direct sum of minimal annihilator ideals. We claim that $A$ is essential; suppose that $A \cap K=(0)$ where $K$ is a non-zero left ideal of $R$. Since $A K \subset A \cap K=(0), A K=(0)$, so $K \subset r(A)$. But since $R$ is semi-prime, $A \cap r(A)=(0)$; since $r(A) A=(0)$ (since $R$ is semiprime) we can find a minimal, non-zero annihilator ideal in $r(A)$, and so lengthen the direct sum giving $A$. Thus $A$ is essential.

LEMMA 4.18. If I is an essential left ideal of R then it contains a regular element.

Proof. We first establish the result for prime rings. Choose a 4 so that $\ell(\mathrm{a})$ is minimal. If a is regular, we are done; if not, by Corollary 2 to Lemma 4.14, $\mathrm{Ra}_{\mathrm{a}} \cap \mathrm{J}=(0)$ for some left ideal $\mathrm{J} \neq(0)$. Since I is essential, $\mathrm{I} \cap \mathrm{J} \neq(0)$ hence we may suppose that $J C 1$. If $x \in J, r \in l(a+x), r(a+x)=0$ hence $r a=-r x \in \operatorname{Ra} \cap J=(0)$ thus $r \in I(a) \cap \ell(x)$. By the minimality of $\ell(a)$ we get $\ell(a) \subset \ell(x)$ for all $x \in J$, thus $\ell(a) J=(0)$. Since $J \neq(0)$ and $R$ is prime, $l(a)=\langle 0)$ results, Lemma 4.16 then tells us that a is regular.

Now let $R$ be semi-prime and let $A=S_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus S_{n}$ be as in Lernma 4.17. It is clear that $!\cap S_{i}$ is essential in $S_{i}$. Since $S_{i}$ is prime, $I \cap S_{i}$ contains an element $r_{i}$ regular in $S_{i}$. Let $r=r_{1}+\ldots+r_{n}$; we claim that $r$ is regular in $R_{\text {; if not }} \ell(r) \cap A \neq(0)$ so we can find $t_{1}, \ldots, t_{n}, S_{1} \ldots, S_{n}$ respectively and not all 0 such that $t r=\sum t_{i} r_{i}=0$. Then $t_{i} r_{i}=0$ all $i$, violating the regularity of the $r_{i}$ in $S_{i}$. The element $r$ is the required regular element in 1

THEOREM 4.9. Let R be a semi-prime Goldie ring. Then $R$ has a ring of left quotients $Q$.

Proof. Suppose that $a \in R$ is regular and $b \in R$. By Lemma 4.16, Ra is essential; moreover, as is easy to see, $M=\{r \in R \mid r b \in R a\}$ is also essential, thus by Lemma 4.18, $M$ contains a regular element $c$. Thus $c b=d a \neq 0$; the condition for a left quotient ring to exist, as given in Theorem 4.1 holds in $R$ so $R$ has a ring of left quotients.

We remark two easy facts without proof.

1. If $I$ is a left ideal of $Q$ then $I=Q(I \cap R)$.
2. If $A_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus A_{n}$ is a direct sum of left ideals in $R$ then $Q A_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus Q A_{n}$ is a direct sum in $Q$.

We now reprove Goldie's theorem (Theorem 4.7).
THEOREM 4.10. Q is a semi-simple ring satisfying the descending chain condition on left ideals.

Proof. If $I \neq(0)$ is a left ideal of $Q$ then there is a left ideal $K$ in $R$ such that $(I \cap R) \oplus K$ is essential in $R$ (take $K$ to be the complement in a largest direct sum containing $I \cap R$ as constituent), Since $Q$ is a left quotient ring for $R$ and $(I \cap R) \oplus K$ is essential in $R$ we get that $Q=I \oplus Q K$. If 1 is the unit element in $Q, i=e+f$ where $e \in I, f \in Q K$. Thus $e=e^{2}+e f$, giving $e^{2}=e \neq 0$. Also $I=I e=Q e$. Thus every non-zero left ideal in $Q$ is generated by an idempotent. In particular, $Q$ can have no non-zero nilpotent ideals, so is semiprime. Since every left ideal of $Q$ is principal, $Q$ satisfies the ascending chain condition on all left ideals so has no infinite direct sums of left ideals.

Let $L \neq(0)$ be a left ideal of $Q$; as we have seen $L=Q e$, so $r(L)=r(Q e)=(1-e) Q$, whence $\ell(r(L))=\ell((1-e) Q)=e Q=L$. Therefore every left ideal of $Q$ is a left annihilator. However, by Lemma 4.15, Q satisfies the descending chain condition on left annihilators, thus on all left ideals. Since, as we have seen, $Q$ has no nilpotent ideals and has descending chain condition it must be semi-simple.
