## MORE ON RINGS WITH ASCENDING CHAIN CONDITIONS

We begin this chapter with an exposition of some results obtained jointly with Lance Small. We shall study nil subrings of rings satisfying the ascending chain condition on left annihilators. Our objective is to show that, quite generally, these are nilpotent.

Until further notice R shall be a ring satisfying the ascending chain condition on left annihilators. It is appropriate to remind the reader that this is equivalent to the descending chain condition on right annihilators. Also, since the left annihilator in a subring is merely the intersection of this subring with the left annihilator in the entire ring, our condition is inherited by all subrings of $R$.

(Tier-1 Error)

## We begin with

LEMMA 5.1. Let R be a ring satisfying the ascending chain condition on left annihilators, If R is nil then every nonzero homomorphic image of R contains a non-zero nilpotent ideal.

Proof. Let $\bar{R}$ be a non-zero homomorphic image of $R$; thus $\overline{\mathrm{R}}$ is isomorphic to $\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{M}$ where $\mathrm{M} \neq \mathrm{R}$ is a two-sided ideal of R .

Let $Z \cap=\{\ell(x) \mid x \in \mathrm{R}, x, \mathrm{M}\}$; by hypothesis $O$ has a maximal element, that is, there is an element a in R and not in $M$ such that $f(a)$ is maximal in $Z M$.

For any $x$ in $R, l(a x) \circlearrowright l(a)$; if, in addition, ax is not in $M$ then by the maximality of $t(a)$ we are forced to conclude that $\ell(\mathrm{ax})=\ell(\mathrm{a})$. Suppose that $\operatorname{ar} \nmid \mathrm{M}$; since ar is nilpotent there is an integer $k$ such that $(a r)^{k} \in M$ but $(a r)^{k-1} \& M$. However, ar is nilpotent; thus there is a least positive integer n such that $(\mathrm{ar})^{\mathrm{n}}=0$. Since $(\mathrm{ar})^{\mathrm{k}-1}$ is of the form ax and is not in M , $\ell\left((\mathrm{ar})^{k-1}\right)=\ell(\mathrm{a})$. Because $(\mathrm{ar})^{\mathrm{n}-k+1}$ is in $\ell\left((\mathrm{ar})^{\mathrm{k}-1}\right)$ we are led to $(\mathrm{ar})^{\mathrm{n}-\mathrm{k}+1} \mathrm{a}=0$, and so $(\mathrm{ar})^{\mathrm{n}-\mathrm{k}+2}=(\mathrm{ar})^{\mathrm{n}-\mathrm{k}+1} \mathrm{ar}=0$. By our choice of $n$ we must conclude that $n-k+2 \geq n$ and so $k \leq 2$. Consequently, $(a x)^{2}$ is in $M$ for every $x$ in $R$.

For any $x$ in $R$, since $x+1$ is formally invertible, we have that $a x+a$ is not in $M$; from this results $\ell(a x+a)=\ell(a)$ and the argument used above yields $(\mathrm{ax}+\mathrm{a})^{2}$ is in M .

In $\overline{\mathrm{R}}=\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{M}$ these translate into: $\overline{\mathrm{a}} \neq 0,(\overline{\mathrm{a}} \overline{\mathrm{x}})^{2}=0$, $(\overline{\mathrm{a}} \overrightarrow{\mathrm{x}}+\overrightarrow{\mathrm{a}})^{2}=0$ for all $\overrightarrow{\mathrm{x}}$, which immediately yield that $\overrightarrow{\mathrm{a}}^{2}=0$ and also $\overline{\mathrm{a} \times \overline{\mathrm{a}}}=0$ for all $\overline{\mathrm{x}}$ in $\overline{\mathrm{R}}$. Thus $\overline{\mathrm{a}} \overline{\mathrm{R}}$ is a nilpotent right ideal of $\overline{\mathrm{R}}$, If $\overline{\mathrm{a}} \overline{\mathrm{R}}=(0)$ then the left annihilator of $\overline{\mathrm{R}}$, would be a non-zero nilpotent ideal. If $\overline{\mathrm{a}} \overline{\mathrm{R}} \neq 0$ is generates a nilpotent two-sided ideal. In this way the lemma has been established.

As an immediate corollary we have the basic result:
COROLLARY. If R is a nil ring satisfying the ascending chain condition on left annihilators then R is locally nilpotent.

Proof. As is well known (see for instance (N)) $R$ contains a maximal locally nilpotent ideal $L(R)$, the Levitzki radical of $R$, which contains all locally nilpotent ideals of R. Moreover, $L(R / L(R))=(0)$, hence $R / L(R)$ certainly contains no nilpotent. ideals.

If $L(R) \neq R$, by the lemma, $R / L(R)$ would have a non-zero nilpotent ideal, which it can not. Thus $R / L(R)=(0)$, hence $R=L(R)$. Therefore, $R$ is locally nilpotent.

We continue the investigation of our class of nil rings with
LEMMA 5.2. Let $R$ be a nil ring satisfying the ascending chain condition on left annihilators. Then there exists an element $\mathrm{x}_{0} \neq 0$ in R such that $R \mathrm{x}_{0}=(0)$.

Proof. Let $M$ be the set of finitely generated subrings in $R$ and let $\eta=\{r(S) \mid S, m\}$. By the remark previously made, there is an $S_{0}$ in $M$ such thar $r\left(S_{0}\right)$ is minimal in $n$. Let $x \in R$; then $S_{1}$, the subring generated by $S_{0}$ and $x_{1}$ is finitely generated. Since $S_{0}\left(S_{1}, r\left(S_{0}\right) \supset r\left(S_{1}\right)\right.$, whence, by the minimality of $r\left(S_{0}\right)$ we conclude that $r\left(S_{0}\right)=r\left(S_{1}\right)$. However, $r\left(S_{1}\right)=r\left(S_{0}\right) \cap r(x)$; together with the above equality we conclude that $r\left(S_{0}\right)=r\left(S_{0}\right) \cap r(x)$, that is $r\left(S_{0}\right) C_{r}(x)$ for every $x$ in $R$. But then $\operatorname{Rr}\left(S_{0}\right)=(0)$.

To finish the proof we note that since $S_{0}$ is finitely generated by the corollary to Lemma 5.1, it must be nilpotent. Hence for some integer $k, S_{o}^{k}=(0), S_{o}^{k-1} \neq(0)$. Since $(0) \neq S_{o}^{k-1} \subset r\left(S_{o}\right)$, thus: $r\left(S_{0}\right) \neq(0)$ and we have proved the lemma.

LEMMA 5.3. If R satisfies the ascending chain condition on left (respectively, right) annihilators and if A is a two-sided ideal of R which happens to be a left annihilator in R then $\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{A}$ satisfies the ascending chain condition on left (respectively, right) annihilators.

Proof. As we have previously observed the ascending chain condition on right annihilators translates into the descending chain condition on left annihilators. In view of the one-to-one correspondence set up by Lemma 4.7 between the left annihilators
in $\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{A}$ and certain left annihilators in R the lemma now becomes clear.

We are now in a position to prove the first of the theorems of this chapter.

THEOREM 5.1. If $R$ is a ring which satisfies the ascending chain conditions on left and right annihilators then any nil subring of R is milpotent.

Proof. Since the ascending chain conditions on left and right annihilators are inherited from the ring by its subrings we may, without loss of generality, assume that $R$ is nil. We wish to show that $R$ is nilpotent. Let $T_{k}=\left\{x \in R \mid \times R^{k}=(0)\right\}$. Since $\mathrm{T}_{1} \subset \mathrm{~T}_{2} \subset \ldots . \mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{k}} \subset \ldots$ and since they are left annihilators, by our hypothesis there is an $n$ such that $T_{n}=T_{n+1}=\ldots$. If $T_{n}=R$ then $R^{n+1}=(0)$ and we are done. Suppose $T_{n} \neq R$; then $\overline{\mathrm{R}}=\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{n}} \neq(0)$. By Lemma $5.3, \overline{\mathrm{R}}$ satisfies the ascending chain condition on right annihilators so by Lemma 5.2 (in its reflected form) there exists an $\bar{x} \neq 0$ in $\bar{R}$ such that $\bar{x} \bar{R}=(0)$. Therefore $x R C T_{n}$ (where $x$ is an inverse image of $\bar{x}$ in $R$ ) and so $x R R^{n}=(0)$. Thus $x R^{n+1}=(0)$; from our choice of $n$ this forces $x R^{D}=(0)$ and so $x \in T_{n}$. But then $\bar{x}=0$ contrary to hypothesis. This contradiction proves that $\bar{R}=(0)$ and so $R=T_{n}$ and $R^{n+1}=(0)$.

If one examines the proof given for Theorem 5.1, one sees that a more general result has actually been proved, that only a part of the ascending chain condition on the right has been used, The exact theorem proved was:

THEOREM 5.1'. Let $R$ be a nil ring satisfying the ascending chain condition on left annihilators and such that the ascending chain of right annihilators $\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{n}}=\left\{\mathrm{x} \mid \mathrm{R}^{\mathrm{n}} \mathrm{x}=(0)\right\}$ becomes stationary. Then R is nilpotent,

An example by Sasiada shows that one can not get by with just the ascending chain condition on left annihilators. Let R be the ring generated over the integers by the elements $x_{1}, x_{2}, \ldots, x_{n}, \ldots$ subject to the conditions $x_{j} x_{i}=0$ for $j \geq i$. Then as can easily be checked, $R$ is nil, satisfies the ascending chain condition on left annihilators yet is not nilpotent.

We now consider nil rings satisfying a polynomial identity. Using a very simple argument due to Posner (see the proof of Lemma 5,7 ) we have that any prime ring satisfying a polynomial identity over its centroid satisfies the ascending chain condition on left and right annihilators. Thus, by our Theorem 5.1, any nil subring of such a ring is nilpotent. We summarize this in

LEMMA 5.4. Let $R$ be a prime ring satislying a polynomial identity over its centroid. Then any nil subring of R is nilpotent.

The above result can also be derived as a consequence of the characterization given by Posner of prime rings satisfying polynomial identities. We shall treat this theorem of Posner later.

Let R be any nil ring satisfying a polynomial identity with properly conditioned coefficients (e.g., R may be an algebra over a field satisfying a polynomial identity with coefficients in the field, $R$ may be any ring, the identity having as coefficients operators, some of which are invertible, $R / P$ may be supposed to satisfy some polynomial identity, depending on $P$, for each prime ideal $P$ of $R$, etc., etc.). A theorem of Kaplansky asserts that such a ring is locally nilpotent. We give a new, and very easy proof of this in the

COROLLARY 1. A nil ring satisfying a polynomial identity is locally nilpotent.

Proof. Let $S$ be a finitely generated subring of $R$ which we suppose is not nilpotent. Since $S^{k}$ is also finitely generated, by Zorn's lemma there exists an ideal $P$ of $R$ maximal with respect to not containing any $S^{k}$. However, $P$ is a prime ideal of $R$ for if $A B C P$ with $A, B$ ideals properly larger than $P$ then $A \supset s^{k_{1}}, B \supset s^{k_{2}}$ whence $P \supset A B \supset s^{k_{1}+k_{2}}$, a contradiction. However, $\bar{R}=R / P$ is a prime ring satisfying a polynomial identity; since, in addition, it is nil, by the lemma it is nilpotent. This is inconsistent with $\overline{\mathrm{R}}$ being prime. Thus no such $P$ exists and we conclude that $S^{k}=(0)$ for some $k$.

THEOREM 5.2. Let $R$ be a nil Goldie ring; then $R$ is nilpotent.

Proof. Let $A=\{x \in R \mid R x=(0)\}$ and let $T$ be the torsion part of $A$. Of course, $T$ is a two-sided ideal of $R$. We can write $T$ as a direct sum of its primary components, $T_{p}$, where if $t \& T_{p}$ then $p^{m(t)} t=0$ where $p$ is a prime.

Let $V_{0}=(0)$ and $V_{1}=\left\{x \in T_{p} \mid p x=0\right\} . \quad V_{1}$ is an ideal of $R$ and is a vector space over the field $P$ of $p$ elements. Since there are no infinite direct sums of left ideals in $R$ and since every subgroup of $V_{1}$, which is an algebra over $P$, is a left ideal of $R$ we must conclude that $V_{1}$ is finite-dimensional over $P$. Let $V_{2}=\left\{x \in T_{p} \mid p^{2} x=0\right\}$, and let $W_{2}=V_{2} / V_{1}$. If $u_{1}, \ldots, u_{k} \ldots$ are in $V_{2}$ then $p u_{1}, \ldots, p u_{k} \ldots$ are in $V_{1}$ which is finite-dimensional over $P$, hence $\Sigma a_{i} p u_{i}=0$ for some nonzero $a_{i}$ in $P$; hence $p \sum a_{i} u_{i}=0$ and so $\sum a_{i} u_{i} \in V_{1}$. But then $W_{2}$ is finite-dimensional over $P$. Similarly, if $V_{i}=\left(x \in T_{p} \mid p^{i} x=0\right)$ then $W_{i}=V_{i} / V_{i-1}$ is a finite-dimensional vector space over $P$.

Each $V_{i}$ is a right ideal of $R_{\text {, hence }} V_{i} / V_{i-1}$ is an $R$-module which is a finite-dimensional vector space over $P$.

Since $R$ induces a nil finite ring of linear transformations on these, this ring of linear transformations must be nilpotent. Therefore $V_{i} R^{n_{i}} \subset V_{i-1}$ for some integer $n_{i}$. From this we get that $V_{i} R^{m_{i}}=(0)$ where $m_{i}=n_{1}+n_{2}+\ldots+n_{i}$.

However, by the ascending chain condition on left annihilators there exists an integer $k_{0}$ such that if $x R^{m}=\langle 0\rangle$ then $x R^{k_{0}}=(0)$. Given any element a in $T_{p}$ it is in some $V_{i}$ hence $a R^{m_{i}}=(0)$ and so $a R^{k_{0}}=(0)$. In other words, $T_{P} R^{k_{0}}=(0)$.

Since $T$ is a direct sum of the $T_{p}{ }^{\prime} s$ we get that $T R^{k_{0}}=(0)$.
Consider $\bar{R}=R / T$ and let $\bar{A}=A / T ; \bar{A}$ is torsion free. Let $V=\bar{A} \otimes_{Z} Q$ where $Z$ is the ring of integers and $Q$ is the field of rational numbers. If $\bar{u}_{1} \ldots ., \bar{u}_{n}$ in $\bar{A}$ are linearly independent (as elements in $\bar{V}$ ) over $Q$ they are a priori linearly independent over $Z$, hence their inverse images $u_{1}, \ldots, u_{n}$ in $A$ are linearly independent over $Z$. Since these then generate a direct sum of left ideals of R (since any subgroup of $A$ is a left ideal of $R$ ) we get, from our hypothesis, that $\bar{V}$ is finite-dimensional over $Q$. Thus $\bar{R}$ induces a ring of linear transformations on $\bar{V}$ by $(\bar{a} \times q) t \overline{\bar{T}}=\bar{a} \bar{r} \times q$. The hornomorphism $\bar{r} \rightarrow t$ has kernel exactly $r(\bar{A})$, thus $\bar{r} / r(\bar{A})$ is a nil ring of $n \times n$ matrices over the rationals, where $n=d i n_{Q} \bar{V}$. By invoking our Theorem 5.1, $\overline{\mathrm{R}} / \mathrm{r}(\overline{\mathrm{A}})$ is nilpotent, hence $(\overline{\mathrm{R}} / \mathrm{r}(\overline{\mathrm{A}}))^{\mathrm{m}}=(0)$, so $\overline{\mathrm{R}}^{\mathrm{m}} \mathrm{Cr}(\overline{\mathrm{A}})$ and so $\overline{\mathrm{A}}^{\mathrm{m}}=(0)$, whence $A R^{m} \subset T$. Since $T R^{k_{0}}=(0)$ we get $A R^{m^{\prime}} R^{k_{0}}=(0)$ and so $A R^{m+k_{0}}=(0)$. By our choice of $k_{0}$ it results that $A R^{k_{0}}=(0)$.

Let $\mathrm{U}=\left\{\mathrm{x}<\mathrm{R} \mid \times \mathrm{R}^{\mathrm{k}_{\mathrm{o}}}=(0)\right\}$. In $\mathrm{R}^{\mathrm{t}}=\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{U}$ we know, by Lemma 5.3 that the ascending chain condition on left armihilators holds. If $\mathrm{R}^{\mathrm{f}} \neq(0)$, since it is nil, we would have by Lemma 5.2
that there is an ideal $\mathrm{B}^{\prime} \neq(0)$ so that $\mathrm{R}^{\prime} \mathrm{B}^{\prime}=(0)$. Thus if B denotes the inverse image in $R$ of $B^{\prime}, R B \subset U$, whence $R B R^{k_{0}}=(0)$. However, this places $B R^{k_{0}}$ in $A$, and the discussion above allows us to conclude that $\left(B R^{k_{o}}\right) R^{k_{o}}=(0)$. Since $B R^{2 k_{0}}=(0)$, by the choice of $k_{0}$ we are lod to $B R^{k_{0}}=(0)$ and so $B \subset U$ and $B^{\prime}=(0)$. This last conclusion is in variance with $B^{\prime} \neq(0)$, forcing us to conclude that $R^{\prime}=(0)$, that is, $R=U$. From the definition of $U$ we obtain $R^{k_{0}+1}=(0)$ and so $R$ is nilpotent. This proves the theorem.

Lanski has generalized Theorem 5.2 to the following: any nil subring of a Goldie ring is nilpotent. His method of proof is to show that the example of Sasiada cited right after Theorem 5.1' can not be imbedded in a Goldie ring. Using this, given a nil but not nilpotent subring of a Goldie ring, he constructs in this subring a sequence of elements that behave like those in Sasiada's example. This enables Lanski to arrive at his result.

We prove another theorem in the vein of nil implies nilpotent. In this theorem we suppose that R is an algebra over an integral domain $\Omega$, that is, $\alpha x=0, \alpha \in \Omega, x \in R$ implies that $\alpha=0$ or $\mathrm{x}=0$ and $\alpha(\mathrm{xy})=(\alpha \mathrm{x}) \mathrm{y}=\mathrm{x}(\alpha \mathrm{y})$.

THEOREM 5.3. Let $R$ be a nil ring satisfying the ascending chain condition on left annihilators. If R satisfies a polynomial identity (with coefficients in $\Omega$ ) then R must be nilpotent.

Proof. By the ascending chain condition on left annihilators there is an integer $k$ such that $x R^{t}=(0)$ then $x R^{k}=(0)$. Let $U=\left\{x, R \mid \times R^{k}=(0)\right\}$ and consider $\bar{R}=R / U$. If $\bar{x} \bar{R}=(0)$ then $x R \subset U$ so $x R^{k+1}=(0)$ resulting in $x R^{k}=(0), x \in U$ and so $\bar{x}=0$. If $U=R$ then $R$ is nilpotent; if $U \neq R$ since $\Omega U$ clearly is in $U, \bar{R}$ is a nil ring in which $\bar{x} \bar{R} \neq(0)$ for $\bar{x} \neq 0$ and satisfies the same identity as $R$. All in all we have shown that if $R$
is not nilpotent we may assume that $x R=(0)$ only if $x=0$. We proceed to show that this situation is impossible.

As is well known (and easy) we can suppose that the identity satisfied by $R$ is of the form

$$
p\left(x_{1}, \ldots, x_{n}\right)=\sum_{\sigma \in S_{n}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(1)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n)}
$$

where $\alpha_{\sigma} \in \Omega, S_{n}$ is the symmetric group of degree $n$.
Let $A=\{x, R \mid R x=(0)\}$; by Lemma 5.2, $A \neq(0)$. If $x_{1}=a \in A, p\left(a, x_{2}, \ldots, x_{n}\right)=0$ gives us, since $R a=(0)$, that

$$
a \sum_{\sigma \in S_{n-1}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(2)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n)}=0
$$

for $x_{i} \in R$, that is

$$
A\left(\sum_{\sigma \in S_{n-1}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(2)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n)}\right)=(0),
$$

Since $\Omega r(A) C_{r}(A), \bar{R}=R / r(A)$ satisfies the identity

$$
q\left(x_{2}, \ldots, x_{n}\right)=\sum_{\sigma \in S_{n-1}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(2)} \ldots x_{\sigma(n)}
$$

of degree $n-1$. By induction, since $\bar{R}$ is nil, satisfies the ascending chain condition on left annihilators (Lemma 5.3) and a polynomial identity of degree $n-1$ or less we conclude that $\bar{R}$ is nilpotent, say $\bar{R}^{m}=(0)$. Therefore $R^{m} \subset r(A)$ leading to $A R^{m}=(0)$. Since, as we have seen above, $l(R)=(0)$ we get the contradiction $A=(0)$. The theorem is now proved,

Let us recall something done in (N) about identities. The standard identity of degree $k$ is defined by

$$
\left[x_{1}, \ldots, x_{k}\right]=\Sigma(-1)^{\sigma} x_{\sigma(1)}, \ldots x_{\sigma(k)}
$$

where $(-1)^{\sigma}$ is the signature of the permutation $\sigma$ in $S_{k}$ and the sum runs over all $\sigma \in \mathrm{S}_{\mathrm{k}}$. If an algebra. A is of dimension
$k$ over a field $F$ then it satisfies the standard identity $\left[x_{1}, \ldots, x_{k+1}\right]$. Thus the $n \times n$ matrices over a field satisfies $\left[x_{1}, \ldots, x_{n^{2}+1}\right]$. It is a fact, first proved by Amitsur and Levitski that they indeed satisfy $\left[\mathrm{x}_{1}, \ldots, \mathrm{x}_{2 \mathrm{n}}\right]$ and that this is the minimal identity. However we shall not need so sharp a result here. For a field $F$ let $r(n)$ be the degree of the minimal identity satisfied by $F_{n}$. Following a very easy argument by Kaplansky we show

LEMMA 5.5. $\mathrm{r}(\mathrm{n}) \geq \mathrm{r}(\mathrm{n}-1)+2$.
Proof. We imbed $F_{n-1}$ in $F_{n}$ as $\left(\begin{array}{cc}F_{n-1} & 0 \\ 0 & 0\end{array}\right)$, Let $t=r(n-1)-1$; hence we can find $a_{1} \ldots, a_{t} \in F_{n-1}$ such that $\left[\mathrm{a}_{1}, \ldots, \mathrm{a}_{\mathrm{t}}\right] \neq 0$. As imbedded in $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{n}}$ we see immediately that $\left[a_{1}, \ldots, a_{t^{\prime}}, e_{k n}, e_{n n}\right]=\left[a_{1}, \ldots, a_{t}\right] e_{k n} e_{n n}=\left[a_{1}, \ldots, a_{t}\right] e_{k n} \neq 0$ for some $k$ (since $\left[a_{1}, \ldots, a_{t}\right]=\left(\begin{array}{ll}A & 0 \\ 0 & 0\end{array}\right)$ where $A \neq 0$ ). Thus indeed $r(n)>t+2=r(n-1)+1$.

From this we clearly have that $\mathrm{r}(\mathrm{n}) \geq 2 \mathrm{n}$. We proceed to a result about finite-dimensional simple algebras,

THEOREM 5.4, Let $R$ be a simple algebra finitedimensional over its center $F$; let $A \subset R$ be a subalgebra over $F$ such that any polynomial identity satisfied by $A$ over the prime field ${ }^{P}$ of $F$ is satisfied by $R$. Then $A=R$.

Proof. Let $\bar{F}$ be the algebraic closure of $F$; the hypothesis carries over to $\mathrm{R} \otimes_{\mathrm{F}} \overline{\mathrm{F}}$ and $\mathrm{A} \otimes_{\mathrm{F}} \overline{\mathrm{F}}$. Hence to prove the theorem we may assume, without loss of generality, that $F$ is algebraically closed. Thus by Wedderburn's theorem $\mathrm{R}=\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{n}}$.

If $A$ is semi-simple then by Wedderburn's theorem $A \approx F_{n_{1}} \oplus \ldots \oplus F_{n_{k}}$ hence $A$ satisfies all the identities satis-
fied over $P$ by $F_{n_{t}}$ where $n_{t}=\max \left(n_{1}, \ldots, n_{k}\right)$ thus by assumption $F_{n}$ satisfies all the polynomial identities over $P$ satisfied by $F_{n_{t}}$. By Lemma 5.10 we deduce that $n_{t}=n$ and hence $A=F_{n}$ the desired conclusion.

Assume then that $A$ is not semi-simple and that its radical $N$ is not ( 0 ). Now $A / N \approx F_{n_{1}} \oplus \ldots \oplus F_{n_{k}}$ where each $n_{i}<n$ since $\operatorname{dim} A / N<\operatorname{dim} A \leq \operatorname{dim} F_{n}$. If $m=\max \left(n_{1}, \ldots, n_{k}\right)$ then $A / N$ satisfies the identities of $F_{m}$ hence $\left[x_{1}, \ldots, x_{r}\right]$ where $r=r(m)$, that is to say, $\left[a_{1} \ldots . a_{r}\right] \in N$ forall $a_{i}$ \& A. Now $N$ is nilpotent, say $N^{t}=(0)$, and is an ideal of $A$, hence satis fies the identity over $P_{1}\left(\left[x_{1}, \ldots, x_{r}\right] y\right)^{t}$, By hypothesis, $F_{n}$ must satisfy this identity and since $F_{n}$ is simple and has no nilpotent ideals we get $F_{n}$ must satisfy $\left[x_{1}, \ldots, x_{r}\right] y$ and so, $\left[x_{1}, \ldots, x_{r}\right]$. Since $r=r(m)$ and $m=\max n_{i}<n$ we get a contradiction. This proves the theorem.

We now turn to a slightly different direction; we intend to introduce the idea of an ultra-product. While this has been used ${ }^{a}$ great deal in logic it has not, until quite recently in the work of Amitsur, been used in ring theory. Much of the material to follow is treated along lines laid out by Amitsur.

Let $\mathrm{A}=\{\alpha\}$ be a set; a set $\mathcal{Z}=\{\mathrm{S}\}$ of subsets of A is a filter if:

1. $\phi$, the empty set, is not in
2. $S_{1}, S_{2} \in \mathcal{F}$ implies $S_{1} \cap S_{2} \in \mathcal{F}$
3. $S \in \mathcal{F}$ and $T \supset S$ then $T \in \mathcal{F}$

A filter is said to be an ultra-filter if it is maximal in the natural ordering of filters: $\mathcal{G}_{1} \leq \mathcal{F}_{2}$ if $s \in \mathcal{F}_{1}$ implies $s \in \mathcal{F}_{2}$. By Zorn's lemma it is clear that any filter can be enlarged to an ultra-filter.

Ultra-filters have the very simple characterization given by
LEMMA 5.6. A filter $f$ on $A$ is an ultra-filter if and only if for any subset $T$ of $A$ either $T \in \mathcal{F}$ or the complement of $T$, $A-T \in \mathcal{J}$.

Proof. If $\mathcal{F}$ is a filter and T is a subset of $\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{T} / \mathcal{F}$ then only one of the following can hold: $\mathrm{T} \cap \mathrm{S}=0$ for some $\mathrm{S}, \mathcal{F}$ or $(A-T) \cap S^{t}=\varphi$ for some $S^{t} \in \mathcal{F}$. Otherwise
$\phi \neq S \cap S^{t}=A \cap S \cap S^{t}=((A-T) \cup T) \cap S \cap S^{t} \subset\left((A-T) \cap S^{t}\right) U(T \cap S)=\epsilon$.

If $\mathcal{F}$ is an ultra-filter and there exists $S^{\prime} \in \mathcal{F}$ with $(A-T) \cap S^{x}=\phi$ then $T \cap S \neq \phi$ for all $S \in \mathcal{F}$. Thus $\{T, \mathcal{F}\}$ forms a base of a filter which, by the maximality of $\mathcal{f}$ forces $T, \mathcal{I}$. By the same argument, if $(A-T) \cap S \neq \phi$ for all $S \in \mathcal{I}$, $A-T \& \mathcal{F}$. This proves the result in one direction.

If, on the other hand, $\mathscr{G}$ is a filter with the property that for any subset $T$ of $A$ either $T \in \mathcal{F}$ or $A-T<\mathcal{F}$ then we claim that $\mathcal{F}$ is an ultra-filter. Suppose $\mathcal{F}^{\prime}$ is a properly larger filter and $T \in \mathcal{F}^{\prime}, T \nmid \mathcal{F}$. Thus $A-T \in \mathcal{F}$ so $A-T \in \mathcal{F}^{\prime}$, whence $\phi=T \cap(A-T)<\mathcal{F}^{\prime}$, a contradiction.

Let $\mathrm{R}_{\alpha}$ be a set of rings indexed by $\alpha \in \mathrm{A}$, and let $\prod_{\alpha \in \mathrm{A}} \mathrm{R}_{\alpha}$ be the complete product of the $\mathrm{R}_{\alpha}$ 's. As usual we can consider this ring as the set of functions with $f(\alpha)$ \& $R_{\alpha}$ under pointwise addition and multiplication.

Let $\$$ be a filter on $\mathrm{A}_{\text {; }}$ we define $\mathrm{f}, \mathrm{g} \in \Pi \mathrm{R}_{\alpha}$ to be equivalent if $\{\alpha \in A \mid f(\alpha)=g(\alpha)\}$ is in $\mathcal{G}$. This is indeed an equivalence relation $\sim$. Let $I=\{f \mid f \sim 0\}$; $I$ is an ideal of $\prod R_{\alpha}$. We define $\prod \mathrm{R}_{\alpha} / \mathcal{F}$ to be $\prod \mathrm{R}_{\alpha} / \mathrm{I}$. We call $\prod \mathrm{R}_{\alpha} / \mathcal{F}$ an ultra-product if $\mathcal{F}$ is an ultra-filter.

A basic property of ultra-products is given in
THEOREM 5.5, Let $\mathrm{R}_{\alpha}$ be a primitive ring with irreducible module $\mathrm{V}_{\alpha^{\prime}}$ commuting ring $\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{R}_{\alpha}}\left(\mathrm{V}_{\alpha^{\prime}}, \mathrm{V}_{\alpha}\right)$ and let $\mathrm{C}_{\alpha}$ be the center of $\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}$. For any ultra-filter $\mathscr{F}^{\alpha}$ on $\{\alpha\}, \mathrm{R}=\prod \mathrm{R}_{\alpha} / \mathcal{F}$ is a primitive ring with irreducible module $\mathrm{V}=\prod \mathrm{V}_{\alpha} / \mathrm{J}$ and commuting ring $\mathrm{D}=\prod \mathrm{D}_{\alpha} / \mathcal{F}$ with center $\mathrm{C}=\prod \mathrm{C}_{\alpha} / \mathcal{F}$.

Proof. We prove the large part of the theorem, leaving it to the reader to show that the commuting ring is indeed as described. In what is to follow we shall not need the result about D or C .

Let $\overline{\mathrm{R}}=\prod \mathrm{R}_{\alpha}$, the complete product of the $\mathrm{R}_{\alpha}$. For $r<\overline{\mathrm{R}}=\prod \mathrm{R}_{\alpha}$ we define an action on $\overline{\mathrm{V}}=\prod \mathrm{V}_{\alpha}$ by $(\mathrm{vr}) \alpha=\mathrm{v}(\alpha) \mathrm{r}(\alpha)$. If $r \equiv 0(\mathcal{I})$ and $v \in \bar{V},\{\alpha \mid(v r)(\alpha)=0\}$ contains $\{\alpha \mid r(\alpha)=0\}$ which is in $\mathcal{F}$ so $\mathrm{vr} \equiv 0(\mathcal{F})$; similarly if $\mathrm{v} \equiv 0(\mathcal{F})$ then $\mathrm{vr} \equiv 0$ (F) for all $\mathrm{r} \in \overline{\mathrm{R}}$. Thus we get $\mathrm{R}=\prod \mathrm{R}_{\alpha} / \mathcal{F}$ to act on $\mathrm{V}=\prod \mathrm{V}_{\alpha} \mathcal{F}$.

We claim that $V$ is an irreducible $R$-module; for let $\mathrm{V} \equiv 0$ (f) and let $\mathrm{u} \in \mathrm{V}$. Let $\mathrm{T}_{0}=\{\alpha \mid \mathrm{v}(\alpha) \neq 0\}$; since $\mathrm{v} \neq 0(\mathcal{f})$, $\{\alpha \mid v(\alpha)=0\} / \mathcal{F}$ hence $T_{0}=A-\{\alpha \mid v(\alpha)=0\}$ is in $\mathcal{F}$. For $\alpha \in T_{0}$ pick $r_{\alpha} \in R_{\alpha}$ so that $v_{\alpha} r_{\alpha}=u_{\alpha}(=u(\alpha))$; we can do this since $R_{\alpha}$ acts primitively on $\mathrm{V}_{\alpha}$ and $\mathrm{v}_{\alpha} \neq 0$. Therefore $\mathrm{vr}=\mathrm{u}$ (F) whence $\mathrm{vR}=\mathrm{V}$ confirming the irreducibility of V . Moreover R acts faithfully on $V$ for if $v r=0$ for all $V \in V$ and $r \in R$ if $r_{1} \in \bar{R}$ is a representative for $r$ then $\left.\{\alpha\}\left(v r_{1}\right)(\alpha)=0\right\}$ is in $\mathcal{J}$ and is contained in $\left\{\alpha \mid r_{1}(\alpha)=0\right\}$. Hence $\left\{\alpha \mid r_{1}(\alpha)=0\right\}$ is in $\mathcal{F}$ leading to $r_{1} \equiv 0$ (f) that is, $r=0$.

We now consider prime rings satisfying a polynomial identity.
LEMMA 5.7. If $R$ is a prime ring satisfying a polynomial identity over its centroid then $R$ is a left Goldie ring and a right Goldie ring.

Proof. We may assume that R satisfies a multilinear identity

$$
p\left(x_{1}, \ldots, x_{n}\right)=\sum_{\sigma \in S_{n}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(1)} \ldots x_{\sigma(n)}
$$

where the $\alpha_{\sigma}$ are in the centroid of $R$ and $\sigma$ runs over the symmetric group $\mathrm{S}_{\mathrm{n}}$.

We claim that in R no direct sum of left ideals can have length $n$, for if $I_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus I_{n}$ is such a direct sum pick $x_{1} \in I_{i}$. From $0=p\left(x_{1}, \ldots, x_{n}\right)=\Sigma \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(1)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n)}$ and from the directness of the sum we get

$$
\left\langle\sum_{\sigma \in S_{n-1}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(1)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n-1)}\right)_{n}=(0) .
$$

Since R is prime this forces

$$
\sum_{\sigma \in S_{n-1}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(1)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n-1)}=0
$$

for $x_{1}, I_{1}, x_{2}, I_{2}, \ldots, x_{n-1}, I_{n-1}$. Repeating the argument we eventually get $I_{1}=(0)$, a contradiction. Similarly we get no infinite direct sums of right ideals.

We now turn to the ascending chain condition on left annihilators. Let $I_{1} \subset I_{2} \subset \ldots C I_{n}$ be a properly ascending chain of left annihilators where $I_{i}=f\left(K_{i}\right), I_{i} \neq I_{i+1}$

By as sumption $I_{i} K_{i-1} \neq(0), K_{1} \supset K_{2} \supset \ldots \supset K_{n}$. In $p\left(x_{1}, \ldots, x_{n}\right)$, put $x_{1}, \ldots, x_{n-1} \in I_{n}, x_{n} \in K_{n}$; using

$$
p\left(x_{1}, \ldots, x_{n}\right)=\sum \alpha_{n} x_{\sigma(1)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n)}
$$

we get

$$
K_{n} \sum_{\sigma \in S_{n-1}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(1)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n-1)}=0
$$

for $x_{i}<I_{n}$. Now (0) $\neq K_{n-1} I_{n-1} C I_{n-1} \cap K_{n-1}$; pick $x_{n-1} \in x_{n-1} \in K_{n-1} I_{n-1}$ and $x_{1}, \ldots, x_{n-2} \in I_{n-1}$. We get

$$
K_{n-1} I_{n-1} \sum_{\sigma \in S_{n-2}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(1)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n-2)}=(0)
$$

so that by the primeness of R ,

$$
I_{n-1} \sum_{\sigma<S_{n-2}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma 1)} \ldots x_{\sigma(n-2)}=(0)
$$

for $x_{1} \ldots \ldots x_{n-2} \in I_{n-1}$. In particular, if they are in $I_{n-2}$ except for $x_{n-2}<K_{n-2} I_{n-2}$ we get

$$
I_{n-1} K_{n-2} I_{n-2} \sum_{\sigma \in S_{n-3}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(1)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n-3)}=(0) .
$$

Continuing we get $I_{n-1} K_{n-2} I_{n-2} K_{n-3} \cdots=(0)$. Since $I_{i} K_{i-1} \neq(0)$ we get a contradiction.

We can argue similarly for right annihilators. This proves the lemma.

We now have all the required pieces to prove an interesting theorem due to Posner.

THEOREM 5.6. Suppose that $R$ is a prime ring satisfying a polynomial identity over its centroid. Then R can be imbedded as an order in $D_{n}$ where $D$ is a division algebra finite-dimensional over its center. Otherwise put, the ring of quotients of R exists and satisfies the same identity as does R .

## DECOMPOSITION IN NOETHERIAN RINGS ${ }^{1}$

The topic treated in this chapter is an attempt to simulate in a non-commutative Noetherian ring the Lasker-Noether primary decomposition in a commutative Noetherian ring. For a large part, the results we shall give are due to Lesieur and Croisot.

A decomposition is given for an arbitrary left ideal as an intersection of special left ideals ... the so-called tertiary ones -and one could hope that this would be a suitable vehicle for a profound study of non-commutative Noetherian rings. Unfortunately, at least as yet, this has not been the case; we know of no effective application of these ideas to obtain the central, deep and not-yet-established theorems that would reveal the make up of a general Noetherian ring.

Let us consider what might be considered a down-to-earth question, whose answer would generalize a known theorem of Levitzki: Let R be a left Noetherian ring and $\lambda, \mu$ left ideals of $R_{\text {; }}$ suppose that for all $a \in \lambda, a^{n(a)}, \mu$ for a suitable integer $n(a)$; is it then true that $\lambda^{k} \subset \mu$ for some integer $k$ ? This would seem to be a natural target for such a decomposition theory yet in this general setting the theory falls short of providing an answer. However, in all fairness, making use of some of its concepts if not the results themselves we have been able to establish the special case of the result in which $a^{n} \epsilon \mu$ for all a \& $\lambda$,

[^0]$n$ a fixed integer, and in the general case when $R$ satisfies a polynomial identity.

All rings R to be considered will be left-Noetherian, that is, satisfy the ascending chain condition on left ideals, and will have unit elements. All modules $M$ will be unital, finitely generated left R-modules.

DEFINITION. An element $\mathrm{a} \in \mathrm{R}$ is an annihilating element for M if $\mathrm{aN}=(0)$ for some submodule $\mathrm{N} \neq(0)$ of M . A left ideal $I$ of $R$ is an annihilating left ideal for $M$ if $I N=(0)$ for some submodule $\mathrm{N} \neq(0)$ of M .

We denote by $O(M)$ the ideal $\{a<R \mid a M=(0)\}$ of $R$,
The next few definitions make sense for any ring and all or parts of the very early lemmas hold in this more general context, but all these will be of most interest in the Noetherian case.

DEFINITION, If $N$ is a submodule of $M$ then the primary radical of $N$, written $\operatorname{rad}(N)$, is the intersection of all prime ideals of R which contain $\mathrm{O}(\mathrm{M} / \mathrm{N}) . \mathrm{N}$ is a primary submodule of M if all annihilating elements for $\mathrm{M} / \mathrm{N}$ are in $\operatorname{rad}(\mathrm{N})$.

A remark which holds for any ring R ; if $\mathrm{x} \in \operatorname{rad}(\mathrm{N})$ then $x^{t} \in O(M / N)$ for some $t$ depending on $x$. This is true since the intersection of all prime ideals of a ring is a nil ideal.

One further definition at this point:

One immediately sees that:
$t-\operatorname{rad}(N)=\{a, R \mid$ for all $\alpha / N$ there exists $\beta \in M, \beta / N$ with $\mathrm{aR} \beta \subset \mathrm{N}\}$.

The theory developed here does not have many of the desirable properties of the primary decomposition theory for commutative Noetherian rings. For instance, the tertiary radical of a left ideal I of R , considered as an R -module does not necessarily contain I . An even greater shortcoming is that one can find left Noetherian rings R and a module M with submodules $\mathrm{N}_{1} \mathrm{CN}_{2}$ for which $t-\operatorname{rad}\left(N_{1}\right)$ ( $t-\operatorname{rad}\left(N_{2}\right)$. In fact one can show that the t-radical of M is monotonic if and only if for all submodules N of M , $\operatorname{rad}(\mathrm{N})=\mathrm{t}-\operatorname{rad}(\mathrm{N})$, and so if all tertiary modules are primary.

Emmy Noether gave a simple example of a situation where tertiary modules are not always primary. Let $Q$ be the field of
of rational numbers and let R be the algebra generated over $Q$ by $a, b, c$ where $a^{2}=a, b^{2}=b, c^{2}=a b=b a=c a=b c=0$ and $\mathrm{ac}=\mathrm{cb}=\mathrm{c}$. It is easy to verify that in the R -module R , the submodule (left ideal) Ra is tertiary but not primary.

We now examine when tertiary modules are primary.
DEFINITION, An R-module is called an Artin-Reese module if for each left ideal I of R and each submodule N of M and each integer $n \geq 0$ we can find an integer $h=h(n) \geq 0$ such that $I^{h} M \cap N \subset I^{n} N$.

LEMMA 6.9. A P-tertiary submodule N of M is primary if and only if $\mathrm{P}^{n} \mathrm{M} \subset \mathrm{N}$ for some n .

Proof. If N is primary then $\operatorname{rad}(\mathrm{N})=\mathrm{t}-\operatorname{rad}(\mathrm{N})=\mathrm{P}$ and so since $x \in \operatorname{rad}(N)$ implies $x^{k(x)} \in \alpha(M / N)$, by a theorem of Levitski, $\mathrm{P}^{\mathrm{n}}(\alpha \mathrm{M} / \mathrm{N})$ (see Lemma 6.3). Hence $\mathrm{P}^{\mathrm{n}} \mathrm{M} \subset \mathrm{N}$.

On the other hand, if $\mathrm{P}^{n} \mathrm{M} \subset \mathrm{N}$ then $\mathrm{P}^{n} \subset \operatorname{rad}(\mathrm{~N})$; since $\operatorname{rad}(\mathrm{N})$ is an intersection of prime ideals this puts P in $\operatorname{rad}(\mathrm{N})$. Together with $\operatorname{rad}(N) C P$ we get the result,

THEOREM 6.4 An R-module M is an Artin-Reese module if and only if every tertiary submodule of M is primary.

Proof. Suppose that M is an Artin-Reese module and that N is a P -tertiary submodule of M . Let $\mathrm{N}^{\prime}=\{\alpha<\mathrm{M} \mid \mathrm{P} \alpha \subset \mathrm{N}\}$, then there exists an integer $n$ so that $P^{n} M \cap N^{1} \subset P N^{1} \subset N$. Thus by Lemma 6.2, $\mathrm{P}^{\mathrm{n}} \mathrm{M} \subset \mathrm{N}$; Lemma 6.9 then tells us that N is primary.

Suppose on the other hand, that all tertiary submodules of M are primary. Let N be a submodule of M and n an integer, and I a left ideal of $R$. Let $I^{n} N=N_{1} \cap \ldots \cap N_{r} \cap N_{1} \cap \ldots \cap N_{\#}^{1}$ be a reduced tertiary decomposition of $I^{n} \mathrm{~N}$ where each $\mathrm{N}_{\mathrm{i}}$ is $P_{i}$-tertiary and each $N_{j}$ is $P_{j}^{1}$-tertiary and where $I^{n} C P_{i}$ for
$i=1, \ldots, z$ and $i^{n} \not \subset P_{j}^{t}$ for $j=1,2, \ldots, s$. Since tertiary modules are primary there exists an integer $h$ such that $I^{h} \mathrm{M} \subset \mathrm{N}_{1} \cap \ldots \cap \mathrm{~N}_{r^{+}}$. Now $\mathrm{N} \subset \mathrm{N}_{1}^{\dagger} \cap \ldots \cap \mathrm{N}_{\mathrm{B}}^{t}$ for if $\alpha \in \mathrm{N}$, a \& In then a $\mathrm{R} \alpha \subset \mathrm{I}^{\mathrm{n}} \mathrm{N} \subset \mathrm{N}_{\mathrm{t}}^{1} \cap \ldots \cap \mathrm{~N}_{\mathrm{s}}^{1}$. If $\alpha / N_{1} \cap \ldots \cap N_{s}^{t}$ then $a \in P_{j}^{t}$ for some $j$ since the $N_{j}^{t}$ are tertiary. This would be a contradiction. Thus $I^{h} M \cap N \subset I^{n} N$.

COROLLARY (Artin-Reese): Every finitely generated module over a commutative Noetherian ring is an Artin-Reese module.

Proof. This is merely a combination of Theorems 6.1 and 6.4.

## RECENT EXAMPLES AND COUNTER-EXAMPLES

In a remarkable paper published recently, Golod and Shafarevitch proved an extremely poweriul theorem -- although rather easy to prove -- which has provided a technique to tackle a wide range of problems. As an immediate consequence of their work one can construct a nil, but not locally nilpotent, algebra thereby providing a negative answer to the Kurosh Problem; one can construct a torsion group which is not locally finite, thereby settling in the negative the general Burnside Problem; one can construct an infinite class field tower, in fact, the field $Q(\sqrt{-3 \cdot 5 \cdot 7 \cdot 11 \cdot 13 \cdot 19})$ carries such a tower above it. It would seem that these are just the beginning of the applications of their theorem.

What is perhaps even more remarkable about what they have done is the beautiful simplicity of their approach. Part of this chapter is devoted to an exposition of a portion of their work; we shall develop it enough to settle the Kurosh and Burnside Problems. We follow the treatment used by Jon Alperin in the Algebra Seminar at the University of Chicago in April, 1964.

Let $F$ be any field and let $T=F\left[x_{1}, \ldots, x_{d}\right]$ be the polynomial ring over $F$ in the $d$ non-commuting variables $\mathrm{x}_{1}, \mathrm{x}_{2}, \ldots, \mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{d}}, \mathrm{T}$ has a degree function and carries the structure of a graded algebra; $T$ can be written as

$$
\mathrm{T}=\mathrm{T}_{0} \oplus \mathrm{~T}_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus \mathrm{~T}_{\mathrm{n}} \oplus \ldots
$$

where $T_{0}=F$ and where $T_{n}$ has a basis of $d^{n}$ elements consist-
ing of the monomials $x_{i_{1}} x_{i_{2}} \cdots x_{i_{n}}$. The elements of $T_{n}$ are said to be homogeneous of degree $n$.

Let $\sigma=\left(f_{1}, f_{2}, \ldots\right)$ be the ideal of $T$ generated by the homogeneous elements $f_{1}, i_{2}, \ldots$ of degrees $2 \leq n_{1} \leq n_{2} \leq \ldots$ respectively. Let $r_{i}$ be the number of $n_{j}$ which are equal to $i$.

Now the algebra $A=T / O L$ inherits the grading of $T$, since $C t$ is homogeneously generated, in fact $A=A_{0} \oplus A_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus A_{n} \oplus \ldots$ where $A_{n} \approx T /\left(O t \cap T_{n}\right)$. Let $b_{n}=\operatorname{dim}_{F}\left(A_{n}\right)$.

The Golod-Shafarevitch theorem gives a sufficient condition that $A$ be infinite dimensional over $F$. This is the ultraimportant

THEOREM 7.1. (Golod-Shafarevitch) For A as described above

1. $b_{n} \geq d b_{n-1}-\sum_{n_{i} \leq n} b_{n-n_{i}}$ for $n \geq 1$.
2. if $r_{i} \leq\left(\frac{d-t}{2}\right)^{2}$ then $A$ is infinite-dimensional over $F$.

Proof. We will exhibit linear mappings $\phi, \psi$ so that the

$$
\text { lence } A_{n-n_{1}} \oplus \ldots \oplus A_{n-n_{k}} \oplus \ldots \overbrace{n-1} \overbrace{\ldots \oplus A_{n-1}}^{d \text {-times }} \overbrace{A_{n} \rightarrow 0}
$$

where the first sum runs over all $n_{i} \leq n$, is exact. Note that if we could do this then (1) would be proved for then $d b_{n-1}=b_{n}+$ dim Ker $\psi$. Since ker $\psi$ is a homomorphic image of $A_{n-n_{1}} \oplus \ldots \oplus A_{n-n_{k}} \oplus \ldots$, dim $\operatorname{Ker} \psi \leq \sum_{n_{i} \leq n} b_{n-n_{i}}$, hence $d b_{n-1} \leq b_{n}+\sum_{n_{1} \leq n} b_{n-n_{i}}$, the required result.

Our task has been reduced to defining the $\varphi$ and $\psi$. To do this we shall define mappings $\Phi$ and $\psi$ for the sequence
(2) $T_{n-n_{1}} \oplus \ldots \oplus T_{n-n_{k}} \oplus \ldots \xrightarrow{5} T_{n-1} \oplus \ldots \oplus T_{n-1} \xrightarrow{\Psi} T_{n} \rightarrow 0$
where $\Phi$ and $\Phi$ are linear and the sequence is exact at $\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{n}}$. From this we shall get the $\varphi, \psi$ for the sequence (1).

We define $\Phi$ by:

$$
*: t_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus t_{d} \longrightarrow \sum_{i=1}^{d} t_{i} x_{i} .
$$

To get $\Phi_{1}$ map $s_{n-n_{1}} \omega_{n-n_{2}} \oplus \ldots \rightarrow \sum s_{n-n_{1}} f_{i}$; since $\sum_{n_{i} \leq n} s_{n-n_{i} f_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{d} u_{i} x_{i}$ with $u_{i} \in T_{n-1}$ define $\Phi: S_{n-n_{1}} \oplus S_{n-n_{2}} \oplus \ldots \rightarrow u_{1} \oplus u_{2} \oplus \ldots \oplus u_{d}$.

By the uniqueness of representation of any element in $\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{n}}$ as $\sum_{i=1}^{d} u_{i} x_{i}, u_{i} \in T_{n-1}, \Phi$ is well-defined. Trivially $\Phi$ and ‡ are linear. It is equally clear that $\Phi$ is onto $T_{n}$ so the sequence (2) is exact at $T_{n}$.

Let $O L_{i}=O C \cap T_{i}$; we show that we can induce (i) from (2). If $t_{1}, \ldots, t_{d}, \sigma t_{n-1}$ then clearly $\sum_{t_{i} x_{i}}, \sigma$ and $\sum t_{i} x_{i} \in T_{n}$ hence $\sum t_{i} x_{i} \in \alpha_{n}$, so $\Psi$ induces down to

$$
\psi: A_{n-1} \oplus \ldots \oplus A_{n-1} \xrightarrow{\psi} A_{n} \longrightarrow 0 .
$$

We consider $\Phi$, Suppose that $s_{n-n_{1}}, s_{n-n_{2}}, \ldots, s_{n-n_{1}}$ are in $\sigma_{n-n_{1}}, \sigma_{n-n_{2}}, \ldots, \sigma_{n-n_{i}}$ respectively. We want to show that $u_{i}, \ldots, u_{d}$ defined by $\sum s_{n=n_{i}} f_{i}=\sum u_{i} x_{i}$ are in
$\sigma_{n-1}$. Since o is linear it is enough to do it for $s_{n-n_{i}} \in \mathcal{U}_{n-n_{i}}$. Now $s_{n-n_{i} f_{i}}=\sum_{j=1}^{d} s_{n-n_{i}} g_{i j} x_{j}$ where $f_{i}=\sum g_{i j} x_{j}$; if $s_{n-n_{i}} \epsilon \sigma$ then $s_{n-n_{i}} g_{i j}<\sigma$. But $s_{n-n_{i}} g_{i j}=u_{j}$ hence $i_{j} \in$ or $\cap T_{n-1}=O C_{n-1}$. Thus $\Phi$ induces down.

All that remains is to show exactness at $\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{n}-1} \oplus \ldots \oplus \mathrm{~A}_{\mathrm{n}-1}$. We first show that $\varphi \psi=0$, If $s_{n-n_{1}}, s_{n-n_{2}}, \ldots \in T_{n-n_{1}}$, $T_{n-n_{2}} \ldots .\left(s_{n-n_{1}} \oplus s_{n-n_{2}} \oplus \ldots\right) \Phi \Psi=\sum_{i=1}^{d} u_{i} x_{i} \quad$ where $\sum u_{i} x_{i}=\sum s_{n-n_{i}} f_{i}$; since the $f_{i} \in O R, \sum s_{n-n_{i}} f_{i} \in O L$ hence $\sum u_{i} x_{i} \in \mathcal{L}$ whence $\Phi \Phi$ maps $T_{n-n_{i}} \oplus \ldots$ into $\mathcal{O}$ and so $\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{n}-\mathrm{n}_{1}} \oplus \mathrm{~A}_{\mathrm{n}-\mathrm{n}_{2}} \oplus .$. is mapped into 0 by $\varphi$. We now want to show that if $\left(t_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus t_{d}\right) \Psi \in \mathcal{O}$ then we can find $u_{1}, u_{2}, \ldots, u_{d}, T_{n-1}$ such that $t_{i}-u_{i} \in$ be for $i=1,2, \ldots, d$ and such that $\sum u_{i} x_{i}=\sum s_{n-n_{i}} f_{i}$ for some $s_{n-n_{i}}$ in the appropriate $\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{n}-\mathrm{n}_{\mathrm{i}}}$,

Suppose then that $\sum t_{i} x_{i} \in \sigma$; being in $O$, which is generated $\mathrm{a} s$ an ideal by the $\mathrm{f}_{\mathrm{j}}, \quad \sum \mathrm{t}_{\mathrm{i}} \mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{i}}=\sum \mathrm{a}_{\mathrm{k} \ell^{\mathrm{f}} \ell} \mathrm{b}_{\mathrm{k} \ell}+ \sum c_{\ell} f_{\ell}$ where the ${ }^{a}{ }_{k \ell}, b_{k \ell}, c_{\ell}$ are homogeneous and $\mathrm{b}_{\mathrm{k} \ell}$ is of degree $\geq 1$. Since $\sum \mathrm{t}_{\mathrm{i}} \mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{i}} \in \mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{n}}$ by picking out the parts of the right degree we may assume that the $\mathrm{a}_{\mathrm{k} \ell} f_{\ell} \mathrm{b}_{\mathrm{k} \ell}$, ${ }^{\mathrm{c}} f_{\ell}$ are all in $\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{n}}$. Since the $\mathrm{b}_{\mathrm{k} \ell}$ are of degree at least 1 , $\mathrm{b}_{\mathrm{k} \ell}=\sum \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{k} \ell \mathrm{m}_{\mathrm{m}}}$ hence

$$
\sum \mathrm{a}_{\mathrm{k} \ell} f_{\ell} \mathrm{b}_{\mathrm{k} \ell}=\sum \mathrm{a}_{\mathrm{k} \ell} f_{\ell} \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{k} \ell \mathrm{~m}^{\prime} \mathrm{m}}=\sum \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{m}} \mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{m}},
$$

where $d_{m}=\sum_{k, l} a_{k l} f_{l} d_{k l m}$ so is in $C$. If $\sum c_{l} f_{l} =\sum_{i=1}^{d} u_{i} x_{i}$ we have $\sum_{i=1}^{d} t_{i} x_{i}=\sum_{m=1}^{d} d_{m} x_{m}+\sum_{i=1}^{d} u_{i} x_{i}$. hence $t_{i}-u_{i}=d_{i} \in \sigma$. But $\left(c_{i} \oplus \ldots \oplus c_{i} \oplus \ldots\right) \Phi=u_{i} \oplus_{\ldots} \ldots u_{d}$ by definition of Q. Hence we have proved the exactness at $A_{n-1} \oplus \ldots \oplus A_{n-1}$. This proves part (1) of the theorem.

We now consider formal power series in $t$ with integer coefficients; we declare $\sum_{n=1}^{\infty} c_{n} t^{n} \geq \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} d_{n} t^{n}$ if $c_{i} \geq d_{i}$ for all i. From part (1) we have

$$
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty} b_{n} t^{n} \geq \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} d b_{n-1} t^{n}-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty} \sum_{n_{i} \leq n} b_{n-n_{i}} t^{n} .
$$

From the definition of $r_{1}$,

$$
\sum_{n_{i}, m} b_{m} t^{n_{i}+m}=\sum^{n_{i}} \sum_{m=0}^{\infty} b_{m} t^{m}=\left(\sum_{i=2}^{\infty} r_{i} t^{i}\right)\left(\sum_{m=0}^{\infty} b_{m} t^{m}\right) .
$$

Let $P_{A}(t)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty} b_{m} t^{m}$. From the above

$$
P_{A}(t)-1 \geq d t P_{A}(t)-\left(\sum_{i=2}^{\infty} r_{i} t^{i}\right) P_{A}(t),
$$

hence

$$
P_{A}(t)\left(1-d t-\sum_{i=2}^{\infty} r_{i} t^{i}\right) \geq 1 .
$$

But then we immediately see that if the coefficients of $\left(1-d t+\sum_{i=2}^{\infty} r_{i} t^{i}\right)^{-1}$ are positive then an infinite number of the $b_{n}$ are not 0 , hence $A$ is infinite dimensional.

THEOREM 7.2. A is infinite dimensional over $F$ if the coefficients in the power series expansion of $\left(1-d t+\sum_{i=2}^{\infty} r_{i} t^{i}\right)^{-1}$ are positive.

We leave it as an excercise to show that if $r_{i} \leq\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)^{2}$ for all $i$ then indeed all the coefficients of (1-dt $\left.+\sum_{i=2}^{\infty} r_{i} t^{i}\right)^{-1}$ are positive. Thus part (2) of Theorem 7.1 is proved.

We use Theorem 7.1 to construct a finitely generated nil algebra which is not nilpotent. This settles the Kurosh Problem in the negative.

THEOREM 7.3. If $F$ is any countable field there exists an infinite dimensional nil algebra over $F$ generated by three elements.

Proof. Let $T=F\left[x_{1}, x_{2}, x_{3}\right]$ as before be the free algebra over $F$ generated by the three non-commuting indeterminates $x_{1}, x_{2}, x_{3} ; T$ is graded, $T=T_{0} \oplus T_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus T_{n} \oplus \ldots$. Consider the ideal $T^{\prime}=T_{1} \oplus T_{2} \oplus \ldots$ of $T$. $T^{\prime}$ is countable so we enumerate its elements as $s_{1}, s_{2}, \ldots$. Let $m_{1} \geq 2$ be an integer and let $s_{1}^{m_{1}}=s_{12}+s_{13}+\ldots+s_{1 k_{1}}$ where $s_{1 j} \in T_{j}$. Choose an integer $\mathrm{m}_{2}>0$ so that $\mathrm{s}_{2} \in \mathrm{~T}_{\mathrm{k}_{1}+1} \oplus \ldots$; $s_{2}{ }^{m_{2}}=s_{2, k_{1}+1}+\ldots+s_{2, k_{2}}$ with $s_{2 j} \in T_{j}$. Thus we may choose integers $k_{1}<k_{2}<\ldots$ such that $s_{i}^{m}=s_{i, k_{i-1}+1}+\ldots+s_{i k_{i}}$ with $s_{i j}<T_{j}$. Let ot be the Ideal of $T$ generated by the $s_{i j}$. By construction of the $s_{i j}$, $r_{k}$ in Theorem 7.1 is at most 1 . Since $d=3$, we have $r_{i} \leq\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)^{2}$. By Theorem 7.1, $T / \partial \mathrm{C}$ is infinite dimensional,
over $F$. Moreover, by construction $T^{1} / O C$ is a nil algebra. This is the required example since $T / O C$ is generated over $F$ by three elements.

This result is not the best possible for as Golod has shown (in a proof which is not difficult) given any field $F$ there is an infinite dimensional nil algebra over $F$ generated by two elements.

We use Theorem 7.3 to construct an example of an infinite torsion group generated by three elements, which is therefore a counter-example to the Burnside Problem.

THEOREM 7.4. If $p$ is any prime number there is an infinite group $G$ generated by three elements in which every element has order a power of p.

Proof. Let $F$ be the prime field with $p$ elements and let $O C$ be the ideal in $T=F\left[x_{1}, x_{2}, x_{3}\right]$ constructed during the proof of Theorem 7.3. Let $A=T / o c$ and let $a_{1}, a_{2}, a_{3}$ be $x_{1}+o t, x_{2}+o r, x_{3}+o r$ respectively. Let $G$ be the multiplicative semi-group generated by $1+a_{1}, 1+a_{2}$ and $1+a_{3}$. If any element is in $G$ it is of the form $1+a$ where $a<T / O L$ hence $a$ is nilpotent; if $a^{n}=0$ then $a^{p^{n}}=0$ so that $(1+a) p^{n}=1$ since we are in characteristic $p$. Thus $G$ is a group in which every element has order a power of $p$. We claim $G$ is infinite, for if $G$ is finite the linear combinations of elements in $G$ over $F$ would form a finite dimensional subalgebra $B$ of $A$. But since $1+a_{i}, 1$ are in $G, a_{i} \in B$ hence $A=B$, contradicting that $A$ is infinite dimensional, This finishes the proof.

We now turn to other things with the advice to the reader to read the entire Golod-Shafarevitch paper and its sequels by Colod. We have only considered here a small portion of the wonderful things they do.

It was mentioned in (N) that the question whether a right primitive ring need be left primitive was open. Recently G. Bergman has given an example of a ring which is right primitive but not left primitive. We now shall exhibit this example.

Let $Q$ be the field of rational numbers and let $Q(x)$ be the field of rational functions in the indeterminate $x$ over $Q$. In $Q(x)$ the mapping $\alpha: r(x) \rightarrow r\left(x^{2}\right)$ defines a monomorphism into but not onto. Let $A$ be the ring of all polynomials $r_{0}(x)+r_{1}(x) y+\ldots+r_{n}(x) y^{n}$, with $r_{i}(x) \in Q(x)$ where equality and addition are as usual but where $y r(x)=r\left(x^{2}\right) y=\alpha(r(x)) y$. $A$ is a non-commutative integral domain where all left ideals are principal, since we have a left division algorithm. It is in A that we shall find the right, but not left, primitive ring $R$,

(Tier-1 Error)

## We first show

LEMMA 7.1. Any subring B of A , containing x and y , which is an algebra over $Q$ is right primitive.

Proof. We shall exhibit a faithful, irreducible right B-module.

Given $r \in A(x)$ we define $r^{*} \in A(x)$ by

$$
r^{*}\left(x^{2}\right)=\frac{r(x)+r(-x)}{2} .
$$

We define a right A-module structure on $Q(x)$ by defining: $r \cdot y=r^{*}, r \cdot q=r q$ for $r, q \in Q(x)$. We claim this endows $Q(x)$ with the structure of a right $A$-module. We merely
must check: for $r, q \in Q(x),(r \cdot y) \cdot q=r \cdot(y q)=r(\alpha(q) y) =(r \alpha(q)) \cdot y$, that is, $r^{*} q=(r o(q))^{*}$. Now $\left\langle r^{*} q\right)\left(x^{2}\right) =r^{*}\left(x^{2}\right) q\left(x^{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2}(r(x)+r(-x)) q\left(x^{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(r(x) q\left(x^{2}\right)+r(-x) q\left((-x)^{2}\right)\right) =\frac{1}{2}\langle r(x) \alpha(q))+r(-x) \alpha(q(-x))=(r \alpha(q))^{*}\left(x^{2}\right)$. Thus $r^{*} q=(r \alpha(q))^{*}$ and so we see that $Q(x)$ becomes a right $A$-module under the defined action.

From the definition of the action we immediately see:

$$
\begin{aligned}
x^{n} \cdot y^{m} & =x^{\left(n / 2^{m}\right)} & & \text { if } 2^{m} / n \\
& =0 & & \text { if } 2^{m} /_{n}
\end{aligned}
$$

Let $M$ be the $B$-submodule of $Q(x)$ generated by $x$. We claim that $M$ is both irreducible and faithful as a $B$-module.

Let $0 \neq \frac{p(x)}{q(x)}$ be in $M$ where $p(x)$ and $q(x)$ are polynomials in $Q$. Let $n=\operatorname{deg} p(x)$; pick $m$ so that $2^{m}>n$. Then $\left(\frac{p(x)}{q(x)}\right) \cdot\left(q(x) x^{2^{m}-n} y^{m}\right)=\left(p(x) x^{2^{m}-n}\right) y^{m}=c x$ where $c \neq 0$ is the leading coefficient of $p(x)$. Thus $x$ is in the submodule generated by any non-zero element of $M$, in other words, since $x$ generates $M, M$ is irreducible.

Suppose now that $0 \neq b=\sum r_{i}(x) y^{i}$ in $B$ annihilates $M$. Let $x p(x) \neq 0$ be a polynomial so that all $\operatorname{xp}(x) r_{i}(x)$ are polynomials and let $0 \neq x p(x) b=\sum_{i} s_{i}(x) y^{i}$ where the $s_{i}(x)$ are polynomials. Thus $\sum s_{i}(x) y^{i}$ is in $B$ and annihilates $M$. Let $j$ be the least integer such that $s_{j}(x) \neq 0$ and let $d=\max \left(\operatorname{deg} s_{i}(x)-\operatorname{deg} s_{j}(x)\right)$. Pick $n$ so that $2^{n} \geq \operatorname{deg} s_{j}(x)$ and $2^{n-j^{1}-1} \geq d$. Let $s_{j}(x)$ have degree $m$. Consider $x^{2^{n}-m} \cdot\left(\sum s_{i}(x) y^{i}\right)$. In the term $x^{2^{n}-m} s_{j}(x)$ the highest power of $x$ occuring with non-zero coefficient is $x^{2^{n}}$ hence in $x^{2^{n}-m} s_{j}(x) \cdot y^{j}, x^{2^{n-j}}$ occurs with non-zero coefficient. For $t>j$ the highest power of $x$ in $x^{2^{n}}-m s_{i}(x)$ is
$2^{n}-m+\operatorname{deg} s_{i}(x) \leq 2^{n}+d$ by definition of $d, 80$ in $x^{2^{n}}-m{ }_{s_{1}}(x) \cdot y^{1}, x$ occurs at best to the power $\frac{2^{n}+d}{2^{i}}$; since

$$
\frac{2^{n+d}}{2^{i}} \leq 2^{n-i}+d<2^{n-j-1}+d \leq 2^{n-j-1}+2^{n-j-1}=2^{n-j} .
$$

in other words $\mathrm{x}^{2^{n-j}}$ occurs with non-zero coefficient in $x^{2^{n}-m}\left(\sum s_{i}(x) y^{i}\right)$ whence $x^{2^{n}-m} \cdot\left(\sum s_{i}(x) y^{i}\right) \neq 0$, contrary to $\sum s_{i}(x) y^{i}$ annihilates $M$. This proves the lemma.

Let $v_{n}$ be the valuation induced by the $n$-th cyclotomic polynomial $P_{n}(x)$ on $Q(x)$.

LEMMA 7.2. If $n$ is odd $v_{n}(r(x))=v_{n}\left(r\left(x^{2}\right)\right)$.
Proof. Since $p_{n}(x)$ is irreducible over $Q$, any polynomial in $Q(x)$ having a common root with $p_{n}(x)$ has $p_{n}(x)$ as a factor. Now $p_{n}\left(x^{2}\right)$ for $n$ odd has as roots $\pm \sqrt{\theta}$ where $\theta$ are the roots of $p_{n}(x)$ hence by the oddness of $n$, $p_{n}(x) \mid p_{n}\left(x^{2}\right)$. Similarly $p_{n}(-x) \mid p_{n}\left(x^{2}\right)$. Thus on comparing degrees, $p_{n}\left(x^{2}\right)=p_{n}(x) p_{n}(-x)$, hence $v_{n}\left(p_{n}\left(x^{2}\right)\right)=v_{n}\left(p_{n}(x)\right)$. If $\left(p_{n}(x), q(x)\right)=1$ then $\left(p_{n}(x), q\left(x^{2}\right)\right)=1$ hence $v_{n}(q(x))=0$. But then for any polynomial $f(x), v_{n}(f(x))=v_{n}\left(f\left(x^{2}\right)\right)$. From this we get the result for rational functions and the lemma is established.

We define $V_{n}$ on $A$, for odd $n$, by

$$
v_{n}\left(\sum r_{i}(x) y^{i}\right)=\min v_{n}\left(r_{i}(x)\right) .
$$

From Lemma 7.2, $v_{n}(r(x))=v_{n}(\alpha(r(x)))$ hence $v_{n}$ defines a valuation on $A$. Let $R=\left\{z+A \mid V_{n}(z) \geq 0\right.$ all odd $\left.n\right\}$. Clearly $R$ is an algebra over $Q$ and contains $x$ and $y$, hence by Lemma $7.1 R$ is right primitive. We now prove

THEOREM 7.5. R is right primitive but not left primitive.

Proof. Let I be a maximal left tdeal of $\mathrm{R}_{\text {; }}$ we shall show that $I$ contains a non-zero two-sided ideal of $R$, therefore $R$ can not be left primitive.

Consider the left ideal AI of A; if AI $\neq A$ it is generated by some element $\mathrm{g}=\mathrm{a}_{0}(\mathrm{x})+\mathrm{a}_{1}(\mathrm{x}) \mathrm{y}+\ldots+\mathrm{a}_{\mathrm{m}}(\mathrm{x}) \mathrm{y}^{\mathrm{m}}, \mathrm{m}>0$, ${ }^{\mathrm{a}} \mathrm{m}(\mathrm{x}) \neq 0$, since the non-zero elements in $Q(\mathrm{x})$ are invertible in $A$. Choose $n$ odd so that $V_{n}(g)=0$. Given any $a=\sum b_{i} y^{i}$ there is an i so that $V_{n}(a)=V_{n}\left(b_{i}\right)$; let $\delta(a)=$ largest i so that $V_{n}(a)=V_{n}\left(b p_{1}\right)$. Clearly $\delta(a c)=\delta(a)+\delta(c)$ for all a, $c \in A$.

Now since $V_{n}(q)=0, \delta(q)=m$ where $m$ is the degree of $g$ in $y$. If $u \in A I$ then $\delta(u) \geq \delta(g)>0$. Let $p_{n}(x)$ be the $n$-th cyclotomic polynomial; $p_{n}(x)$ is in $R$ and $V_{n}\left(p_{n}(x)\right)=1$. Also $p_{n}(x) \& I$ for it is invertible in $Q(x)$, and so in $A$, and $A I \neq A$. Since 1 is maximal, $R=I+R_{p_{n}}(x)$ so for some $i \in I, r \in R$, $1=1+r p_{n}(x)$; thus $1-r p_{n}(x) \in I$. But $\delta\left(1-r p_{n}(x)\right)=0$, thus $0=\delta(i)>0$ since $i \in 1$.

Therefore if $I$ is a maximal left ideal of $R$ then $A I=A$. Thus $1=a_{1} i_{1}+\ldots+a_{s} i_{s}$ for $a_{j} \in I, i_{j} \in I$. We can find $w \neq 0 \in Q(x)$ so that for all $n, V_{n}\left(w a_{i}\right) \geq 0$ for $1 \leq i \leq s$. Thus $w a_{1} \subset R$ hence $w=w\left(a_{1} i_{1}+\ldots a_{s} i_{s}\right)=\left(w a_{1}\right) i_{1}+\ldots+\left(w a_{s}\right) i_{s}$ is an element of RI CI. Since w \& I, Rw C I. However, since $w$ is invertible in $A, V_{n}\left(w^{-1} t w\right)=V_{n}(t)$ for all $t \in A$, in particular $\mathrm{w}^{-1} \mathrm{Rw}=\mathrm{R}$ results. Thus $\mathrm{Rw}=\mathrm{wR}$ leading to $\mathrm{w} \mathrm{R}=\mathrm{Rw} \mathrm{C} 1$. I thus contains the two-sided ideal w R of R , This proves the theorem.

We conclude this chapter with an example, due to Sasiada, of a simple radical ring.

Let $F$ be any field and let $A$ be ring of formal power series over $F$ in the two non-commuting variables $x$ and $y$. Let $A^{\prime}$ be all the elements in $A$ whose constant term is 0 .

It is easy to verify that $A^{\prime}$ is a radical ring. Suppose for the moment that we know that $\mathbf{x}$ is not in the two-sided ideal of $A$ generated by $x-y x^{2} y$. Let $U$ be an ideal of $A^{t}$ which contains $x-y x^{2} y$ and is maximal with respect to the exclusion of $x$. Let $R=A^{t} / U ; R$ is a radical ring having a unique minimal ideal $S \neq(0)$ which is contained in every non-zero ideal of R and which contains $\overline{\mathrm{x}}=\mathrm{x}+\mathrm{U}$. From this it is trivial that $S$ is a radical ring devoid of non-trivial two-sided ideals. To know that $S$ is simple we need but know that $s^{2} \neq(0)$. But in $R, \bar{x}=\bar{y} \bar{x}^{2} \bar{y}$ where $\bar{y}=y+U$, so that $x=(\bar{y} \bar{x})(\bar{x} \bar{y}) \neq 0 \in S^{2}$, hence $S^{2} \neq(0)$. Thus $S$ would be a simple radical ring. To produce the desired example we must merely show that $x$ is not in the two-sided ideal of A generated by $x-y x^{2} y$. The proof we give here for this fact is from the recent paper by Cohn and Sasiada.

We introduce some notation. With A and $\mathrm{A}^{\prime}$ as above, let $M$ be the two-sided ideal of $A$ generated by $u=x-y x^{2} y$ and let $E$ be the set of elements in A having constant term 1. We begin with

LEMMA 7.3. If $\mathrm{c}=\sum_{1}^{\mathrm{m}} \mathrm{a}_{i} \mathrm{u} \mathrm{b}_{i}$ and $\mathrm{b}_{i} \in \mathrm{~A}^{\prime}$ then there exist $a_{i}^{\prime} \in A, b_{i}^{\prime}, E, b_{i}^{\prime} \in A^{\prime}$ for $i>1$ such that $c=a_{1}^{1} u b_{1}^{1}+\sum_{2}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}^{1}$.

Proof. Since $b_{1} / A^{t}, b_{1}=\beta b_{1}^{t}$ where $\beta \neq 0 \in F$ and $b_{i}<E_{;}$let $\beta_{i}$ be the constant term of $b_{i}$. Then $a_{1} u b_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}=\left(\beta a_{1}+\sum_{2}^{n} \beta_{i} a_{i}\right) u b_{i}+\sum_{2}^{n} a_{i} u\left(b_{i}-\beta_{i} b_{i}\right)$. This is the lemma with $a_{1}^{1}=\beta_{3}+\sum_{2}^{n} \beta_{i} a_{i}$ and $b_{i}^{1}=b_{i}-\beta_{i} b_{i}^{1}$ \& $A^{\prime}$, for $i>1$.

LEMMA 7.4. If $\mathrm{cx}+\mathrm{dy}=\sum_{1}^{\mathrm{n}} \mathrm{a}_{i} \mathrm{u} \mathrm{b}_{i}$ with $\mathrm{b}_{1} \notin \mathrm{~A}^{t}$ then there exist $b_{i}^{*}$, A' such that $c x+d y=c u\left(1+b_{1}^{*}\right)+\sum_{2}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}^{*}$.

Proof. Using Lemma 7.3 we may suppose, given the hypothesis $b_{i}^{\prime} \notin A^{\prime}$, that $b_{1} \in E$ and $b_{i} \in A^{\prime}$ for $i>1$, without changing the original $a_{i}$ for $i>1$. Write $b_{1}=1+b_{1}^{\prime} x+b_{1}^{\prime \prime} y, b_{i}=b_{i}^{\prime} x+b_{i}^{\#} y$ for $i>1$; substituting these in $c x+d y=\sum_{i}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}$, using $u=x-y x_{n}^{2} y$ and comparing coefficients of $x$ yield $c=a_{1}+a_{2} u b_{1}^{1}+\sum_{2}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}^{1}$. Now $1+u b_{1}^{1}$ is invertible in $A$ and its inverse, $1-u b_{1}^{\prime}+\left(u b_{1}^{\prime}\right)^{2}=\ldots +(-1)^{n}\left(\mathrm{ub}^{\mathrm{t}}\right)^{\mathrm{n}}+\ldots$ is of the form $1+\mathrm{ug}$ for g , A , Thus we have $a_{i}=c(1+u g)-\sum_{2}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}^{\prime}(1+u g)$; substituting this value for $a_{1}$ in the given expression for $c x+d y$ yields

$$
\begin{aligned}
c x+d y & =c(1+u g) u b_{1}-\sum_{2}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}^{+}(1+u g) u b_{1}^{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i} \\
& =c u(1+g u) b_{1}+\sum_{2}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}^{*},
\end{aligned}
$$

where $b_{i}^{*}=b_{i}^{\prime}(1+u g) u b_{i}+b_{i}$ is in $A^{2}$. Since both $b_{i}$ and $1+g u$ are in $E,(1+g u) b_{1}$ is in $E$ so of the form $1+b_{1}^{*}$, $b_{1}^{*} \in A^{\prime}$. This is precisely the assertion of the lemma.

LEMMA 7.5. Suppose the product dy has the form $d y=\sum_{i}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}$, where $n$ is the minimal such for $d y$. Then each $b_{i}$ is in $A^{\prime}$.

Proof. Suppose some $b_{i} / A^{\prime}$, say $b_{1}$; using the previous lemma, with $c=0$, yields $d y=\sum_{2}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}^{\text {* }}$ contradicting the minimal nature of $n$.

We now come to the heart of the discussion. This is
LEMMA 7.6. $M$, the ideal generated by $u=x-y x^{2} y$, contains no monomial.

Proof, Suppose that the lemma is false; then there is some monomial $c \in M, c=\sum_{i}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}$. For all such monomials pick a monomial o where n is minimal possible. For this $n$ pick $c$ of lowest degree lying in $M$. We assert that $c$ must finish in $x_{\text {; if not, }} c=c_{1} y$. By Lemma 7.5 all the $b_{i} \in A^{\prime}$ and writing $b_{i}=b_{i}^{\prime} x+b_{i}^{\prime \prime} y$ and substituting in the relation for $c$ gives $c_{1}=\sum_{1}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}{ }^{i t}$ on comparing the coefficient of $y$. Since $c_{i}$ is of lower degree than $c$ this is not possible. Hence we know that $c=c_{1} x$ and $c_{1} x=\sum_{i}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}$.

If all the $b_{i}$ are in $A^{t}$ writing $b_{i}=b_{i}^{t} x+b_{i}^{n} y$ will lead to the contradiction $c_{1}=\sum_{1}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}^{\prime}$. Thus we may suppose $b_{1}$ <A'; by Lemma 7.4, $c_{1} x=c_{1} u d_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n} a_{i} u d_{i}$ where $d_{1}\left\langle A^{\prime}, d_{i} \in A^{\prime}\right.$ for $i>1$.

We claim that for any $\mathrm{r} \leq \mathrm{n}$ we can find monomials $P_{1}, \ldots, P_{r}$ of positive degree and elements $d_{1}, \ldots, d_{n}, A$ (depending on $r$ ) such that
(i) $c_{1} p_{1} \ldots p_{r} x=c_{1} \sum_{1}^{r} p_{1} \ldots p_{1-1} u d_{i}+\sum_{r+1}^{n} a_{j} u d_{j}$,
(2) $d_{i} /$ A $^{t}$ for some $1 \leq i \leq n$.

Note that our preceding discussion established this for $\mathrm{q}=0$. We now proceed by induction.

Suppose for $r<n$ elements $d_{1}, \ldots, d_{n}$ and monomials $p_{1}, \ldots, p_{r}$ of positive degree can be found satisfying ( 1 ) and (2). We want to pass to rt1. We divide into two cases.

1. If $d_{j} \in A^{\prime}$ for all $j>r$ then $d_{i_{\alpha}} / A^{\prime}$ for some $i_{0} \leq r$. Write $d_{j}=d_{j}^{1} x+d_{j}^{n} y$ for $j>r$ and $d_{i}=\lambda_{i}+d_{i}^{1} x+d_{i}^{n} y$ for $i \leq r$. Substituting in (1) and equating multipliers of $x$ yields that

$$
\begin{gathered}
c_{1} p_{1} \ldots p_{r}=c_{1} \sum_{1}^{r} \lambda_{i} p_{1} \ldots p_{i-1}+c_{1} \sum_{1}^{r} p_{1} \ldots p_{i-1} u d_{i}^{1} \\
+\sum_{r+1}^{n} a_{j} u d_{j}^{\prime} ;
\end{gathered}
$$

hence if $s$ is the least $i$ such that $\lambda_{i} \neq 0$, on transposing we get

$$
-\lambda_{s} p_{1} \cdots p_{s-1}(1+i)=\sum_{1}^{r} c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{i-1} u d_{i}^{\prime}+\sum_{r+1}^{n} a_{j} u d_{j}^{\prime},
$$

where $f \in A^{\prime}$ (since the $p^{\prime} s$ are of positive degree). Transpose the s-th term of the first sum on the right to the left hand side. We then have

$$
\begin{gathered}
c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{s-1}\left(\lambda_{s}(1+\varepsilon)-u d_{s}^{\prime}\right)=\sum_{\substack{i=1 \\
i \neq s}}^{r} c_{1} p_{i} \cdots p_{i-1} u d_{i}^{t} \\
+\sum_{j+1}^{n} a_{j} u d_{j}^{t} .
\end{gathered}
$$

But $\lambda_{s}(1+f)-u d_{s}^{t}$ is invertible in $A$ giving us $c_{1} P_{1} \cdots p_{s-1}$ as a combination of $\sum \mathrm{a}_{i}^{*} \mathrm{ub}_{i}^{*}$ involving $\mathrm{n}-1$ terms. This contradicts the choice of $n$ and so case (1) is not possible.
II. Suppose, then that $d_{j} / A^{\prime}$ for some $j>r$, say for $j=r+1$. Applying Lemma 7,4 to (1) gives

$$
\begin{gathered}
c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r} x=\sum_{1}^{r} c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{i-1} u d_{i}+c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r}\left(1+d_{r+1}\right) \\
+\sum_{r+2}^{n} a_{j} u d_{j},
\end{gathered}
$$

where all the $d_{k}{ }^{\prime}{ }^{\prime}, 1 \leq k \leq n$ are in $A^{\prime}$. Writing $d_{k}=d_{k}^{\prime} x+d_{k}^{\prime \prime} y$ and substituting in this leads, on comparing multipliers of x , to
(3) $c_{1} p_{1} \ldots p_{r} y x^{2}=\sum_{1}^{T} c_{1} p_{1} \ldots p_{i-1} u d_{i}^{\prime}+c_{1} p_{1} \ldots p_{r} u d_{r+1}^{\prime}$

$$
+\sum_{r+2}^{n} a_{j} u d_{j}^{\top}
$$

If in (3) all the $d_{k}^{t}$ are in $A^{\prime}$ we can write $d_{k}^{\prime}=e_{k}^{\prime} x+e_{k}^{\prime \prime} y$, substitute, pick out the multiplier of $x$ to get

$$
\begin{gather*}
c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r} y x=\sum_{1}^{r} c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{i-1} u e_{i}^{1}+c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r} u e_{r+1}^{1}  \tag{4}\\
+\sum_{r+2}^{n} a_{j} u e_{j}^{1} .
\end{gather*}
$$

If in (4) all the $e_{k}^{1}$ are in $A^{1}$ we can repeat the by-now familiar argument to get
(5)

$$
\begin{gathered}
c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r} y=\sum_{1}^{r} c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{i-1} u f_{i}+c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r} u f_{r+1} \\
+\sum_{r+2}^{n} a_{j} u f_{j} .
\end{gathered}
$$

Since this is a minimal representation of $c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r} y$, invoking Lemma 7.5 , we have all the $f^{\prime} \mathrm{s}$ in $A^{\prime}$ and writing $f_{i}=g_{i}^{\prime} x+g_{i}^{\prime \prime} y$ leads to a cancellation of $y$ and the result

$$
\begin{gathered}
c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r}=\sum_{1}^{r} c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{i-1} u g_{1}^{n}+c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r} u g_{r+1}^{n} \\
+\sum_{r+2}^{n} a_{j} u g_{j}^{n}
\end{gathered}
$$

hence

$$
c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r}\left(1-u g_{r+1}^{n}\right)=\sum_{1}^{r} c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{i-1} u g_{i}^{n}+\sum_{r+2}^{n} a_{j} u g_{j}^{n} .
$$

But $1-u g_{r+1}^{\prime \prime}$ is invertible so we get a representation of the monomial $\mathrm{c}_{1} \mathrm{p}_{1} \cdots \mathrm{p}_{T}$ in $n-1$ terms of the form $\mathrm{s}_{1} u t_{1}$; this contradicts the choice of $n$. Hence in (3) or (4) some $d_{i}$ or some $\frac{e^{t}}{i}$ is not in $A^{t}$. In either case this completes the induction.

The inductive step having been made we have that there exist $p_{1}, \ldots, p_{n}$ monomials of positive degree and $d_{1}, \ldots, d_{n} \in A$ such that

$$
c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{n} x=\sum_{1}^{n} c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{1-1} u d_{1} .
$$

But this puts us back into case I of our argument, which we saw was not possible. Hence the lemma is proved,

Since $M$ contains no monomials it does not contain $x$. Hence, as we saw earlier, this forces the existence of a simple radical ring.

As Hamsher has pointed out this also enables you to construct a countable simple radical ring. Let $F$ be countable; in A consider the subring generated by x and $\mathrm{y} \cdots$ it too is countable. Adjoin all the quasi-inverses of its elements to it and consider the subring now generated .. it is countable. Continue and take the direct limit to get a countable radical subring $T$ of $A$ containing $x$ and $y$. In $T, x$ is not in the ideal generated by $x-y x^{2} y$; now do as before, taking an ideal $M$ of $T$ maximal with respect to exciusion of $x$ and containing $x-y x^{2} y$. The minimal ideal of $T / M$ is a countable simple radical ring.
